, the most advanced version of Tensor Programs before this work, is unable to express
Eq.

2.1
for hidden weights of a network (weight matrices where both dimensions tend to infinity).
In this work, we extend
\textsc{Netsor}\top
to
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
that
can
express it and develop its Master Theorem.

2.2
abcd-Parametrizations

In this work, we consider how such optimization should be parametrized
wrt the width of a neural network, generalizing the the abc-parametrization
of
[
45
]
.

For concreteness, consider an
L
-hidden-layer biasless perceptron:
For weight matrices
W^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
W^{2},\ldots,W^{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
and nonlinearity
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, such a neural network on input
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

is given by

\displaystyle h^{l}(\xi)=W^{l}x^{l-1}(\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad x^{l}(\xi)=\phi(h^{l}(\xi))\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\text{for $l=1,\ldots,L$,}

(2.3)

where
x^{0}(\xi)=\xi

and the network output is
f(\xi)=W^{L+1\top}x^{L}(\xi)
for
W^{L+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1}
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Fix a set of update functions
\bm{Q}=\{Q^{l}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t+1}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{t\geq 0,l\in[L+1]}
.
An
abcd-parametrization
of the MLP in
Eq.

2.3
is specified by a set of numbers
\{a_{l},b_{l},c_{l},d_{l}\}_{l}

such that

(a)

We parametrize each weight as
W^{l}=n^{-a_{l}}w^{l}
for actual
trainable parameter
w^{l}

(b)

We initialize each
w_{\alpha\beta}^{l}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,n^{-2b_{l}})

(c)

The learning rate is
\eta n^{-c_{l}}
for some width-independent

\eta

(d)

The gradients of
w^{l}
are multiplied by
n^{d_{l}}
before being
processed by
Q^{l}_{t}
: i.e., the update at time
t
is

w^{l}\leftarrow w^{l}-\eta n^{-c_{l}}Q^{l}_{t}(n^{d_{l}}g_{0},\ldots,n^{d_{l}}g_{t})

(2.4)

where
g_{s},s=0,\ldots,t
, are the gradients of
w^{l}
at time

s
and
Q^{l}_{t}
is applied entrywise.

A simple example is the “standard parametrization” that is the default for, e.g., PyTorch, where nothing scales with width other than the initialization.

Example 2.2.2
.

The
standard abcd-parametrization (SP)
is defined by

l

[2,L]

1

L+1

a_{l}

0

0

0

b_{l}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

0

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

c_{l}

0

0

0

d_{l}

0

0

0

In
2.2.1
, beyond the obvious addition of scaling exponent
d_{l}
, compared to abc-parametrization of
[
45
]
, we also now have layer dependent
c_{l}
.
This is without loss of generality, because of the redundancy in
a_{l},b_{l},c_{l}
, as shown in
[
45
, Eq 5]
.
This takes the more general form as follows for abcd-parametrization:

Proposition 2.2.3

(abcd Redundancy)
.

For every
l\in[L+1]
,

for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, at any finite width
n
,
f_{t}(\xi)
stays fixed for all
t
and
\xi
if we set

a_{l}\leftarrow a_{l}+\theta,\ b_{l}\leftarrow b_{l}-\theta,\ c_{l}\leftarrow c_{l}-\theta,\ d_{l}\leftarrow d_{l}+\theta.

(2.5)

Remark 2.2.4

(Reduction to abc-Parametrization for SGD)
.

In the case of SGD (i.e., when
Q^{l}_{t}(g_{0},\ldots,g_{t})=g_{t}
),
an abcd-parametrization reduces to an abc-parametrization with the
mapping

(a_{l},b_{l},c_{l})\leftarrow(a_{l},b_{l},c_{l}-d_{l}).

(2.6)

Remark 2.2.5

(Omitted Constants)
.

As in
[
45
]
, our concern here is the correct way
to scale with width
.
In practice, there should be tunable hyperparameters in front of the powers of
n
in
2.2.1
, as investigated in
[
47
]
.

Remark 2.2.6

(Alternative Definition of
d_{l}
for Adam)
.

In the idealized case of Adam and similar adaptive optimizers where the
\epsilon
in
Eq.

2.2
is 0,
Q^{l}_{t}
is degree-0 homogeneous and
d_{l}
itself is redundant.
When
\epsilon>0
, this is no longer true.
But the
almost homogeneity
yields an alterative but equivalent way to define
d_{l}
: instead of
g_{s}
being multiplied by
n^{d_{l}}
, we let
\epsilon
be multiplied by
n^{-d_{l}}
.

Remark 2.2.7

(For Adam, SP with Tuned LR Learns Features)
.

If one sets the global learning rate for SP (
2.2.2
) to its largest stable value, then for SGD, SP is in the kernel regime
[
45
]
.
But for SignSGD and Adam, assuming perfect scale invariance, SP’s largest stable learning rate is
\Theta(1/n)
, so that with this setting (i.e., setting
c_{l}=1
for every
l
), SP is actually in the feature learning regime.
The reader is not expected to understand the underlying reasoning at this point, but the claim above can be derived by calculating
r=0
from
2.8.5
and invoking
2.8.12
and
2.8.19
.
This difference in default scaling may be a contributing factor to the success of Adam compared to SGD.

2.3
Setup

Here we set up the notation and conventions regarding the data, (pre)activations, and training of the network as well as the main technical assumptions for our rigorous results.

Data and (Pre)Activations

Consider a set of
\mathcal{M}
inputs
\bm{\xi}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
considered
as a
d\times\mathcal{M}
matrix whose columns
\xi^{a},a=1,\ldots,\mathcal{M}

represent individual inputs. Then we write
\bm{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
for
the function outputs on these inputs, and write
\bm{h}_{t}^{l}
and

\bm{x}_{t}^{l}
(with shape
n\times\mathcal{M}
) for the pre- and post-activations
of all of such inputs in layer
l
at time
t
. Similarly, let
d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}

and
d\bm{x}_{t}^{l}
(with shape
n\times\mathcal{M}
) represent the scaled gradients
n^{a_{L}+b_{L}}\nabla_{\bm{h}_{t}^{l}}\bm{f}_{t}

and
n^{a_{L}+b_{L}}\nabla_{\bm{x}_{t}^{l}}\bm{f}_{t}
at time
t
(this
scaling ensures that
d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}
and
d\bm{x}_{t}^{l}
have typical
size
\Theta(1)
entrywise at initialization
t=0
).
2
2

2

Notation-wise, we do not bold the
d
in
d\bm{h}^{l}_{t}
unless the output dimension
e
is larger than 1, in which case
\bm{d}\bm{h}^{l}_{t}
represents
n^{a_{L}+b_{L}}J_{\bm{h}_{t}^{l}}\bm{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times e\times\mathcal{M}}
.
See
2.9.14
.

Error Signal and Training Routine

While
[
45
]
considered only the batch-size-1 case to simplify notation, here, because of our notational advance, we can afford to consider the following more general setting.

Setup 2.3.1
.

We consider the function evolution under an
abcd-parametrization and a sequence of error signal functions
\varepsilon_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}\ (t\geq 0)
,
that returns the output error signal given the function values.
A
training routine
is the package of 1) the learning rate
\eta
, 2) collection
\bm{Q}
of update functions (as in
2.2.1
) and 3) a sequence of
\varepsilon_{t}
as above.

The
\varepsilon_{t}
error signals simultaneously encapsulate both full batch as well as mini-batch training.
For example, we can set
\varepsilon_{t}(\bm{f})=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\bm{f},\bm{y})\odot batchmask_{t}

for some loss function
\mathcal{L}
and a target vector
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
where
batchmask_{t}
is the vector that is 1 on elements in the
batch at time
t
and 0 otherwise.

Furthermore, we can implement train-test split via
\varepsilon_{t}
:
For example,
\bm{\xi}

can be split into two parts,
\bm{\xi}=(\bm{\xi}^{train},\bm{\xi}^{test})
such
that
\varepsilon_{t}
is always 0 on the
\bm{\xi}^{test}
.
Then the evolution of
\bm{f}_{t}
can track the evolution of function values on the test set due to changes from the training set.

The
\varepsilon_{t}
framework more generally covers settings like reinforcement and online learning where the error signal is not obtained from just a simple loss function.

Technical Assumptions

For all rigorous results in this work, we will consider the following smoothness assumption.
This is sufficient for deriving the NTK and
\mu
P limits but more assumptions, stated later, are required for Dynamical Dichotomy, i.e., the classification of abcd-parametrizations.

Assumption 2.3.2

(Smoothness)
.

Assume
\phi^{\prime}
,
\varepsilon_{t}
, and
Q^{l}_{t}
for all
l,t
are pseudo-Lipschitz.
3
3

3

Recall a function
f:\bm{\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}\to\bm{\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}}
is called
pseudo-Lipschitz
if
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq C\|x-y\|(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}|x_{i}|^{d}+|y_{i}|^{d})
for some
C,d>0
.
This is morally the same as saying
f
has a polynomially bounded derivative.

This is a very weak assumption satisfied by typical loss functions (e.g., MSE or cross entropy), update functions (e.g., SGD or Adam
4
4

4

specifically, Adam in the form
Eq.

2.2
with
\epsilon>0
), and nonlinearities (e.g., tanh or gelu).
The notable exception here is that relu itself is not covered because its derivative has a discontinuity.
But this is a common technicality not treated in the theoretical literature.
Nevertheless, we expect all theorems in this work should apply to relu as well and can be proven rigorously in the future.

With this setup in mind, we next describe the two prototypical infinite-width limits, the neural tangent and maximal update limits, for nonlinear entrywise updates before completely classifying the space of abcd-parametrization.

2.4
Neural Tangent

The “classical” neural tangent (abc-)parametrization (NTP) can be generalized easily to an abcd-parametrization using the intuition that the input to any nonlinear update function should be
\Theta(1)
(we won’t go through this calculation here but c.f.
2.8.8
and
2.8.11
).
After defining this generalization next, we adapt the well-known continuous-time heuristic for deriving the NTK limit to the nonlinear updates case (
Eq.

2.7
), before writing down a succinct expression of the infinite-width limit made possible by our new bra-ket notation (
Eqs.

2.8
and
2.9
).

Definition 2.4.1
.

The
neural tangent abcd-parametrization (NTP)

2.5
) by

Recovering “Classical” NTP

Reducing to abc-parametrization in the SGD case via
Eq.

2.6
(i.e., subtracting the last 2 rows), we recover exactly the classical NTP
[
45
, Table 1]
.

2.4.1
Continuous Time Intuition

If we consider
\bm{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
as a function of parameters
\Theta_{t}
over continuous time
t
, then, for SGD, we have the typical equation

\bm{f}^{\prime}_{t}=-\eta\left\langle\frac{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}{\partial\Theta_{t}},\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{t}}{\partial\Theta_{t}}\right\rangle=-\eta\left\langle\frac{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}{\partial\Theta_{t}},\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{t}}{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}\frac{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}{\partial\Theta_{t}}\right\rangle

where
\mathcal{L}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(\bm{f}_{t})
and
\mathcal{L}
is the loss function.
For a general (memoryless stationary) update function
Q
, this just becomes
5
5

5

Technically, we should include terms involving
d_{l}
from
2.2.1
in
Eq.

2.7
, but for simplicity, let’s just assume that
Q
is temporarily redefined to have already included them

\bm{f}^{\prime}_{t}=-\eta\left\langle\frac{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}{\partial\Theta_{t}},Q\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{t}}{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}\frac{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}{\partial\Theta_{t}}\right)\right\rangle,

(2.7)

with
Q
applied entrywise.
In both cases, when width is large in NTP (
2.4.1
), the weights essentially move so little that
\frac{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}{\partial\Theta_{t}}
is invariant to
t
(in so far as this contraction in
Eq.

2.7
is concerned).
What changes from
Q=\mathrm{id}
(SGD case) to the general case is that

\bm{f}^{\prime}_{t}
is no longer linear in the error signal
\varepsilon_{t}(\bm{f}_{t})=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{t}}{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}
; instead, it is the result of a
nonlinear
operator
\mathcal{K}_{Q}
mapping the error signal to the function update:

\bm{f}^{\prime}_{t}=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{Q}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{t}}{\partial\bm{f}_{t}}\right)=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\varepsilon_{t}(\bm{f}_{t})).

When
Q=\mathrm{id}
,
\mathcal{K}_{Q}
reduces to the linear operator represented by the NTK.
But note that
\bm{f}^{\prime}_{t}
is still linear in the learning rate
\eta
for general
Q
.

Now we turn to make this intuition rigorous.

2.4.2
Infinite-Width Limit

We can associate random vectors
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}_{t}^{l}\rangle,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle

to
\bm{x}_{t}^{l}
and
\bm{h}_{t}^{l}
as discussed in
Section

1.2.1
(and similar
to the SGD case studied in
[
45
]
). But as in the case with SGD,
these random vectors will turn out to be independent of
t
(because
of the lack of feature learning). Therefore, we will drop the subscript

t
in the notation below. The construction of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l}\rangle,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}\rangle

follow from the general rules of the
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program we develop
below,
6
6

6

Actually they are the same random vectors as constructed in
[
43
]
since this can be done in
\textsc{Netsor}\top
.

but here they can be stated simply as follows:

Definition 2.4.2
.

For each
l=1,\ldots,L
, the ket
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
is constructed
as a mean-zero Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l-1}\rangle

when
l>1
or
\bm{\xi}^{\top}\bm{\xi}
when
l=1
. Simultaneously,
\bm{x}^{l}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\phi(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}\rangle)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}

for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
7
7

7

\{\bm{h}^{1},\bm{x}^{1}\},\ldots,\{\bm{h}^{L},\bm{x}^{L}\}
are mutually independent
by construction as well, but we will not need this fact.

Likewise, we can associate random vectors
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}\rangle,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle

to gradients
d\bm{x}^{l},d\bm{h}^{l}
(again, suppressing subscript
t
because
it will turn out the kets are independent of
t
).

Definition 2.4.3
.

For each
l=L,\ldots,1
, the ket
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
is independent
from
\{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l}\rangle,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}\rangle\}_{l=1}^{L}
and is a mean-zero
Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
\langle d\bm{h}^{l+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l+1}\rangle

when
l<L
or the all-1s matrix when
l=L
. Simultaneously,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\phi^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}\rangle)\odot\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}

for all
l
.

Memoryless Stationary Case

Finally, having constructed these kets, we can define the generalization
of NTK we need.
To make the formula short, we employ the following convention: we write
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{0}\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{L+1}\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times\mathcal{M}}
is the row vector of 1s.
Then we set
\langle\bm{x}^{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{0}\rangle^{\top}
and
\langle\bm{x}^{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{0}\rangle=\bm{\xi}^{\top}\bm{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}
;
8
8

8

One perhaps would be inclined to rescale
\langle\bm{x}^{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{0}\rangle=\bm{\xi}^{\top}\bm{\xi}/d
, but in our context,
d
is a constant while we only focus on scaling with
n
.
So we choose to be slightly more brief by omitting this scaling with
d
.

likewise for
\langle d\bm{h}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{L+1}\rangle
.
At this point,
the reader may find it helpful to review
Section

1.2.1
, especially on
Bar Notation
.

Definition 2.4.4

(Neural Tangent Operator, Memoryless Stationary Case)
.

For any function
Q:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
we define
the
neural tangent
Q
-operator

\mathcal{K}_{Q}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}

by the following: for any
\bm{\chi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\bm{\chi})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\mathrm{Diag}\sum_{l=1}^{L+1}\langle{d\bm{h}^{l}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}^{l-1}}\rangle

(2.8)

where the “bar” notation abbreviates application of
Q
as in
Eq.

1.2
.

Let’s digest the notation a bit:
In
Eq.

2.8
,
1) the subscript
\bm{\chi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
represents multiplication by
\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi})
,
2)
\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}
is a random scalar variable, and
3)
\langle{d\bm{h}^{l}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}^{l-1}}\rangle
is a deterministic
\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
matrix.
The
\mathrm{Diag}
in
Eq.

2.8
takes its diagonal.
Thus,

\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\bm{\chi})
has entries

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\bm{\chi})^{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L+1}\langle{dh^{la}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{x^{l-1,a}}\rangle

for each
a\in[\mathcal{M}]
.

Example 2.4.5

(SGD Example)
.

As an example, when
Q
is identity, the “bar” can be removed, and
\mathcal{K}_{Q}
reduces to
the linear operator represented by the NTK:
9
9

9

Again,
\bm{\chi}
is a row vector. In prior works, it’s usually treated as a column vector in which case one would write
K\bm{\chi}
instead.

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{id}}(\bm{\chi})

\displaystyle=\bm{\chi}K,\quad\text{where $K\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}$ is the NTK}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\sum_{l=1}^{L+1}\langle d\bm{h}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle\odot\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l-1}\rangle.

Example 2.4.6

(SignSGD Example)
.

As another example, consider
Q=\operatorname{sign}
(
Eq.

SignSGD

with
\epsilon=0
).
If the batch size is 1, i.e.,
\bm{\chi}
is nonzero on exactly one input, say
\xi^{b},b\in[\mathcal{M}]
, then
Eq.

2.8
is linear in
\operatorname{sign}(\bm{\chi})
because
\operatorname{sign}(xy)=\operatorname{sign}(x)\operatorname{sign}(y)
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\bm{\chi})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sign}(\bm{\chi})K_{\operatorname{sign}},\quad\text{where $K_{\operatorname{sign}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}$ is}

\displaystyle K_{\operatorname{sign}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{L+1}\langle{d\bm{h}^{l}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle}\odot\langle{\bm{x}^{l-1}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l-1}\rangle}

for each
a\in[\mathcal{M}]
.
This expression was concurrently derived in
[
28
]
.
However, when batch size is larger than 1,
\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\bm{\chi})
is no longer linear in
\operatorname{sign}(\bm{\chi})
generally, and there is no simplification like this.
In particular, in the continuous time gradient flow setting, SignSGD with a large batch size is
not
equivalent to that with batch size 1 but very small learning rate, in contrast to SGD.

We can now state the generalized Neural Tangent limit for memoryless stationary updates.
In particular, this covers SGD and SignSGD (
Eq.

SignSGD

).

Theorem 2.4.7

(Neural Tangent Limit, Memoryless Stationary Case)
.

Consider any training routine (
2.3.1
) with memoryless stationary update function
Q
(
2.1.1
).
Adopt
2.3.2
.
Then

\bm{f}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{N}(0,\langle\bm{x}^{L}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{L}\rangle)

and for every
t
,
\bm{f}_{t}
converges
almost surely to random function
\mathring{\bm{f}_{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t+1}-\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\varepsilon_{t}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t})),\quad\text{for all $t$}.

(2.9)

The proof can be found in
Section

3.3
.
Note, as in the SGD case,
\mathring{f}_{t}
is deterministic conditioned on
\mathring{f}_{0}
.
We remind the reader that
Eq.

2.9
simultaneously covers full batch, mini-batch, train-test split, and other schemes by changing
\varepsilon_{t}
, as discussed under
2.3.1
.
This will be the same for all theorems in this work.

Memoryful Nonstationary Case

The memoryless stationary condition allowed a clean mathematical formulation of the NT limit.
But we can remove it easily at the cost of some more notation.

Definition 2.4.8

(Neural Tangent Operator, Memoryful Nonstationary Case)
.

For memoryless but nonstationary update functions
\bm{Q}_{t}=\{Q^{l}_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{l}
,
we define
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
with the same equation as
Eq.

2.8
, except the bar notation abbreviates
Q^{l}_{t}
where
l
is the same as in the
d\bm{h}^{l}
under the bar.

For general update functions
\bm{Q}_{t}=\{Q^{l}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t+1}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{l}
,
we define
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}:\mathbb{R}^{(t+1)\times\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
,

\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}(\bm{\chi}_{0},\ldots,\bm{\chi}_{t})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\mathrm{Diag}\sum_{l=1}^{L+1}\langle{d\bm{h}^{l}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{\leq t}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}^{l-1}}\rangle

(2.10)

where
\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{\leq t}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}
is shorthand for
Q^{l}_{t}\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{0}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}},\ldots,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{t}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\right)
.

With this in mind, the following theorem yields the NT limit of Adam (
Eq.

Adam

) as a corollary.

Theorem 2.4.9

(Neural Tangent Limit, Memoryful Nonstationary Case)
.

If the update functions
\bm{Q}
are memoryless but not necessarily stationary, then
2.4.7
holds with
Eq.

2.9
replaced by

\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t+1}-\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}(\varepsilon_{t}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t})),\quad\text{for all $t$}.

(2.11)

For general
\bm{Q}
, not necessarily memoryless,
2.4.7
holds with
Eq.

2.9
replaced by

\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t+1}-\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}(\varepsilon_{0}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{0}),\ldots,\varepsilon_{t}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t})),\quad\text{for all $t$}.

(2.12)

The proof is a straightforward adaptation of the proof of
2.4.7
in
Section

3.3
.

2.4.3
Lack of Feature Learning

Just as for the SGD case, the neural tangent limit cannot learn features, for example, in the sense that the feature kernel (the Gram matrix of the input representations) does not evolve during training (
2.8.19
).
Likewise, pretraining is futile in this limit as finetuning it would be no different than finetuning a randomly initialized network (
2.8.22
).
Nevertheless, the NT limit is “maximal” among all nondegenerate limits without feature learning in that all other limits are just given by a neural tangent operator that involves a subsum of
Eq.

2.8
(
2.8.21
).

2.5

As for NTP, the “classical” maximal update (abc-)parametrization can be generalized easily to an abcd-parametrization using the intuition that the input to any nonlinear update function should be

After defining this generalization next, we study its limit for shallow MLP.
The deep case will have to wait until we develop the new Tensor Program theory in the next section.

2.6
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
: Tensor Program with Nonlinear Outer Products

As mentioned above, the gradient processing done by entrywise optimizers in general cannot be expressed previously by even the most expressive Tensor Program language.
Here we fix this issue by adding “nonlinear outer products” to Tensor Programs.
Like in previous works, we algebraically construct such programs’ limit objects (“kets”, in our new notation) and link them to the analytic properties of vectors in the corresponding programs via a
Master Theorem
.
Unlike previous works, we also construct the limits of matrices, which are operators on kets.
This is purely a conceptual change, but this perspective helps express the deep
\mu
-limit much more efficiently than possible before.

2.6.1
The
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
Language

Definition 2.6.1
.

A
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program
generates a sequence
\bm{x}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-vectors and a sequence
\bm{c}
of
\mathbb{R}
-scalars inductively defined via one of the following ways from an initial set
\bm{c}^{0}\subseteq\bm{c}
of random scalars, an initial set
\bm{x}^{0}\subseteq\bm{x}
of random
\mathbb{R}^{n}
vectors, and an initial
set
\mathcal{W}
of random
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
matrices.
11
11

11

which will be sampled with iid Gaussian entries in
2.6.3
or general non-Gaussian entries in
2.6.4
.

We will think of
\bm{c}
as a vector and
\bm{x}
as a matrix with the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
vectors as columns; then
\bm{c}^{0}
is just a subvector of
\bm{c}
and
\bm{x}^{0}
is a submatrix of
\bm{x}
.
At each step of the program, one can

Avg

choose a vector
x\in\bm{x}
(think of
x
as a column in
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times|\bm{x}|}
) and append to
\bm{c}
a scalar
12
12

12

We replaced the
Moment
instruction of
\textsc{Netsor}\top^{+}
with
Avg
here, but there is no loss of expressivity since
Moment
is just a composition of
Avg
and
Nonlin
+

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}x_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}

(2.16)

MatMul

choose a matrix
W\in\mathcal{W}
and vector
x\in\bm{x}
, and append to
\bm{x}
the vector

Wx\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\quad\text{or}\quad W^{\top}x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

OuterNonlin

choose integer
r\geq 0
and function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{x}|(r+1)+l}\to\mathbb{R}
; append to
\bm{x}
the vector
13
13

13

We can equivalently allow
\bm{x}
in each slot below to be different multi-vectors (which are subsets of
\bm{x}
), since
\psi
here can just be chosen to ignore the irrelevant subsets of
\bm{x}
. For notational simplicity, we don’t do this.

y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad y_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{n^{r}}\sum_{\beta_{1},...,\beta_{r}=1}^{n}\psi(\bm{x}_{\alpha};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}};...;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}};\bm{c})

(2.17)

where
\bm{x}_{\gamma}
is the
\gamma
th row in
\bm{x}
as a matrix and
|\bm{x}|
is the number of vectors in
\bm{x}
.
We call
r+1
the
order
of
\psi
in this context.

Note that while
2.6.1
doesn’t directly give an instruction to transform scalars into scalars (in contrast to
MatMul
and
OuterNonlin
that transforms vectors into vectors), this can be done by combining instructions.

Lemma 2.6.2
.

If
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{c}|}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\bm{c}
are the scalars in a
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program, then
\psi(\bm{c})\in\mathbb{R}
can be introduced as a new scalar in the program.

Proof.

Think of
\psi
as a function that ignores vector arguments and depend only on scalars, and use it in
OuterNonlin
to create the vector whose entries are identically equal to
\psi(\bm{c})
. Applying
Avg
to this vector gives the desired result.
∎

2.6.2
Setups

We are interested in the behavior of
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
programs in two typical settings:

Setup 2.6.3

(Gaussian)
.

Assume
14
14

14

Compared to
[
44
]
, we have WLOG simplified the setup by assuming 1)
\sigma_{W}=1
for every
W
, 2)
Z^{\bm{x}^{0}}=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{0}\rangle=\mathcal{N}(0,I)
, and 3)
\mathring{\theta}=0
for every
\theta\in\bm{c}^{0}
. This is WLOG because
\sigma_{W}
,
\mathring{\theta}
, and the mean and covariance of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{0}\rangle
can all be absorbed into
OuterNonlin
via the appropriate linear functions.

1.

Every entry of every
W\in\mathcal{W}
is sampled iid from
\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
.

2.

Every entry of every initial vector
x\in\bm{x}^{0}
is sampled iid from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

3.

The initial scalars
\bm{c}^{0}
converge almost surely to 0.

4.

All functions
\psi
used in
OuterNonlin
are pseudo-Lipschitz.

Setup 2.6.4

(Non-Gaussian)
.

Assume the same as
2.6.3
but replace 1) and 4) with

1*.

there exists a sequence
\nu_{3},\nu_{4},\ldots>0
such that all matrices have independent entries
15
15

15

For all of our results, it does not matter how the matrices for different
n
are correlated, e.g., whether they are independent or the matrices are all upper left submatrix of fixed infinite iid matrix. This is because our proof only depends on how moments of vectors behave with
n

n
correlations.

drawn from distributions with zero mean, variance

, and all higher

\nu_{k}n^{-k/2}
; and
16
16

\mathcal{N}(0,1)

4*.

17

17

Recall from

.

We further require initial scalars

18

But the moments do not need to be bounded as a function of the order.

While

2.6.3
Limit Objects

As before, when the width
n
of the program goes to infinity, one can infer how the program behaves via a calculus of random variables.
We define them below via the new ket notation instead of the earlier
Z
notation.

Definition 2.6.5

(Ket Construction)
.

We recursively define the random variable
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle
(called a
ket
) for each vector
x
and deterministic number
\mathring{\theta}
for each scalar
\theta
in the program.
For a vector
Wx
produced by
MatMul
, we also define random variables
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\hat{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu
and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\dot{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu
(called
hat-ket
and
dot-ket
respectively) such that
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\rangle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\hat{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu+\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\dot{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu
.
Their recursive definitions are given below.

Init

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{0}\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\mathcal{N}(0,I)\in\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{x}^{0}|}
and
\mathring{\bm{c}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}0\in\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{c}^{0}|}
.
19
19

19

These
Init
rules depend on the fact that, in
2.6.3
and
2.6.4
, initial vectors are sampled with variance 1 and initial scalars converge to 0.

Avg

If
\theta
is generated by
Avg
as in
Eq.

2.16
, then
\mathring{\theta}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle=\langle 1\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle
.

OuterNonlin

If

is generated by

as in

Dot

Every dot-ket is a linear combination of previous kets, expressed by the following equation

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\dot{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle\langle\nabla_{W^{\top}\bm{x}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\bm{x}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\frac{\partial\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle}{\partial\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W^{\top}y\hat{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu}.

by “lowering the index.”

Thus, a more appropriate notation for

is perhaps

so that

\displaystyle\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle\mkern 3.0mu\check{\mkern-3.0mu\langle}W^{\top}\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}

and

Definition 2.6.7
.

Let
W
be an initial matrix in a
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program.
We define
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
to be the linear operator on kets
24
24

24

To be rigorous, we need to specify the “Hilbert space” of kets.
This is somewhat pedantic and not crucial to the key points of this paper, but the Hilbert space can be constructed as follows:
Let
\sigma(\pi)
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the kets of the program
\pi
.
Let
\Sigma(\pi)\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bigcup_{\pi^{\prime}\supseteq\pi}\sigma(\pi)
be the union (more precisely, the direct limit) of
\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
over all programs
\pi^{\prime}
extending
\pi
.
Then the Hilbert space in question is the
L^{2}
space of random variables over the
\Sigma
of our program.

that acts by

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\rangle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\hat{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu+\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\dot{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu.

Any linear operator that is equal to
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
for some initial matrix
W
is called an
initial operator
.
A set of initial operators is called
independent
if their corresponding initial matrices are distinct.
25
25

25

i.e., independently sampled in
2.6.3
or
2.6.4
.

We have already seen an example of a linear operator on kets: expressions like
\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle{}_{\chi}\langle z\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}
.

2.6.7
puts
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
in the same space as

.
This allows us to add them in the sequel, which simplifies the presentation of the
\mu
-limit.

We can immediately see a few properties of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
by considering the counterpart when
n
is finite.

Proposition 2.6.8
.

For any initial matrix
W
, the operator
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
is bounded.
26
26

26

i.e., there exists real number
L>0
such that for any ket
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle
,
\langle Wx\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wx\rangle\leq L\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle
.

Proof.

This follows from the classical operator norm tail bounds on iid random matrices (see, e.g.,
[
38
]
), which passes to the limit via
2.6.10
below.
∎

Proposition 2.6.9
.

For any initial matrix,
the operator
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W^{\top}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
is the adjoint
27
27

27

“adjoint” in the sense of Hilbert space operators; see
Footnote

24
.

of the operator
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
.

Proof.

By
2.6.10
below,
\frac{1}{n}\langle x,Wy\rangle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wy\rangle
and
\frac{1}{n}\langle W^{\top}x,y\rangle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}\langle W^{\top}x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle
.
Since
\langle x,Wy\rangle=\langle W^{\top}x,y\rangle
for any
n
, we have

\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wy\rangle=\langle W^{\top}x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle,

i.e.,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W^{\top}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
is the adjoint of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
.
∎

2.6.4
The Master Theorem

Our key foundational result is that the Master Theorem of earlier Tensor Programs generalizes to
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
programs.
This underlies all of our theorems about adaptive optimization.

Theorem 2.6.10

(
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
Master Theorem)
.

Consider a
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program with (Gaussian)
2.6.3
or (non-Gaussian)
2.6.4
.
Then, as
n\to\infty
, its scalars
\bm{c}
satisfy

\bm{c}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}\mathring{\bm{c}}.

In
2.6.4
, this convergence also happens in
L^{p}
for every
p\in[1,\infty)
.
In either setup, if the initial scalars are all
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
(
1.2.5
), then

\bm{c}-\mathring{\bm{c}}=\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})

as well.

Remark 2.6.11
.

If the initial scalars are
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
, then we can
almost
say that the distribution of
\bm{c}
converge to the delta distribution on
\mathring{\bm{c}}
in Wasserstein distance, at a rate of
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
.
See
Section

4.3.4
.
An analogous
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
-convergence result also holds for vectors converging to their kets (in a suitable sense).
See
Lemma

4.8.2
.

2.7
Maximal Update for Deep MLP

In this section we describe the infinite-width limit of
\mu
P for arbitrarily deep MLP.
The main difference here compared to the shallow case (
Section

2.5.1
) is the presence of
n\times n
iid matrices in the middle of the network, which behaves like initial operators (
2.6.7
) in the limit.

Theorem 2.7.1

(Deep
\mu
-Limit, Memoryless Stationary Case)
.

Consider any training routine (
2.3.1
) with memoryless stationary update function
Q
(
2.1.1
).
Adopt
2.3.2
.
As
n\to\infty
,
\bm{f}_{t}
converges almost surely to some
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}

for every
t
, which is recursively defined from
t=0
by the following
dynamics:
28
28

28

We remind the reader that, in
\mu
P (
2.5.1
),
w_{t}^{L+1}
is the output layer weights normalized so that
w_{t}^{L+1}
has
\Theta(1)
-sized entries (whereas
W_{t}^{L+1}=\frac{1}{n}w_{t}^{L+1}
).
The same point applies to
w_{t}^{1}
(but
W_{t}^{1}=w_{t}^{1}
).
We use lower case
w
for input and output weights while upper case
W
for other layers to emphasize that the former are
vector-like
parameters (one dimension going to
\infty
) while others are
matrix-like
(two dimensions going to
\infty
).

1.

(Forward and Backward Propagation)

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}=\langle w_{t}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{L}_{t}\rangle,\quad\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}=\varepsilon_{t}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t})

Remark 2.7.2

(
\mu
P is Most Natural)
.

Observe that all of the equations above are essentially the “ket” versions of what one does in a finite network.
This holds for general architectures: the
\mu
-limit can always be obtained straightforwardly transcribing the tensor operations in a finite network to their counterparts acting on kets in the infinite-width limit.
See
2.9.25
.
In this sense,
\mu
P is the
most natural parametrization
.

As before, the general case without stationarity or memorylessness is straightforward given
2.7.1
, albeit with some more notation.

Theorem 2.7.3

(Deep
\mu
-Limit, Memoryful Nonstationary Case)
.

is memoryless but not stationary, then

holds if the bars in

and

are interpreted as
Q^{l}_{t}
(where

or

on the LHS).

If

is not memoryless, then

holds if
