\displaystyle\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}_{\leq t}^{l}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{\leq t}}\langle\bm{x}_{\leq t}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}

\displaystyle=Q^{l}_{t}\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}_{0}^{l}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{0}}\langle\bm{x}_{0}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}},\ldots,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}}\langle\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\right)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{L}_{\leq t}\rangle\cdot\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{\leq t}}

\displaystyle=Q^{L+1}_{t}\left({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{L}_{0}\rangle\cdot\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{0}},\ldots,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{L}_{t}\rangle\cdot\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}}\right).

Remark 2.7.4
.

2.5.2
and
2.5.3
also hold if the output dimension is greater than 1, but the equations need to be interpreted slightly differently.
See
Eq.

2.35
.

Remark 2.7.5

(What is
\mu
P “maximal” in?)
.

For SGD,
[
45
]
showed
\mu
P is the
unique
stable parametrization where every weight matrix is updated maximally
[
45
, Defn 5.2]
and the output weight matrix is also initialized maximally
[
45
, Defn 5.4]
.
These definitions still make sense here and this statement holds as well if “stable” is replaced with “stable and faithful” (
2.8.7
,
2.8.8
).

2.8
Dynamical Dichotomy: The Classification of abcd-Parametrizations

In this section, we characterize the infinite-width limits of all possible abcd-parametrizations under reasonable assumptions of the optimizer and network nonlinearity, generalizing the work done for SGD in
[
45
]
.
First, we filter out the uninteresting limits: the unstable (training blows up), the trivial (training gets stuck at initialization), and the unfaithful (the update functions
\bm{Q}
and/or nonlinearities
\phi
are trivialized).
We sort all other limits into a
Dynamical Dichotomy
(
2.8.20
) between feature learning and operator regimes (the latter being the nonlinear version of
kernel regime
in the SGD case).
The
\mu
and NT limits are respectively their archetypes (indeed, the
maximal
parametrizations in these regimes (
2.8.21
)).
Like in the SGD case, this dichotomy is not tautological: it implies certain network training dynamics cannot be the infinite-width limit of any abcd-parametrization (
2.8.24
).
Likewise, pretraining is still futile in the operator regime even with adaptive optimizers (
2.8.22
).

Our results here hold for not only memoryless stationary but also memoryful nonstationary updates.

2.8.1
Technical Assumptions

Definition 2.8.1
.

We say a function
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

preserves positivity
if
F(x)>0

whenever
x>0
. We say it
preserves sign
if
\operatorname{sign}F(x)=\operatorname{sign}(x)

for all
x
(where
\operatorname{sign}
takes value in
\{-1,0,1\}
).

For proving the Dynamical Dichotomy Theorem for entrywise updates, we will make the following technical assumptions.
Roughly speaking, we will focus only on “relu-like” nonlinearities and sign-preserving update functions with mild smoothness.

Assumption 2.8.2
.

Suppose

1.

\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are nonnegative and pseudo-Lispchitz.

2.

\phi
preserves positivity.

3.

There exists
\delta>0
such that
t^{\delta}\phi(x/t)
converges uniformly (as a function in
x\in\mathbb{R}
) to
\mathrm{relu}(x)^{\delta}
as
t\to 0
.

4.

Q^{l}_{t}
is pseudo-Lipschitz for all
l,t
, and
Q^{l}_{0}
preserves sign for all
l
.

Remark 2.8.3

(Sufficiency)
.

As in
[
45
]
, the pseudo-Lipschitzness of
\phi,\phi^{\prime},Q^{l}_{t}
are sufficient for letting us use our new Master Theorem (
2.6.10
) to take the limits of any parametrization, get the operator limits, and prove their properties.
Any assumptions beyond such is required only for proving that
r=0
implies feature learning (
3.1.3
).
In particular, the reason we only require
Q^{l}_{0}
to preserve signs (instead of for all
t
) is because we will only need to show that features evolve in the first step.

Remark 2.8.4

(Necessity)
.

2.8.2
is satisfied by typical smooth versions of relu like gelu and its powers.
However, note that these are only a specific set of conditions that allow us to easily prove our desired result and are likely very far from a necessary set of conditions.
For example, 1) the pseudo-Lipschitz conditions can likely be relaxed to allow
\phi=\mathrm{relu}
and
Q^{l}_{t}=\operatorname{sign}
, and 2) the uniform convergence in
2.8.2
certainly can be relaxed to some measure-theoretic convergence.
In fact, we expect all theorems below in this section to hold for
generic
activations and update functions.
We leave this to future work.

2.8.2
Size of Feature Learning

In
[
45
]
, the number
r
of an abc-parametrization measures how much the features change over training.
We adapt its definition to abcd-parametrizations.

Definition 2.8.5
.

Define

r_{l}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\begin{cases}c_{l}+a_{l}-1&\text{if $l>1$}\\
c_{l}+a_{l}&\text{if $l=1$}\end{cases},\quad r_{\leq l}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\min_{m=1}^{l}r_{l},\quad r\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}r_{\leq L}

Morally, in any “reasonable” abcd-parametrization (in the sense of stable (
2.8.7
) and faithful (
2.8.8
) discussed below), we have
\Delta W_{t}^{l}\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}=\Theta(n^{-r_{l}})

and
\Delta\bm{x}_{t}^{L}=\Theta(n^{-r})
, where
\Delta\bullet_{t}=\bullet_{t}-\bullet_{0}
is the cumulative change of
\bullet_{t}
.
Concretely, for NTP and
\mu
P we have, for all
l\in[1,L]
,

\displaystyle r=r_{l}

\displaystyle=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\quad\text{in NTP (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{defn:NTP})}

\displaystyle r=r_{l}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{in $\mu$P (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{defn:mup})}

(2.23)

The reader should sanity check that
r_{l}
and
r
are invariant to the symmetry in
Eq.

2.5
.

Remark 2.8.6
.

Ostensibly,
2.8.5
is different from and much simpler than
[
45
, Defn 3.2]
for abc-parametrizations.
But, in fact, they are equivalent for stable and faithful abcd-parametrizations (under the reduction
Eq.

2.6
to abc-parametrizations).
The comparative simplicity of
2.8.5
is also due to the faithfulness.

2.8.3
Stability and Faithfulness

We will only care about any parametrization satisfying two basic properties:
1) does not blow up during at initialization or during training as
width
\to\infty
and 2) does not trivialize the update functions

Q_{t}^{l}
. The former property is known as
stability
and has already
been studied in
[
45
]
for SGD. The latter is specific to nonlinear entrywise updates, and we will call this the
faithful
property. By “trivialize,”
we mean that the input to
Q_{t}^{l}
is either i) too small and
thus linearizing
Q_{t}^{l}
around the origin or ii) too large and
only depends on
Q_{t}^{l}
’s behavior “around infinity”. Both
scenarios ignore the bulk of
Q_{t}^{l}
’s values as a function.
If such behaviors are actually desired, then one can change
Q^{l}_{t}
to such effects.
For example, the linearizing behavior in case
i) can be implemented by choosing a linear
Q_{t}^{l}
and modifying

c_{l},d_{l}
appropriately so that the input to
Q_{t}^{l}
has
constant typical size (wrt width).

Recall from
Section

1.2.3
the
entry-wise semantics
of big-O notation.
We formalize the
stability
and
faithfulness
properties below.

Definition 2.8.7

(Stability)
.

We say an abcd-parametrization of an
L
-hidden layer MLP is

1.

stable at initialization
if

\bm{h}_{0}^{l},\bm{x}_{0}^{l}=\Theta(1),\forall l\in[L],\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{f}_{0}=O(1).

(2.24)

2.

stable during training
if for any training routine, any time
t\geq 0
,
l\in[L]
, we have

\Delta\bm{h}_{t}^{l},\Delta\bm{x}_{t}^{l}=O(1),\forall l\in[L],\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta\bm{f}_{t}=O(1).

We say the parametrization is
stable
if it is stable both at initialization and during training.

Definition 2.8.8
.

We say an abcd-parametrization is
faithful at time
t
if the input
to
Q_{{}_{t}}^{l}
is
\Theta(1)
for every
l\in[L]
.
We also say it is
faithful at initialization
if this is true at
t=0
.

Remark 2.8.9
.

The condition
\bm{h}_{0}^{l},\bm{x}_{0}^{l}=\Theta(1)
in
Eq.

2.24
is in truth more of a “faithfulness to
\phi
” condition than just stability (which would strictly speaking be more like
O(1)
than
\Theta(1)
).
But we never need to distinguish these notions, so following
[
45
]
, we will keep the definition as is.

Lemma 2.8.10
.

Adopt
2.8.2
. An abcd-parametrization is stable at initialization iff

a_{1}+b_{1}=0;\quad a_{l}+b_{l}=1/2,\ \forall l\in[2,L];\quad\text{and}\ \ a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}\geq 1/2.

(2.25)

This condition is the same as
[
45
, Thm H.6(1)]
.
For example, SP, NTP, and
\mu
P are all stable at initialization.
In this situation, some easy calculation shows that, at initialization, the last layer gradients have entry size
\Theta(n^{-a_{L+1}})
and while all other layers’ gradients have entry size
\Theta(n^{-a_{l}-a_{L+1}-b_{L+1}})
.
Hence,

Lemma 2.8.11
.

In
Lemma

2.8.10
, the abcd-parametrization is furthermore faithful at initialization iff

d_{l}=a_{l}+a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}\text{\ \ for $l\leq L$}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{L+1}=a_{L+1}.

(2.26)

For example, NTP and
\mu
P are faithful at initialization but SP (
2.2.2
) is not (but if
\bm{Q}
is scale invariant then SP is equivalent to a faithful parametrization).

Theorem 2.8.12

(Stability and Faithfulness Characterization)
.

Adopt
2.8.2
.
Suppose, at initialization, an abcd-parametrization is both faithful and stable. Then it remains so for all
t\geq 1
iff

r_{l}\geq 0\ \text{ for all }l\in[L+1]\quad\text{and}\quad a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}+r\geq 1\quad\text{and}\quad b_{L+1}\leq c_{L+1}.

(2.27)

In words: 1)
r_{l}\geq 0
for all
l\in[L]
ensures the features do not blow up, while
2)
r_{L+1}\geq 0
and
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}+r\geq 1
resp. ensure that
W_{t}^{L+1}\bm{x}_{t}^{L},W_{0}^{L+1}\Delta\bm{x}_{t}^{L}=O(1)
, so
\bm{f}_{t}
does not blow up;
29
29

29

Recall
\Delta\bullet_{t}=\bullet_{t}-\bullet_{0}
is the cumulative change of
\bullet_{t}
.

finally, 3)
b_{L+1}\leq c_{L+1}
ensures that
W^{L+1}
does not change scale after updates, since otherwise the
d_{l}
in
Eq.

2.26
is no longer faithful.

Remark 2.8.13
.

One can check that
2.8.12
reduces to
[
45
, Thm 3.3]
(the stability characterization of abc-parametrizations) plus the additional constraint that
W^{L+1}_{t}=O(W^{L+1}_{0})
for all
t
(which is imposed by
b_{L+1}\leq c_{L+1}
in
Eq.

2.27
).
As remarked above, this constraint is due to the faithfulness requirement.
But in fact, if we allow
d_{l}
to vary during training, then this constraint can be removed and the appropriate version of
2.8.12
would be equivalent to
[
45
, Thm 3.3]
.

2.8.4
Nontriviality

Even among stable and faithful parametrizations, we are only interested in
nontrivial
parametrizations (as in
[
45
]
),
where in the infinite width limit the network will not be stuck at
initialization during training.

Definition 2.8.14

(Nontriviality)
.

We say an abcd-parametrization of an
L
-hidden layer MLP is
trivial
if for every training routine and any time
t\geq 1
,
\bm{f}_{t}-\bm{f}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0
as
n\to\infty
(i.e., the function does not evolve in the infinite-width limit). We say the parametrization is
nontrivial
otherwise.

Nontriviality is characterized by a
disjunction
of equations in
a_{l},b_{l},c_{l}
, just as for SGD in
[
45
]
.

Theorem 2.8.15
.

Adopt
2.8.2
.
A stable and faithful abcd-parametrization is nontrivial iff

a_{L+1}+c_{L+1}=1\quad\text{or}\quad a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}+r=1.

(2.28)

This is essentially equivalent to
[
45
, Thm 3.4]
.
For example, NTP and
\mu
P are both nontrivial.

2.8.5
Feature Learning and Operator Regimes

Finally, we ask: what are the different possible behaviors among the nontrivial, stable, and faithful parametrizations?
As in
[
45
]
, we will see a dichotomy between feature learning and a
nonlinear
version of the kernel regime which we call the
operator regime
.

Definition 2.8.16

(The Operator Regime)
.

For memoryless stationary updates,
we say an abcd-parametrization of an
L
-hidden layer MLP
is in the operator regime
if there exists a function
\mathcal{K}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
, which we call an
operator
, such that for all training routines and every
t\geq 0
, as width
n\to\infty
,

\bm{f}_{t+1}-\bm{f}_{t}-\eta\mathcal{K}(\bm{f}_{t})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0.

(2.29)

For memoryless, nonstationary updates, we allow
\mathcal{K}
to depend on
t
.
For general entrywise updates, we make the same definition if for all
t
,

\bm{f}_{t+1}-\bm{f}_{t}-\eta\mathcal{K}_{t}(\bm{f}_{0},\ldots,\bm{f}_{t})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0.

(2.30)

for some
\mathcal{K}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{(t+1)\times\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
.

Notice that the operator regime is defined solely in the
function space
, without talking about the internals of the network, in contrast to feature learning.

Definition 2.8.17

(Feature Learning)
.

We say an abcd-parametrization of an
L
-hidden layer MLP
admits feature learning in the
l
th layer
if there exists some training routine such that

\Delta\bm{x}_{t}^{l}=\Omega(1)

(2.31)

for some
t\geq 0
.
We say the parametrization
admits feature learning
if it does so in any layer.

We say the parametrization
fixes the
l
th layer features
if for all training routine,

\|\Delta\bm{x}_{t}^{l}\|^{2}/n\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

for all
t\geq 0
. We say the parametrization
fixes all features
if it does so in every layer.

We make similar definitions as above replacing
feature
with
prefeature
and
\bm{x}^{l}
with
\bm{h}^{l}
.

Definition 2.8.18

(Feature Kernel Evolution)
.

We say an abcd-parametrization of an
L
-hidden layer MLP
evolves the
l
th layer feature kernel
if there exists some training routine such that

\bm{x}_{t}^{l}\bm{x}_{t}^{l\top}/n-\bm{x}_{0}^{l\top}\bm{x}_{0}^{l}/n=\Omega(1)

for some
t\geq 0
.
We say the parametrization
evolves feature kernels
if it does so in any layer.

We say the parametrization
fixes the
l
th layer feature kernel
if for all training routine,

\bm{x}_{t}^{l\top}\bm{x}_{t}^{l}/n-\bm{x}_{0}^{l\top}\bm{x}_{0}^{l}/n\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0,\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty,

for all
t\geq 0
. We say the parametrization
fixes all feature kernels
if it does so in every layer.

We make similar definitions as above replacing
feature
with
prefeature
and
x^{l}
with
h^{l}
.

2.8.6
Classification of abcd-Parametrizations

Figure 2.1:
A Caricature of abcd-Parametrizations.

The nontrivial stable faithful parametrizations form a high dimensional polyhedron.
Those on a part of its boundary admit feature learning, while all others are in the operator regime.

\mu
P is a vertex in the former, while NTP, latter.
The overall shape is similar to
[
45
, Fig. 2]

The classification of abcd-parametrizations is similar to that of abc-parametrizations
[
45
, Thm H.13]
.
We remind the reader that this holds for not only memoryless stationary but more generally memoryful nonstationary updates.

Theorem 2.8.19
.

Adopt
2.8.2
. Consider a nontrivial, stable, and faithful abcd-parametrization of an
L
-hidden layer MLP. Then

1.

The following are equivalent to
r=0

(a)

feature learning

(b)

feature learning in the
L
th layer

(c)

feature kernels evolution

(d)

feature kernel evolution in the
L
th layer

(e)

prefeature learning

(f)

prefeature learning in the
L
th layer

(g)

prefeature kernels evolution

(h)

prefeature kernel evolution in the
L
th layer

2.

The following are equivalent to
r>0

(a)

the operator regime

(b)

fixes all features

(c)

fixes features in the
L
th layer

(d)

fixes all feature kernels

(e)

fixes feature kernel in the
L
th layer

(f)

fixes all prefeatures

(g)

fixes prefeatures in the
L
th layer

(h)

fixes all prefeature kernels

(i)

fixes prefeature kernel in the
L
th layer

3.

If there is feature learning
or
feature kernel evolution
or
prefeature learning
or
prefeature kernel evolution in layer
l
, then there is feature learning
and
feature kernel evolution
and
prefeature learning
and
prefeature kernel evolution in layers
l,\ldots,L
.

4.

If
r=0
, then
\bm{f}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0
and
\bm{f}_{t}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}
for some deterministic
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}
.
However, the converse is not true.

Consequently, we can generalize Dynamical Dichotomy to (nonlinear) entrywise updates.

Corollary 2.8.20

(Dynamical Dichotomy)
.

A nontrivial, stable, and faithful abcd-parametrization either admits feature learning or is in the operator regime, but not both.

Of course, the canonical examples here are
\mu
P in the feature learning regime and NTP in the operator regime.
In the SGD case,
2.8.19
and
2.8.20
are equivalent to their counterparts
[
45
, Thm H.13, Cor H.14]
other than a minor technical difference as discussed in
2.8.13
.

Remark 2.8.21

(Maximality)
.

The
\mu
and NT limits are resp. the “maximal” limits in the feature learning and operator regimes, in the sense that all parameter tensors contribute to the function update, and that any other limits in those regimes are just “downgrades” of
\mu
and NT limits by zeroing out the initialization or learning rate of some parameters.
See also
2.7.5
.

Remark 2.8.22

(Pretraining is still futile in the operator regime even with adaptive optimizers)
.

[
45
, Thm H.17]
holds almost verbatim in our case as well, after replacing “stable” with “stable and faithful” and “kernel regime” with “operator regime”:
Finetuning any pretrained network in the operator regime would be equivalent to finetuning a randomly initialized network.
Thus, pretraining in the operator regime is useless.

Remark 2.8.23

(Function Space Picture)
.

In the memoryless stationary case, an operator regime limit resides solely in the
function space picture
, i.e.
\bm{f}_{t+1}
being solely determined by the function values
\bm{f}_{t}
themselves (as opposed to the internal activations of
f
as well) along with learning rate
\eta
and error signals
\varepsilon_{t}
.
However, as in
[
45
, Remark 3.11]
, this is not true of any feature learning limit because one can construct counterexamples where
\bm{f}_{t}
are close for two infinite-width limits but
\bm{f}_{t+1}
are far.

Remark 2.8.24

(Not All Dynamics are Infinite-Width Limits)
.

Compared to the kernel regime in the SGD case, the operator regime now allows nonlinear evolution in the function space picture.
Nevertheless, in such dynamics,
\bm{f}_{t+1}-\bm{f}_{t}

must be linear in
\eta
for every
t
.
Thus, any function space evolution nonlinear in
\eta
cannot be the infinite-width limit of any entrywise optimizer.
30
30

30

The same holds for any adaptive optimizer with ingredients discussed under
Optimizer Coverage
of
Section

2.1
.

For example,
\bm{f}_{t+1}-\bm{f}_{t}=-\eta\bm{f}_{t}-\eta^{2}\bm{f}_{t}^{2}
is not a valid limit.

Remark 2.8.25

(Uniform Parametrization)
.

[
45
, Sec G]
identified a subclass of abc-parametrizations, called
uniform parametrizations
, where all layers “learn the same amount of features” and the output layer is initialized and updated maximally.
This is used in
[
40
]
to give an alternative presentation of
\mu
P as well as discussion of joint width-depth limit.
This notion also makes sense for abcd-parametrizations:
For every
s\in[0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}]
, there is a unique stable and faithful abcd-parametrization, called
UP
s
such that
r_{l}=s
for all
l=1,\ldots,L
and
r_{L+1}=1
and
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}=1-s
.
For example, UP
0
is
\mu
P and UP
{}_{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
is NTP.

2.9
Infinite-Width Limits for Any Architecture

Having written down the infinite-width limits of adaptive optimizers for MLPs, we now turn to general architectures.
An astute reader may have already absorbed the key insights from the previous sections and can use them to derive the NT or
\mu
limits for each new architecture in an
ad hoc
fashion.
In contrast, here we describe the algorithm to do this once and for all, uniformly for all “reasonable” architectures.
This uniformity of course requires abstraction, which is not conducive to quick comprehension; on the flip side, this algorithm will always be here for someone to fall back to if the
ad hoc
approach does not work out.

The main work here is not the proofs (which can be easily adapted from the MLP case and so are omitted), but the
definitions
: What is an architecture? What architecture counts as “reasonable”? How to define abcd-parametrization for any such architecture? What are their infinite-width limits?
Answering these questions in the most generality requires careful thought.

2.9.1
Representating Architectures via Tensor Programs

Definition 2.9.1

(Architecture and Representability)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{n}=(\mathbb{R})^{j}\oplus(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{k}\oplus(\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})^{l}
.
We shall call this the
parameter space with
l
matrix,
k
vector, and
j
scalar parameters.

A function family
f=\{f_{n}(-;-):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{T}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{e}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
(with fixed input and output dimensions
d
and
e
independent of
n
) is called an
architecture
.
It has
l
matrix,
k
vector, and
j
scalar parameters.

We say an architecture
f
is
representable
if there is a
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program
\pi
and vectors
x^{1},\ldots,x^{e}
in
\pi
such that
1)
\pi
has
j+d
initial scalars,
k
initial vectors, and
l
initial matrices; and
2) for any
n
, when they are instantiated with
\xi\in(\mathbb{R})^{d}
and
\Theta\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
,
31
31

31

In particular, the first
j
initial scalars are set to the
(\mathbb{R})^{j}
part of
\Theta
and the last
d
scalars are set to
\xi
.

the sum of
x^{i}
’s entries yields
f_{n}(\xi;\Theta)_{i}
for each
i\in[{e}]
.
In this case,
(\pi,x^{1},\ldots,x^{e})
is called a
representation
of
f
, and
\pi
is called a
representation program
of
f
.

As demonstrated in
[
41
,
43
]
,
2.9.1
covers essentially every architecture in practice: RNNs, residual network, transformers, etc.

Remark 2.9.2
.

In this definition, only the symbolic structure of the program matters; the random sampling of
2.6.3
and
2.6.4
plays no role.

Remark 2.9.3
.

For simplicity, we only considered the case when the notion of “width” is the same throughout the network.
Nevertheless, this definition can be easily modified to cover the nonuniform case, but stating it would be much more complex.

Remark 2.9.4
.

In truth, we could have phrased
2.9.1
using
\textsc{Netsor}\top
instead of
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
, since we do not know of any neural network in the wild that is not
\textsc{Netsor}\top
-representable (
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
is only required for expressing adaptive optimization like Adam).
But there is little cost in stating the more general version, which can potentially matter in the future.

Remark 2.9.5
.

[
46
]
also defined a notion of
representable functions
using
\textsc{Netsor}\top
.
In comparison, our definition is much more general: Beyond the superficial difference of
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
here vs
\textsc{Netsor}\top
there,
[
46
]
dealt with input and output layer weights in special ways, whereas here we do not, instead opting to uniformly deal with scalar, vector, and matrix parameters.
The input and output weights of
[
46
]
are vector parameters in this view.

Example 2.9.6
.

The
L
-hidden-layer MLP in
Eq.

2.3
has
d+1
vector parameters (where
d
corresponds to input dimension and
1
corresponds to output dimension) and
L-1
matrix parameters.
It is represented by the program that generates 1)
h^{1}
using
OuterNonlin
and
h^{l},l\geq 2,
using
MatMul
; 2)
x^{l}
using
OuterNonlin
; and 3) generate function output by summing
W^{L+1}\odot x^{L}
(so we can take
x^{1}
in
2.9.1
to be
W^{L+1}\odot x^{L}
).

2.9.2
abcd-Parametrization for Any Architecture

Definition 2.9.7

(abcd-Parametrization for Representable Architectures)
.

Consider a representable architecture with representation program
\pi
.
Fix a set of update functions
\bm{Q}=\{Q^{W}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t+1}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{t\geq 0,W}
where
W
ranges over every matrix, vector, and scalar parameters.
An
abcd-parametrization
of this architecture is specified by
a_{W},b_{W},c_{W},d_{W}
for each such
W
, along with an additional number
a_{\mathrm{out}}
, such that

(a)

We parametrize
W
as
W=n^{-a_{W}}w
for actual
trainable parameter
w
;

(b)

We initialize each entry of
w
iid from
\mathcal{N}(0,n^{-2b_{W}})
;

(c)

The learning rate is
\eta n^{-c_{W}}
for some width-independent

\eta
;

(d)

The gradients of
w
are multiplied by
n^{d_{W}}
before being
processed by
Q^{W}_{t}
: i.e., the update at time
t
is

w\leftarrow w-\eta n^{-c_{W}}Q^{W}_{t}(n^{d_{W}}g_{0},\ldots,n^{d_{W}}g_{t})

where
g_{s},s=0,\ldots,t
, are the gradients of
w
at time

s
and
Q^{W}_{t}
is applied entrywise;

and the function output is multiplied by
n^{-a_{\mathrm{out}}}
.
32
32

32

i.e.,
f_{n}(\xi;\Theta)_{i}=n^{-a_{\mathrm{out}}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}x^{i}_{\alpha}
instead of just a plain sum.

Remark 2.9.8
.

In the MLP case, the
a_{\mathrm{out}}
was absorbed into
a_{L+1}
.
But in the generality of
2.9.1
, output weights are not singled out,
33
33

33

In fact, output weights may be ill defined in some architectures, such as if
f(\xi)=\sum_{\alpha}x^{L}(\xi)_{\alpha}
.

so we need to separately specify
a_{\mathrm{out}}
.

Remark 2.9.9
.

As always, we are only concerned with scaling with
n
here, but there can be a tunable constant hyperparameter in front of every power of
n
in
2.2.1
.

Remark 2.9.10
.

The random initialization in
2.9.7
is always mean-zero.
For some applications, such as layernorm/batchnorm weights
W
(that is initialized as all 1s), this may seem insufficient.
However, one can just refactor the parameter:
For this particular example, we can refactor
W=1+W^{\prime}
where
W^{\prime}
is the initial vector of the program
\pi
.

W^{\prime}
can then be initialized as
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma n^{-2b_{W^{\prime}}})
for some tunable constant hyperparameter
\sigma
(which is set to 0 by practitioners typically).

NTP and
\mu
P naturally generalize to general representable architectures.

Definition 2.9.11

(NTP for general architecture)
.

For any representable architecture, its
Neural Tangent Parametrization (NTP)
is defined by the following setting of
a,b,c,d
for matrix, vector, and scalar parameters as well as the output multiplier
a_{\mathrm{out}}
.

matrix

vector

scalar

out

a

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

0

0

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

b

0

0

0

-

c

1

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

0

-

d

1

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

0

-

Definition 2.9.12

(
\mu
P for general architecture)
.

For any representable architecture, its
Maximal Update Parametrization (
\mu
P)
is defined by the following setting of
a,b,c,d
for matrix, vector, and scalar parameters as well as the output multiplier
a_{\mathrm{out}}
.

matrix

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

Remark 2.9.13
.

In comparison to their counterparts for MLP, the NTP and
\mu
P above consider scalar parameters (which are not present in the MLP in
Eq.

2.3
).
Otherwise,
2.4.1
can be recovered from
2.9.11
by mapping the columns
[2,L]
to “matrix,”
1
to “vector,” and
L+1
to “vector” but with the value of
a
taken from the “out” column.

2.5.1
can be recovered likewise from
2.9.12
.

Before we formulate their limits, we need to discuss how to construct the “backpropagation” of an arbtrary of
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program.
34
34

34

This has been constructed previously for
\textsc{Netsor}\top
programs in
[
12
]
.

2.9.3
Interlude: Backpropagation and Total Programs

Recall that
when
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the notation
\langle x_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha}
denotes its average entry; this applies more generally to tensors, such as
\langle x_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{r}}\rangle_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{r}}
when
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\cdots\times n}=(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes r}
.

Definition 2.9.14

(Backpropagation Program)
.

Consider any
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program
\pi
and a vector
x
in
\pi
.
Then
\pi
’s backpropagation program wrt
x
is an extension of
\pi
defined by constructing the following objects on top of
\pi
:
(Intuitively, one should interpret
d^{x}y=n\frac{\partial\langle x_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\partial y}
if
y
is a vector and
d^{x}c=\frac{\partial\langle x_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\partial c}
if
c
is a scalar.)

•

d^{x}x:=1_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

•

For any
MatMul
instruction
z:=Wy
in
\pi
, we construct
d^{x|z}y:=W^{\top}d^{x}z
(via another
MatMul
)

•

For any
Avg
instruction
c:=\langle z_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha}
in
\pi
, we construct
d^{x|c}z:=(d^{x}c){1}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
(via
OuterNonlin
)

•

Suppose
y=\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}};\bm{c})\rangle_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{r}}
.
For each
i=0,\ldots,r
,
let

\bm{g}^{i}_{\beta_{0}\cdots\beta_{r}}=d^{x}y_{\alpha}\bm{\psi}_{i}(\bm{x}_{\beta_{0}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}};\bm{c})\in\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{x}|},

where
\bm{\psi}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{x}|(r+1)+l}\to\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{x}|}
yields the derivative of
\psi
against
\bm{x}
in the
i
th slot.
When
i=r+1
, we make the analogous definition for
\bm{g}^{r+1}_{\beta_{0}\cdots\beta_{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{c}|}
.
We write
{\bm{\beta}}=(\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{r})
,
{\bm{\beta}}[i\mapsto\alpha]=(\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{i-1},\alpha,\beta_{i+1},\ldots,\beta_{r})
, and
{\bm{\beta}}_{-i}=(\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{i-1},\beta_{i+1},\ldots,\beta_{r})
.
Then we construct
d^{x|y}\bm{c}=(d^{x|y}c^{1},\ldots,d^{x|y}c^{|\bm{c}|})
and
d^{x|y}\bm{x}=(d^{x|y}x^{1},\ldots,d^{x|y}x^{|\bm{x}|})
by

\displaystyle d^{x|y}\bm{c}

\displaystyle:=\langle\bm{g}^{r+1}_{\bm{\beta}}\rangle_{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{c}|}

OuterNonlin
+
Avg

\displaystyle d^{x|y}\bm{x}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=0}^{r}\langle\bm{g}^{i}_{{\bm{\beta}}[i\mapsto\alpha]}\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}_{-i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\bm{x}|}

Explicitly,

\displaystyle d^{x|y}\bm{x}_{\alpha}=\langle\bm{g}^{0}_{\alpha\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{r}}\rangle_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{r}}+\langle\bm{g}^{1}_{\beta_{0}\alpha\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{r}}\rangle_{\beta_{0}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{r}}+\cdots+\langle\bm{g}^{r}_{\beta_{0}\cdots\beta_{r-1}\alpha}\rangle_{\beta_{0}\cdots\beta_{r-1}}

•

Finally, for every vector or scalar
y
in
\pi
other than
x
,

\displaystyle d^{x}y:=\sum_{u}d^{x|u}y

where
u
ranges over all vector or scalar in
\pi
whose construction used
y
.
35
35

35

If this sum is empty, then the RHS is set to 0.

The subprogram
36
36

36

The notion of
subprogram
is formally defined in
[
44
, Defn I.1]
. Roughly it means a contiguous subset of instructions in the program.

constructing all of these new objects is denoted
d^{x}\pi
(so that the backpropagation program is
\pi|d^{x}\pi
).

Recall (
[
44
, Defn I.1]
) that “
|
” (as in “
\pi|\pi^{\prime}
”) signifies the concatenation of programs.

Remark 2.9.15
.

If
r=0
in
OuterNonlin
(i.e., we just have a
Nonlin+
instruction), then the formulas simplify to

\displaystyle d^{x|y}\bm{c}

\displaystyle:=\langle d^{x}y_{\alpha}\bm{\psi}_{1}(\bm{x}_{\alpha};\bm{c})\rangle_{\alpha}

\displaystyle d^{x|y}\bm{x}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=y_{\alpha}\bm{\psi}_{0}(\bm{x}_{\alpha};\bm{c}).

Definition 2.9.16

(Total Program
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
)
.

Consider a representable architecture
f
with representation
(\pi,x^{1},\ldots,x^{e})
.
Gather all of
\pi
’s backpropagation programs wrt
x^{i}
into a single (large) program:

\bm{d}\pi\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\pi|d^{x^{1}}\pi|\cdots|d^{x^{e}}\pi.

Let
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an input to
f
.
Then
\bm{d}\pi(\xi)
will denote the program
\bm{d}\pi
with
\xi
inserted as the appropriate initial scalars (c.f.
2.9.1
).
As in
Section

2.3
, we consider a set of
\mathcal{M}
inputs
\bm{\xi}=\{\xi^{1},\ldots,\xi^{\mathcal{M}}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then we set

\bm{d}\bm{\pi}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{d}\pi(\xi^{1})|\cdots|\bm{d}\pi(\xi^{\mathcal{M}}),

whose initial data are the
j
scalars,
k
vectors, and
l
matrices corresponding to
\mathcal{T}_{n}
in
2.9.1
; they are shared among all subprograms
\bm{d}\pi(\xi^{i})
.
We call
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
the
total program
of
f
.

For any vector
y
in
\pi
, we write
\bm{y}=(y(\xi^{1}),\ldots,y(\xi^{\mathcal{M}}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\mathcal{M}}
to denote the multi-vector of its counterparts in
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
.
We also write
\bm{d}\bm{y}=(d^{x^{j}}y(\xi^{a}))_{j\in[e],a\in[\mathcal{M}]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times e\times\mathcal{M}}
for the multi-vector in
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
of its error signals.

2.9.4
Training Setup

The setup in
Section

2.3
is defined with the MLP in mind, but can be generalized easily to deal with general architectures, as we do here for clariy:
1) Because we consider multi-dimensional outputs here, the error signal functions
\varepsilon_{t}
obviously need to take the more general signature
\varepsilon_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}
.
2) The update functions
\bm{Q}
now contain a
Q^{\Theta}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t+1}\to\mathbb{R}
for every matrix, vector, and scalar parameter
\Theta
.

Memoryless
still means
Q^{\Theta_{t}}
only depends on its last argument, and
stationary
still means
Q^{\Theta}_{t}
is the same regardless of
\Theta
and
t
.
3) The smoothness assumption we require is the natural adaptation of
2.3.2
:

Assumption 2.9.17

(Smoothness)
.

Assume
\varepsilon_{t}
and
Q^{\Theta}_{t}
for all
\Theta,t
are pseudo-Lipschitz and all nonlinearities used in the representing program has pseudo-Lipschitz derivatives.

2.9.5
Neural Tangent Limit

Memoryless Stationary Case

Definition 2.9.18

(Neural Tangent Operator for General Architecture)
.

Fix a representable architecture
f
with representation
(\pi,x^{1},\ldots,x^{e})
.
For any function
Q:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
we define
the
neural tangent
Q
-operator

\mathcal{K}_{Q}:\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}

by the following: for any
\bm{\chi}\in\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{Q}^{W}(\bm{\chi})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\mathrm{Diag}_{\mathcal{M}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h=Wz\\
g=Wy\end{subarray}}\langle{\bm{d}\bm{g}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}}\langle\bm{z}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{y}}\rangle

(2.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{Q}^{v}(\bm{\chi})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\langle{\bm{d}\bm{v}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{v}\rangle\cdot\bm{\chi}}

(2.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\bm{\chi})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\sum_{W}\mathcal{K}_{Q}^{W}(\bm{\chi})+\sum_{v}\mathcal{K}_{Q}^{v}(\bm{\chi})

(2.34)

where the “bar” notation abbreviates application of
Q
as in
Eq.

1.2
and all kets and bras are evaluated in
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
(
2.9.16
) via
2.6.5
.
To interpret these formulas, we need to tell you two things:

1) Ranges of arguments.

Here,
W
ranges over all matrix parameters and
v
over all vector parameters of
f
, and all kets are calculated from
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
by sampling matrix parameters from
\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
and vector parameters from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
37
37

37

Again, we can insert hyperparameters like
\sigma_{W}
and
\sigma_{v}
, but for simplicity we omit them here.

The sum in
Eq.

2.32
sums over all vectors
h,z,g,y
in
\pi
satisfying
h=Wz
and
g=Wy
(potentially
h=g
and
z=y
).

2) Tensor operations.

Here
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{g}\rangle,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle,\bm{\chi}\in\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}
while
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{z}\rangle,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
.
First, we take the convention that each bra has the same shape as the corresponding ket:
38
38

38

Intuitively,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{g}\rangle
corresponds to
\bm{d}\bm{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times e\times\mathcal{M}}
while
\langle\bm{d}\bm{g}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
corresponds to
\bm{d}\bm{g}
’s “transpose” with shape
\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}\times n}
.

\langle\bm{d}\bm{g}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}},\langle\bm{d}\bm{h}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}
.
Second,
the meaning of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}}\langle\bm{z}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
is

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}}\langle\bm{z}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}=\sum_{a\in[\mathcal{M}],i\in[e]}\chi^{ai}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d^{x^{i}}h(\xi^{a})\rangle\langle z(\xi^{a})\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}

(2.35)

(i.e., contraction of all matching indices), which generalizes the case of
e=1
in
2.9.19
.
Likewise, in
Eq.

2.33
,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{v}\rangle\cdot\bm{\chi}\in\mathbb{R}
contracts all indices.
Finally,
\mathrm{Diag}_{\mathcal{M}}
in
Eq.

2.32
takes its argument tensor of shape
e\times\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
and returns a tensor of shape
e\times\mathcal{M}
by “taking the diagonal over the
\mathcal{M}
dimension.”

Theorem 2.9.19

(Neural Tangent Limit for General Architecture)
.

Consider a representable architecture
f
with representation
(\pi,x^{1},\ldots,x^{e})
and any training routine in NTP (
2.9.11
) with memoryless stationary update function
Q
.
Adopt
2.9.17
.
Further assume

1.

f
has no scalar parameters,
and

2.

\langle 1\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{i}(\xi)\rangle=0
for every input
\xi
and output index
i\in[e]
at initialization.
39
39

39

More precisely, the ket
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{i}(\xi)\rangle
is evaluated in
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
(
2.9.16
) via
2.6.5
.

Recall
\bm{f}_{t}
denotes the function after
t
steps of updates from random initialization.
Then

\bm{f}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{N}(0,\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle)

where
\bm{x}

evaluated on all
\mathcal{M}
inputs.
Additionally,
for every

,

converges
almost surely to a random function
\mathring{\bm{f}_{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{f}}_{s+1}-\mathring{\bm{f}}_{s}

\displaystyle=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\varepsilon_{s}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{s})),\quad\text{for all $s$}.

(2.36)

Remark 2.9.20
.

Note Simple GIA Check
[
43
]
may not be satisfied in general architectures, so that we cannot necessarily calculate kets like
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle
by assuming matrix parameters and their transposes are independent (even if no transposes occur in
\pi
), e.g., ignoring
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\dot{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu
in our calculations.
Nevertheless,
2.9.19
still holds if one calculates
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle
correctly using the rules of
2.6.5
.

Remark 2.9.21
.

Assumption
39
(
\bm{x}
being mean zero) in
2.9.19
is obviously necessary for us to arrive at a Gaussian Process limit at initialization.
Assumption
1
is also necessary for two reasons: 1) at initialization, random initialization of scalar parameters would make the initial process a mixture of Gaussian processes (or a GP conditioned on the values of scalar parameters).
Even if the scalar parameters are deterministic, the gradient wrt scalar parameters will also be a random process (possibly correlated to the process of the function) at initialization.
2) Function space picture (c.f.
2.8.23
and
[
45
, Remark 3.11]
) would no longer hold: One cannot track the evolution of the
\mathring{f}_{t}
solely by knowing what it is at time
t=0
. Instead, one would need to track the values of the scalar parameters and their gradients as well.
So in a sense we will have a “function space + scalar parameters” picture.
The complete evolution of
\mathring{f}_{t}
can then be described by a) the joint process of the function output, scalar parameters, and their gradients at initialization, together with b) an evolution equation involving how they evolve given their previous values in time.
This is similar to
[
24
]
.

Memoryful Nonstationary Case

As before, with some more notation we can write down the NT limit for any representable architecture.

Definition 2.9.22

(Neural Tangent Operator, Memoryful Nonstationary Case)
.

For memoryless but nonstationary update functions
\bm{Q}_{t}=\{Q^{\theta}_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}:\text{parameter }\theta\}
,
we define
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}:\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}
with the same equation as
Eqs.

2.32
,
2.33
and
2.34
, except the bar notation abbreviates
Q^{W}_{t}
or
Q^{v}_{t}
.

For general update functions
\bm{Q}_{t}=\{Q^{\theta}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{t+1}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{\theta}
,
we define
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}:\mathbb{R}^{(t+1)\times e\times\mathcal{M}}\to\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}^{W}(\bm{\chi}_{0},\ldots,\bm{\chi}_{t})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\mathrm{Diag}_{\mathcal{M}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h=Wz\\
g=Wy\end{subarray}}\langle{\bm{d}\bm{g}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{\leq t}}\langle\bm{z}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{y}}\rangle

(2.37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}^{v}({\bm{\chi}_{0},\ldots,\bm{\chi}_{t}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\langle{\bm{d}\bm{v}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{v}\rangle\cdot\bm{\chi}_{\leq t}}

(2.38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}(\bm{\chi}_{0},\ldots,\bm{\chi}_{t})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\sum_{W}\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}^{W}(\bm{\chi}_{0},\ldots,\bm{\chi}_{t})+\sum_{v}\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}^{v}(\bm{\chi}_{0},\ldots,\bm{\chi}_{t})

(2.39)

where
\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{\leq t}}\langle\bm{z}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}
is shorthand for
Q^{W}_{t}\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{0}}\langle\bm{z}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}},\ldots,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{t}}\langle\bm{z}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\right)

and
\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{v}\rangle\cdot\bm{\chi}_{\leq t}}
is shorthand for

With this in mind, the following theorem yields the NT limit of Adam (

Adam

) as a corollary.

Theorem 2.9.23

(Neural Tangent Limit, Memoryful Nonstationary Case)
.

If the update functions
\bm{Q}

holds with

\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t+1}-\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}(\varepsilon_{t}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t})),\quad\text{for all $t$}.

(2.40)

For general
\bm{Q}
, not necessarily memoryless,

holds with

\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t+1}-\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}_{t}}(\varepsilon_{0}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{0}),\ldots,\varepsilon_{t}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t})),\quad\text{for all $t$}.

(2.41)

2.9.6
Maximal Update Limit

Given a representable architecture, the representing program describes the symbolic procedure for computing the output of the network given an
assignment of
the input and parameters to concrete values.
This procedure is well defined for any network width (by construction).
Naturally, it remains well defined as explicitly spelled out below even when we “pass to the infinite-width limit.”
In short,
2.9.24
specifies what it means for a program to compute something given
an assignment of
the input and parameters to concrete infinite-width limits (e.g., kets and operators).

Definition 2.9.24
.

For any program
\pi
and a vector
y
in
\pi
,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle
can be thought of as a function, defined via
2.6.5
, of
\{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\}_{W},\{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}v\rangle\}_{v},\{\mathring{c}\}_{c}

range over the initial matrices, vectors, and scalars respectively.

\Theta
that assigns an operator

to each initial matrix

,

to each initial vector

\Theta\{c\}

\Theta\{\bullet\}
is some object in some program and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\Theta\{\bullet\}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
or
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\Theta\{\bullet\}\rangle
are their “limits.”

Memoryless Stationary Case

Theorem 2.9.25

(
\mu
-Limit for General Architecture)
.

Consider a representable architecture
f
with representation
(\pi,x^{1},\ldots,x^{e})
and any training routine in
\mu
P (
2.9.12
) with memoryless stationary update function
Q
.
Adopt
2.9.17
.

Then for each
t\geq 0
,
\bm{f}_{t}
converges almost surely to
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}
computed from the following.

1.

(Forward and Backward Propagation)
Let
\Theta_{t}
be the assignment that assigns
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{t}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
to each matrix parameter
W
,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}v\rangle
to each vector parameter
v
, and
\mathring{c}_{t}
to each scalar parameter
c
.

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{t}\{\langle 1\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle\}\in\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{t}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}

(2.42)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}\bm{y}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Theta_{t}\{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle\}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}\bm{d}\bm{y}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Theta_{t}\{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{y}\rangle\}\in\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}

for any vector

in
\pi

(2.43)

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{a}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{t}\{\mathring{\bm{a}}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\bm{d}\mathring{\bm{a}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{t}\{\bm{d}\mathring{\bm{a}}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{e\times\mathcal{M}}

for any scalar
a
in
\pi

(2.44)

Here
\bm{x}
is the multi-vector consisting of
x^{1},\ldots,x^{e}
evaluated on all
\mathcal{M}
inputs, and all kets and limits are calculated in
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
via
2.6.5
.

2.

(Parameter Updates)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}W_{t+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{t}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}-\eta\overline{\sum_{h=Wx}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}\bm{h}_{t}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}}\langle\bm{x}_{t}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}

for every matrix parameter
W

(2.45)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}v_{t+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}v_{t}\rangle-\eta\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{d}v_{t}\rangle\cdot\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}}

for every vector parameter
v

(2.46)

\displaystyle\mathring{c}_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\mathring{c}_{t}-\eta\overline{\bm{d}\mathring{c}_{t}}

for every scalar parameter
c

(2.47)

where the tensor operations and summation over
h=Wx
should be interpreted as in
2.9.18
.

3.

(Initialization)
\{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}:\text{matrix parameter $W$}\}
is a set of independent initial operators. Additionally,

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}v_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(0,1)

for every vector parameter
v

(2.48)

\displaystyle\mathring{c}_{0}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)

for every scalar parameter
c

(2.49)

all independent from one another.

Here, we used the notation
W,v,c
for matrix, vector, and scalar parameters, in contrast to
y
and
a
for vector and scalar generated by the program
\pi
.
The former are exemplified by weights while the latter by (pre)activations.

Example 2.9.26
.

In the MLP case with program
\pi
given in
Eq.

2.3
, there are no generated scalars, so we can ignore
Eq.

2.44
.
The generated vectors (in
\bm{d}\bm{\pi}
) are
\bm{h}^{1},\ldots,\bm{h}^{L}
and
\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{L}
as well as their error signals
d\bm{h}^{l},d\bm{x}^{l}
.
So
Eq.

2.43
reduces to the 2nd and 3rd rows in
2.7.1
(1).
Finally, the function output is given by averaging
w^{L+1}\odot x^{L}
, so the
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}=\Theta_{t}\{\langle 1\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle\}
in
Eq.

2.42
reduces to

\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}=\langle w_{t}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{L}_{t}\rangle
in
2.7.1
(1).

Remark 2.9.27

(Scalar Parameters)
.

In contrast to the NT limit (
2.9.19
), here we allow scalar parameters.
The sampling of
\mathring{c}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
in
Eq.

2.49
for every scalar parameter
c
is just a consequence of the sampling in
2.9.7
.
But in fact for any deterministic initialization
C\in\mathbb{R}
of
c
, we can set
\mathring{c}_{0}=C
in
Eq.

2.49
, and
2.9.25
still holds.
Indeed, for scalar parameters, the most natural initialization is probably either 0 (for additive parameters like bias) or 1 (for multiplicative parameters like layernorm weights).
When scalar parameters have deterministic initializations,
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}
is always deterministic as well;
however, in
2.9.25
, as written,
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}
is only deterministic conditioned on
\mathring{c}_{0}
for every scalar parameter
c
.

Remark 2.9.28

(
\mu
P is Most Natural)
.

As discussed in
2.7.2
,
\mu
P is in a sense
the most natural parametrization
because its infinite-width limit is just a direct “ket-translation” of the finite-width computations, no matter the architecture.
Compare this with NTP, where the Gaussian process and kernel behaviors can only happen under some relative restrictive conditions, like “no scalar parameters” (
2.9.19
and
2.9.21
).

Remark 2.9.29
.

In the
\mu
-limit for a general architecture,
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{0}
no longer needs to be 0, unlike
2.7.1
.
For example, if in an MLP with relu activation, the output is given by the average entry of the last layer activation vector, then obviously
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{0}
will always be positive.

(

.

If

and

If

2.10
Extensions

2.10.1
Weight Decay

Consider the update equation (
Eq.

2.4
)
with
decoupled
weight decay
\lambda
:
41
41

41

We will assume all layers have the same
\lambda
, for simplicity. The generalization to layer-specific
\lambda
is straightforward.

w^{l}_{t}=(1-\lambda)w^{l}_{t-1}-\eta n^{-c_{l}}Q_{t}^{l}(n^{d_{l}}g_{0},\ldots,n^{d_{l}}g_{t}).

(2.50)

This decoupled weight decay
\lambda
is equivalent to a traditional weight decay value
\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{\eta n^{-c_{l}}}}
.
It’s obvious that
\lambda
should be invariant to the width
n
: if
\lambda\to\infty
with
n
, then
1-\lambda<0
eventually; if
\lambda\to 0
with
n
, then weight decay has no effect in the limit.

The theory in this section covers AdamW
[
27
]
, SGD with weight decay, and so on.

Maximal Update Limit

2.7.1
holds with

,

and

(2.51)

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W^{l}_{t}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}-\eta\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}}\langle\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}},\ \forall l\in[2,L]

Neural Tangent

Fix decoupled weight decay
\lambda
.

Definition 2.10.1
.

For each
l=1,\ldots,L
and
s=0,1,\ldots
, the ket
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}_{s}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
is constructed
as a mean-zero Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
(1-\lambda)^{2s}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}_{s}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l-1}_{s}\rangle

when
l>1
or
\bm{\xi}^{\top}\bm{\xi}
when
l=1
. Simultaneously,
\bm{x}^{l}_{s}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\phi(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}_{s}\rangle)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}

for
l=1,\ldots,L
.
Furthermore, for any
s,t\geq 0
,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}_{s}\rangle
and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}_{t}\rangle
are jointly Gaussian with covariance
(1-\lambda)^{s+t}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}_{s}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l-1}_{t}\rangle
.

Definition 2.10.2
.

For each
l=L,\ldots,1
and
s=0,1,\ldots
, the ket
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}_{s}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
is independent
from
\{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l}_{t}\rangle,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}_{t}\rangle\}_{l=1}^{L}
for any
t
and is a mean-zero
Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
(1-\lambda)^{2s}\langle d\bm{h}^{l+1}_{s}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l+1}_{s}\rangle

when
l<L
or the all-1s matrix when
l=L
. Simultaneously,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}_{s}\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\phi^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}_{s}\rangle)\odot\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}_{s}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}

for all
l
.
Furthermore, for any
s,t\geq 0
,
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}_{s}\rangle
and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}_{t}\rangle
are jointly Gaussian with covariance
(1-\lambda)^{s+t}\langle d\bm{h}^{l+1}_{s}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l+1}_{t}\rangle
.

Theorem 2.10.3
.

With decoupled weight decay
\lambda
,

2.4.7
holds with the update equation
Eq.

2.9
replaced by

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t+1}-\mathring{\bm{f}}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\eta\sum_{s=0}^{t}(1-\lambda)^{t-s}\mathrm{Diag}\sum_{l=1}^{L+1}\langle{d\bm{h}^{l}_{t+1}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}_{s}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{s}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}_{s}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}^{l-1}_{t+1}}\rangle.

For memoryless nonstationary updates, interpret the bar as
Q_{t}^{l}
.
For memoryful nonstationary updates, replace the nonlinear outer product with

\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}_{\leq s}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{\leq s}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}_{\leq s}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}
(c.f.
2.7.3
for the notation).

Note that when
\lambda=0
,
\bm{h}^{l}_{s}
is invariant to
s
as are
\bm{x}^{l}_{s},d\bm{h}^{l}_{s},d\bm{x}^{l}_{s}
, and this exponentially weighted sum just reduces to a simple sum and thus to

Eq.

2.9
.

In the memoryful case, interpreting the bar in

as
Eq.

Adam

yields the NT limit of AdamW.
The limit theorems (

and

) for general architecture also hold with analogous modifications.

2.10.2
Update Clipping and Normalization

We now add update clipping or normalization to
Eq.

2.50
:
42
42

42

Traditionally,
gradient clipping
for SGD clips the norm of the entire gradient (for the whole network). Naively, one would just do this before applying the update function
Q
. However, in Adam, for example, this would be meaningless because of Adam’s normalization. For general
Q
, the
d_{l}
terms can be trivially adjusted according to how the global gradient norm scales. So this notion of clipping or normalization (before
Q
) is not very interesting.

w^{l}_{t}=(1-\lambda)w^{l}_{t-1}-\eta n^{-c_{l}}\cdot\nu^{-1}Q_{t}^{l}(n^{d_{l}}g_{0},\ldots,n^{d_{l}}g_{t}),

(2.54)

where

•

if we are doing update normalization, then we set
\nu\leftarrow\|Q_{t}^{l}(n^{d_{l}}g_{0},\ldots,n^{d_{l}}g_{t})\|
;

•

if we are doing update clipping, then calculate additionally
\nu\leftarrow\min(\nu,\theta^{l})
where
\theta^{l}
is a threshold hyperparameter for layer
l
.

Some recent works
[
49
,
35
,
50
,
6
,
5
,
26
]
normalize the update by the current weight (Frobenius) norm instead of by the update norm.
We explore its implications in
Section

2.10.2
.
For now we focus on the formulation above.

The key questions we explore here are:

1.

How should the abcd values adjust to update clipping and normalization?

2.

What do the neural tangent and maximal update limits look like?

3.

How should the threshold hyperparameter
\theta^{l}
scale with width
n
?

The key intuition is as follows:
In all “reasonable” parametrizations (more precisely, faithful ones (
2.8.8
)),
Q_{t}^{l}(n^{d_{l}}g_{0},\ldots,n^{d_{l}}g_{t})
is entrywise
\Theta(1)
.
Thus, its norm
\nu
scales like
\sqrt{\#\text{entries}}
, as can be verified via the Master Theorem (
2.6.10
).
For example, for the input and output weights of the MLP (
Eq.

2.3
), this is
\sqrt{n}
, while for the hidden weights, this is
n
.
Therefore, for update clipping, the threshold should be

\theta^{l}=\theta^{l}_{0}n^{e_{l}}\quad\text{where}\quad e_{l}=\begin{cases}\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}&\text{if $l=1,L+1$}\\
1&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(2.55)

for some tunable hyperparameter
\theta^{l}_{0}
independent of width.
Otherwise, either the clipping has no effect (if
\theta^{l}
is larger than this) or
\nu
after update clipping is always equal to the threshold
\theta^{l}
(if
\theta^{l}
is smaller than this).

At the same time,
c_{l}
should be
{e_{l}}
smaller than if there is no update normalization or clipping (i.e., the learning rate should be larger).
Thus, for example,

Definition 2.10.4
.

The NTP with update normalization or clipping is

l

[2,L]

1

L+1

a_{l}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

0

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

b_{l}

0

0

0

c_{l}

0

0

0

d_{l}

1

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

where update clipping thresholds
\theta^{l}
scale as in
Eq.

2.55
.

Definition 2.10.5
.

The
\mu
P with update normalization or clipping is

l

[2,L]

1

L+1

a_{l}

0

0

1

b_{l}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

0

0

c_{l}

0

-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

d_{l}

1

1

1

where update clipping thresholds
\theta^{l}
scale as in
Eq.

2.55
.

Likewise, the classification of parametrizations in
Section

2.8
hold for update normalization or clipping if we replace all mentions of
c_{l}
in
Section

2.8
with
c_{l}+e_{l}
.
For example,

Definition 2.10.6
.

With update clipping or normalization, we redefine

r_{l}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\begin{cases}c_{l}+e_{l}+a_{l}-1&\text{if $l>1$}\\
c_{l}+e_{l}+a_{l}&\text{if $l=1$}\end{cases},\quad r_{\leq l}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\min_{m=1}^{l}r_{l},\quad r\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}r_{\leq L}

Then
\bm{x}^{L}_{t}-\bm{x}^{L}_{0}=\Theta(n^{-r})
still and it remains that
r=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
for NTP (
2.10.4
) and
r=0
for
\mu
P (
2.10.5
) with the new definitions above.

General Architectures

For general architectures, there is one
e_{w}
for every parameter tensor
w
, and should be set to
0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},
and
1
respectively for scalar, vector, and matrix parameters.
Then the obvious generalization of the above discussion holds.

Maximal Update Limit

Definition 2.10.7
.

For any random vector
\bm{X}
, let
|\bm{X}|\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\sqrt{\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\|\bm{X}\|^{2}}
.
Then let
\tilde{\bm{X}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{X}/|\bm{X}|
.

Then
2.7.1
holds with
Eqs.

2.20
,
2.21
and
2.22
replaced with (essentially, compared to

,
2.52
and

we added a tilde
\tilde{\ }
to all bars.)
43
43

43

Unfortunately, LaTeX breaks
\mathring{\chi}_{t}
in the subscripts, so here we just write

instead.

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}w^{1}_{t+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}w^{1}_{t}\rangle-\eta\tilde{\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}_{t}^{1}\rangle_{{\bm{\chi}}_{t}}\bm{\xi}^{\top}}}

(2.56)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}W^{l}_{t+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W^{l}_{t}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}-\eta\tilde{\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle{}_{\bm{\chi}_{t}}\langle\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}},\ \forall l\in[2,L]

(2.57)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}w_{t+1}^{L+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}w_{t}^{L+1}\rangle-\eta\tilde{\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}_{t}^{L}\rangle\cdot{\bm{\chi}}_{t}}}.

(2.58)

Here, we think of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}}\langle\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}
as a scalar random variable with the same distribution as
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{M}}\mathring{\chi}_{t}(\xi_{i})\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}h^{l}_{t}(\xi_{i})\rangle\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{l-1}_{t}(\xi_{i})\rangle^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}}\in\mathbb{R}
.

If doing update clipping, then interpret
\tilde{\bm{X}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{X}/\min(\theta_{0},|\bm{X}|)
for
\theta_{0}
being the
\theta^{l}_{0}
(
