2.55
) for appropriate layer
l
.

Similar statements hold for other
\mu
-limit theorems, including those in general architectures.

Neural Tangent Limit

2.10.3
holds with a tilde added to all bars, where the tilde should be interpreted according to
\tilde{\bm{X}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{X}/|\bm{X}|
if doing update normalization or
\tilde{\bm{X}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{X}/\min(\theta_{0},|\bm{X}|)
if doing update clipping.
Similar statements hold for other NT limit theorems, including those in general architectures.

Normalization by Current Weight Norm

Now suppose in
Eq.

2.54
, we normalizd by the current weight norm instead, i.e.,

\nu\leftarrow\|w^{l}_{t-1}\|_{F}.

For simpicity, assume the weight decay
\lambda
is zero; our conclusion will turn out to hold even when this is not the case.

Let’s consider what happens to the hidden weights.
Consider
W^{l}
for
l\in[2,L]
. The Frobenius norm of its initialization
W^{l}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
(a random Gaussian matrix with
W_{\alpha\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
) scales like
\Theta(\sqrt{n})
(in fact, asymptotically
\sqrt{n}+O(1)
).
But, in a stable parametrization (
2.8.7
), the Frobenius norm of
\delta W^{l}_{t}=W^{l}_{t}-W^{l}_{t-1}
, a nonlinear outer product with
O(1/n)
entries, scales like
O(1)
.
This means that

\frac{\|\delta W^{l}_{t}\|_{F}}{\|W^{l}_{0}\|_{F}}=\frac{\|\delta w^{l}_{t}\|_{F}}{\|w^{l}_{0}\|_{F}}=O(1/\sqrt{n}).

Therefore,

\frac{\|W^{l}_{0}+\delta W^{l}_{1}+\cdots+\delta W^{l}_{t}\|_{F}}{\|W^{l}_{0}\|_{F}}=\frac{\|W^{l}_{t}\|_{F}}{\|W^{l}_{0}\|_{F}}=\frac{\|w^{l}_{t}\|_{F}}{\|w^{l}_{0}\|_{F}}\to 1

for any
t
as
n\to\infty
.
44
44

44

The fact that
\|w_{t}\|_{F}
is essentially
\|w_{0}\|_{F}
really implies that this is
not the right quantity
to normalize or clip the update by.
For example, other norms (like spectral norm) does not have this property and could be better suited (if we ignore the computational issues for the moment).
As another example, if Frobenius norm is desired, we should perhaps want to normalize by
\|\Delta w_{t}\|_{F}
instead of
\|w_{t}\|_{F}
.

So
\nu=\|w^{l}_{t-1}\|_{F}=\Theta(\|w^{l}_{0}\|_{F})=\Theta(n^{1-b_{l}})
(in fact,
\nu/n^{-b_{l}}\to 1
).

In contrast, for the input layer weight
W=W^{1}
, we have
\Delta W_{t}=O(W_{0})
(entrywise and in Frobenius norm) in a stable parametrization.
If the parametrization is furthermore faithful (
2.8.8
), then for
W=W^{L+1}
, this is true as well (c.f.
2.8.13
).
So
\|w^{1}_{t}\|_{F}/\|w^{1}_{0}\|_{F}
and
\|w^{L+1}_{t}\|_{F}/\|w^{L+1}_{0}\|_{F}
are both
\Theta(1)
(but
\not\to 1
generally) and
\nu=\Theta(n^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-b_{l}})
in both cases.

Therefore, if we subtract
b_{l}
from
e_{l}
in
Eq.

2.55
, then all discussion above applies.
For example, the parametrizations for current weight norm can be obtained by adding the
b_{l}
row to the
c_{l}
row.

Definition 2.10.8
.

The NTP with update normalization or clipping with current weight norm is the same as
2.10.4
.

Definition 2.10.9
.

The
\mu
P with update normalization or clipping with current weight norm is

l

[2,L]

1

L+1

a_{l}

0

0

1

b_{l}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

0

0

c_{l}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}

d_{l}

1

1

1

where the clipping thresholds
\theta^{l}
scale as
n^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
.

The limit equations for
\mu
P in this case is just
Eqs.

2.56
,
2.57
and
2.58
but with the tilde interpreted as dividing by
\sqrt{\langle w^{1}_{t}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}w^{1}_{t}\rangle}
,
1
, and
\sqrt{\langle w^{L+1}_{t}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}w^{L+1}_{t}\rangle}
respectively.
Similar modifications apply to the NT limits.

Likewise, the classification of parametrizations hold if we replace each
c_{l}
with
c_{l}+e_{l}-b_{l}
.

We have assumed that the weight decay
\lambda
is 0 at the beginning.
In the general case, define
\delta W_{t}=W_{t}-(1-\lambda)W_{t-1}
instead, but the same calculations will yield the same conclusions.

2.10.3
Second Moment Factoring ala Adafactor

The key observation here is that, even though the factored update function
Q
is not entrywise anymore, the update itself is still a nonlinear outer product of
d\bm{h}_{\leq t}
and
d\bm{x}_{\leq t}
(with some scalars variables inserted in an appropriate
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program), All of our theory in fact holds as-is for this more general kind of update function, since they all factor through the
OuterNonlin
instruction of
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
.

2.10.4
Future Optimizers

As technology advances, we may get better optimizers of very different forms than we discussed here.
But we can quite reasonably expect them to continue to efficiently utilize GPUs, i.e., perform large matrix-multiplies.
As such, these optimizers can always be analyzed in a Tensor Program.
So the lesson is: as long as one understands Tensor Programs, one can always derive the correct way of scaling an optimizer (with width).

2.11
Proof Sketch

\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
programs equipped with their Master Theorem (
2.6.10
) provide the main tool to all of our rigorous results.
For example, to prove the neural tangent or
\mu
limit equations, one can:
express the optimization dynamics using a
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program, mechanically compute the kets according to
2.6.5
, and apply
2.6.10
to compute the limit (see full proofs in
Sections

3.2
and
3.3
).
What remains is to prove
2.6.10
using a strategy which we now outline.

In a program, all vectors can be collected into an
n\times M
matrix
V
where
n
is the dimension of each vector, and
M
is the total number of vectors.
The Master Theorem can be interpreted as saying that each row of
V
(i.e., the slice for each
\alpha\in[n]
) is roughly an iid sample from some distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathbb{R}^{M}
(the distribution of some multivariate ket computed from
2.6.5
).
Specifically,
2.6.10
and all previous versions of the Master Theorem formalize this by saying: this matrix
V
of vectors looks similar to a matrix
V^{\prime}
of iid samples from
\mathcal{D}
, as measured by applying arbitrary pseudo-Lipschitz “test function
\psi
” to both sides and taking averages (the scalars in
2.6.10
are exactly of this form by
Avg
).

Core Insight

Our core insight here is that
V
is in fact similar to
V^{\prime}
in a stronger sense without needing to refer to any test function
\psi
: There is a “small” matrix
\delta V
of the same shape as
V
such that
V-\delta V
is distributed exactly the same as
V^{\prime}
.
In general, if this happens, then we say
V
is
distributionally equivalent, or dequivalent, to

V^{\prime}
(
4.3.15
).
The definition of “small” roughly means that
\delta V
is
\tilde{O}(1)
in
L^{2}
norm, so that dequivalence is morally just “close in Wasserstein distance.”
This “Wasserstein” notion is a stronger sense of closeness of
V
to
V^{\prime}
than the “test function” notion described in the previous paragraph because the test function
\psi
is (by assumption) smooth enough that
\delta V
contributes a vanishing amount to the average (
4.3.11
).

To prove this core insight, there are two parts.

Part 1:

We show that, in any
\textsc{Netsor}\top
program (i.e., a program with no scalar variables and no
Tensor
operation),
V
is dequivalent to
V^{\prime}
.
This can be done by re-analyzing the proof of the
\textsc{Netsor}\top
Master Theorem in
[
44
]
in a fairly straightforward way.

Part 2:

For any
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program
\pi
(the subject of our work here), we construct a
parallel
\textsc{Netsor}\top
program
(
4.8.1
) and show, by induction, that the vectors of the two programs are dequivalent (i.e., distributed exactly the same after subtracting “small” vectors).
This parallel program essentially replaces 1) all scalar variables in the original program by their deterministic limits, as computed in
2.6.5
(
Avg
), and 2) all
OuterNonlin
operations by
Nonlin
operations, as computed in
2.6.5
(
OuterNonlin
), so that the corresponding vectors of these programs share their kets.
Then, by Part 1 above, we will have proven
V
dequivalent to
V^{\prime}
for the original
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program
\pi
.

In this induction, we need to prove and use a lemma that, in the simplest case as an illustration, says the following:
For any pseudo-Lipschitz function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
and random vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with iid standard Gaussian entries, the following two tensors
T
and
T^{\prime}
are equivalent:
1) the tensor
T
with entries
T_{\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{k}}=\psi(x_{\beta_{1}},\ldots,x_{\beta_{k}})
, and
2) the tensor
T^{\prime}
with entries
T^{\prime}_{\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{k}}=\psi(x^{1}_{\beta_{1}},\ldots,x^{k}_{\beta_{k}})

where
x^{1},\ldots,x^{k}
are iid copies of
x
.
The proof of this lemma interestingly requires Baranyai’s theorem (
4.1.5
), a classical theorem from the theory of combinatorial design about perfect matching in complete hypergraphs.

Chapter 3
Proofs of Infinite-Width Limits

Here we prove the classificatioon of abcd-parametrizations as well as the NT and
\mu
-limit equations, assuming the
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
Master Theorem (
2.6.10
).

3.1
Proof of Classification of abcd-Parametrizations

Here we seek to prove the main claims of
Section

2.8
:
2.8.19
,
2.8.15
, and
2.8.12
.

Overview

The proof is a straightforward modification of the proof of
[
45
, H.13]
.
The main subtle point is how
r=0
implies
Item

1
of
2.8.19
(
Item

4(a)iii
in the proof outline below).
The reasoning for this in
[
45
]
is very specific to SGD and the activation functions tanh and (smoothed) relu.
Here, we adopt a different logic, given in
Section

3.1.4
and
3.1.3
, based on the “asymptotic relu-ness” of the nonlinearity.

On the other hand, the implication of
Item

2
in
2.8.19
by
r>0
is similar to
[
45
]
, adapted naturally to general
Q^{l}_{t}
functions, so we omit the full proof here.
However, we provide the derivation of the NT limit in
Section

2.4
, which gives an instructive example of the proof for general operator regime parametrizations.

Proof Outline

In the sequel, we provide full details of
Items

4(a)i
and
4(a)iii
of the outline below.
All other steps in this outline are straightforward adaptations of
[
45
]
or easy in a self-contained way.

1.

The characterization of stability at initialization (
Lemma

2.8.10
) is already proven in
[
45
, Thm H.19]
. So assume stability at initialization from here on.

2.

Some simple calculations then verifies the characterization of faithfulness at initialization (
Lemma

2.8.11
). So assume faithfulness at initialization from here on.
1
1

1

Note this effectively fixes
d_{l}
given
a_{l},b_{l}
, so that the abcd-parametrization now has the same degrees of freedom as an abc-parametrization. This is a sanity check for why we can adapt most of the arguments from
[
45
]
.

3.

If
b_{L+1}>c_{L+1}
, then it’s easy to see that we lose faithfulness after 1 step of update (because the input to
Q^{l}_{t}
is
\omega(1)
).

4.

Otherwise
b_{L+1}\leq c_{L+1}
.

(a)

First, assume that
r_{l}\geq 0
for all
l\in[L+1]
and
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}+r\geq 1
. (i.e., we assume
Eq.

2.27
)

i.

Then we can build a
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program computing the network evolution (which we do in
Sections

3.1.1
and
3.1.2
). The Master Theorem yields its infinite-width limit, described in
Section

3.1.3
, which will be obviously stable and faithful (
Lemma

3.1.2
).

ii.

If
r>0
, then, by adapting the corresponding reasoning in
[
45
]
, it’s easy to derive all properties in
2.8.19
(2), as well as the validity of
2.8.15
(assuming
r>0
).
We derive the NT limit in
Section

3.3
as an example.

iii.

If
r=0
, then the reasoning in
[
45
]
cannot be straightforwardly adapted. In
Section

3.1.4
, using a different method based on “asymptotic relu-ness” of the nonlinearity
\phi
(
2.8.2
), we prove all properties in
2.8.19
(1,3,4), the validity of
2.8.15
(assuming
r=0
), and the fact that we are not in operator regime.

(b)

Suppose
r_{l}<0
for some
l\in[L]
, and
l^{*}
is the smallest such
l
. Then the infinite-width limit derived above up to time 1, layer
l^{*}
shows that
\Delta\bm{x}^{l}_{1}=\omega(1)
for some choice of
\bm{\xi}
and training routine, so we lose stability.

(c)

Otherwise, if
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}+r<1
or
r_{L+1}<0
, then the infinite-width limit derived above up to time 1 shows that
\Delta\bm{f}_{1}=\omega(1)
, so we lose stability as well.

(d)

Combining all caseworks, we 1) derive the nontriviality condition
2.8.15
; 2) see
Eq.

2.27
is necessary for faithfulness and stability as well, proving
2.8.12
; and 3) prove
2.8.19
.

3.1.1
Program Setup

In
Sections

3.1.1
,
3.1.2
and
3.1.3
, we implement
Item

4(a)i
: we construct the Tensor Program that encodes the training of an
L
-hidden layer MLP under an abcd-parametrization satisfying
Eqs.

2.25
,
2.26
and
2.27
and take its infinite-width limit.
For the most part, they are straightforward adaptations of
[
45
, Sec. H.3-H.5]
; nevertheless, we show full details to demonstrate the usage of the new
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
language and its Master Theorem (
2.6.10
).

In this section, we first describe the initial matrices, vectors, and scalars of the program, along with necessary notations.

For ease of presentation, we assume the input dimension
d=1
.
The general
d
case is a trivial adaptation.

Initial Matrices, Vectors, Scalars

1.

Initial matrices:
W_{0}^{2},\ldots,W_{0}^{L}
, sampled like
(W_{0}^{l})_{\alpha\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
.

2.

Initial vectors: input layer matrix
W_{0}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1}
and
normalized
output layer matrix
\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}W_{0}^{L+1}n^{a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1}
, sampled like
(W_{0}^{1})_{\alpha},(\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1})_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

3.

Initial scalars: We define the following scalars (where we explain the intuition in parenthesis). The reader can skip this part on a first read but come back when referred to.

(a)

(
n
times the scale of coordinates of
\Delta W_{t}^{l}
) For
l\geq 2
, define

\theta_{W^{l}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}n^{-r_{l}}=n^{1-c_{l}-a_{l}}

(b)

(scale of coordinates of
\Delta W_{t}^{1}
and
\Delta h_{t}^{1}
) Define

\theta_{1}=\theta_{W^{1}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}n^{-r_{1}}=n^{-c_{1}-a_{1}}

(c)

(scale of coordinates of
\Delta W_{t}^{L+1}
)

\theta_{L+1}=\theta_{W^{L+1}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}n^{-a_{L+1}-c_{L+1}}

(d)

(scale of
\Delta h_{t}^{l}
and
\Delta x_{t}^{l}
) For
l\in[L]
, define

\displaystyle\theta_{h^{l}}=\theta_{x^{l}}=\theta_{l}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\max_{m\leq l}\theta_{W^{m}}=\max(\theta_{W^{l}},\theta_{l-1})=n^{-r_{\leq l}}

(3.1)

Note that
\theta_{L}=n^{-r}
with
r
defined in
2.8.5
.

(e)

(scale of
W_{t}^{L+1}
)

\theta_{f}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}n^{-(a_{L+1}+b_{L+1})}

(3.2)

(f)

(convenience scalars)

\displaystyle\theta_{x^{l-1}/h^{l}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{x^{l-1}}/\theta_{h^{l}}=n^{r_{\leq l}-r_{\leq l-1}}

\displaystyle\theta_{W^{l}/h^{l}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{W^{l}}/\theta_{h^{l}}=n^{r_{\leq l}-r_{l}}

\displaystyle\theta_{W^{l}x^{l-1}/h^{l}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{W^{l}}\theta_{x^{l-1}}/\theta_{h^{l}}=n^{r_{\leq l}-r_{l}-r_{\leq l-1}}

\displaystyle\theta_{L+1/f}

\displaystyle=\theta_{L+1}/\theta_{f}=n^{b_{L+1}-c_{L+1}}

\displaystyle\theta_{L+1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=n\theta_{L+1}=n^{1-a_{L+1}-c_{L+1}}

\displaystyle\theta_{Lf}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=n\theta_{L}\theta_{f}=n^{1-(r+a_{L+1}+b_{L+1})}

(g)

Depending on the the value of
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}
, we will also construct the values of
f
at initialization as initial scalars. See
Section

3.1.2
for an explanation.

Eq.

2.27
implies all of these
\theta
s either converge to 0 or stay constant at 1. This means that, assuming appropriate regularity conditions on the nonlinearities and rank stability, we can apply the Master Theorem (if
\theta
blows up to
\infty
then we can’t do that).

Notations

We use
:=
to more clearly denote assignment happening in the program, as opposed to mathematical equality. To clearly demonstrate the application of
OuterNonlin
, we will rewrite expressions in the form

y=\hyperref@@ii[eqn:outernonlin_order2]{\texttt{Nonlin}^{2}}{}(\bm{x};\bm{z};\bm{c})

for vector
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, multi-vectors
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times j}
, and multi-scalar
\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}

if there exists a function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{k+j+l}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

y_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}\psi(\bm{x}_{\alpha};\bm{z}_{\beta};\bm{c}).

(3.3)

This is the order-2 form of
Eq.

2.17
.
We also write

y=\hyperref@@ii[eqn:outernonlin_order1]{\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}}{}(\bm{x};\bm{c})

if there exists a function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{k+l}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

y_{\alpha}=\psi(\bm{x}_{\alpha};\bm{c}).

(3.4)

This is the order-1 form of
Eq.

2.17
.
All usages of
OuterNonlin
in the program below will be through
\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}
(majority of cases) and
\texttt{Nonlin}^{2}
(only when weight updates are involved).
This program will not use
OuterNonlin
order higher than 2.

Preview of Names for Vectors

In the program, for each
\bm{z}\in\{\bm{x}^{l},\bm{h}^{l}\}_{l}
, we will construct vectors
\delta\bm{z}_{t}
to mathematically represent
\theta_{z}^{-1}(\bm{z}_{t}-\bm{z}_{t-1})
(intuition: change in
z
scaled to have
\Theta(1)
coordinates). Similarly, for
w\in\{W^{L+1},W^{1}\}
, we will construct
\delta w_{t}
to mathematically represent
\theta_{w}^{-1}(w_{t}-w_{t-1})
(intuition: change in
w
scaled to have
\Theta(1)
coordinates). Then, mathematically,
\bm{z}_{t}=\bm{z}_{t-1}+\theta_{z}\delta\bm{z}_{t},w_{t}=w_{t-1}+\theta_{w}\delta w_{t}
.

We will also construct
d\bm{z}
to mathematically represent
\theta_{f}^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{z}}\bm{f}
(intuition: gradient
\nabla_{\bm{z}}\bm{f}
scaled to have
\Theta(1)
coordinates). For weight changes, we have the following identity

\displaystyle W_{t}^{l}-W_{t-1}^{l}

\displaystyle=-\eta\theta_{W^{l}}\frac{1}{n}Q^{l}_{t}(n^{d_{l}-a_{l}}\bm{\chi}_{t-1}\bm{x}_{t-1}^{l-1\top})

\displaystyle=-\eta\theta_{W^{l}}\frac{1}{n}Q^{l}_{t}(\bm{\chi}_{t-1}\bm{x}_{t-1}^{l-1\top})

(3.5)

for
l=L+1
,

\displaystyle W_{t}^{l}-W_{t-1}^{l}

\displaystyle=-\eta\theta_{W^{l}}\frac{1}{n}Q^{l}_{t}(n^{d_{l}-a_{l}}\theta_{f}d\bm{h}_{t-1}^{l}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{t-1})\bm{x}_{t-1}^{l-1\top})

\displaystyle=-\eta\theta_{W^{l}}\frac{1}{n}Q^{l}_{t}(d\bm{h}_{t-1}^{l}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{t-1})\bm{x}_{t-1}^{l-1\top})

(3.6)

for
l\in[2,L]
, and
for
l=1
,

\displaystyle W_{t}^{l}-W_{t-1}^{l}

\displaystyle=-\eta\theta_{W^{l}}Q^{l}_{t}(n^{d_{l}-a_{l}}\theta_{f}d\bm{h}_{t-1}^{l}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{t-1})\bm{\xi}_{t-1}^{\top})

\displaystyle=-\eta\theta_{W^{l}}Q^{l}_{t}(d\bm{h}_{t-1}^{l}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{t-1})\bm{\xi}_{t-1}^{\top}).

(3.7)

Here, the 2nd equality of each block is due to our assumption of
Eqs.

2.25
,
2.26
and
2.27
(see discussion at the start of this section), which means
n^{d_{l}-a_{l}}\theta_{f}=1
for all
l\leq L
above and
n^{d_{L+1}-a_{L+1}}=1
.

3.1.2
Program Construction

Here we construct the
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
Program encoding the training of an MLP. We separately describe the first forward and backward passes followed by the later forward and backward passes.

First Forward Pass

We compute
\bm{h}^{1}_{0}:=W^{1}_{0}\bm{\xi}
via
\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}
and then construct the following multi-vectors via
\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}
and
MatMul
respectively:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{0}^{l}:=\phi(\bm{h}_{0}^{l})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\mathcal{M}},\quad\bm{h}_{0}^{l+1}:=W_{0}^{l+1}\bm{x}_{0}^{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\mathcal{M}},\quad\text{for $l=1,\ldots,L-1$,}

(3.8)

Function Output

The first output is
\bm{f}_{0}=W_{0}^{L+1\top}\bm{x}_{0}^{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
, but we will define
\bm{f}_{0}
in the program slightly differently.

Case when
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}>1/2

Then
\bm{f}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0
. In the program, we will construct
\bm{f}_{0}
as an
initial (multi-)scalar
mathematically defined by
W_{0}^{L+1\top}\bm{x}_{0}^{L}
.
2
2

2

We cannot define it using
Avg
+
OuterNonlin
because, intuitively, the mechanism of this convergence is through CLT, not Law of Large Numbers.

Case when
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}=1/2

If
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}=1/2
, then
\bm{f}_{0}
converges to a nontrival
Gaussian via CLT
[
41
]
, so we will condition on
\bm{f}_{0}
:

Given values
\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}}
, we will condition on the event
\mathcal{E}
that
\bm{f}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1\top}\bm{x}_{0}^{L}

equals
\bm{c}
.

(3.9)

The distribution of
\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}
conditioned
on
\mathcal{E}
is given by

\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}_{\mathcal{E}}\sqrt{n}\bm{x}^{+\top}\bm{c}+\Pi\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}

where
\bm{x}
is shorthand for
\bm{x}_{0}^{L}
,
\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}

is an iid copy of
\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}
, and
\Pi
is the orthogonal
projection into the orthogonal complement of the column space of
\bm{x}

(and
\bullet^{+\top}
denotes the pseudo-inverse transpose as usual).

By standard formulas for pseudo-inverse and orthogonal projection,
we can write

\bm{x}^{+\top}=\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}\bm{\Sigma}^{+},\quad\Pi=I-\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{x}^{\top},\quad\text{where}\ \ \bm{\Sigma}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}/n\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}.

If we further define
\bm{\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{x}^{\top}\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}/n
,
then

\Pi\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}=\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}-\bm{x}\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{\gamma}\quad\text{and}\quad\sqrt{n}\bm{x}^{+\top}\bm{c}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bm{x}\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{c}.

By the Master Theorem,
\bm{\gamma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0
because
\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}

is independent from
\bm{x}
, and
\bm{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}\mathring{\bm{\Sigma}}

for some PSD matrix
\mathring{\bm{\Sigma}}
. At this point in the program,
all (multi-)scalars we used (like
\bm{c}
) are constant with
n
and
can be absorbed into nonlinearities. By the rank stability property
of any program without scalars
[
44
]
, the rank of
\bm{\Sigma}

is fixed for large enough
n
, almost surely, so
\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}\mathring{\bm{\Sigma}}^{+}

by the continuity of pseudo-inverse on fixed-rank matrices.
Therefore, we have

\frac{\bm{c}}{\sqrt{n}},\bm{\gamma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0\implies\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\frac{\bm{c}}{\sqrt{n}},\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{\gamma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0.

Thus, the mathematical conditioning done in
Eq.

3.9
is achieved programmatically as follows:

1.

We introduce
\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}
as an initial vector and
\bm{c}
(the value of
\bm{f}_{0}
) as initial scalars

2.

We introduce
\bm{\Sigma}
as a multi-scalar via
\bm{\Sigma}:=\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}/n
(
\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}
followed by
Avg
)

3.

We introduce
\bm{\gamma}
as a multi-scalar via
\bm{\gamma}:=\bm{x}^{\top}\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}/n
(
\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}
followed by
Avg
)

4.

We replace
\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}
(an initial vector)
in the program with (the non-initial vector)

\widehat{W}_{\mathcal{E}}^{L+1}:=\bm{x}\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\frac{\bm{c}}{\sqrt{n}}+\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}-\bm{x}\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{\gamma}

constructed using
\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}
(\bm{x},\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1};\bm{\Sigma},\bm{\gamma},\bm{c},\theta_{f}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}})
.
3
3

3

recall
\theta_{f}
from
Eq.

3.2
.

Since
\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\frac{\bm{c}}{\sqrt{n}},\bm{\Sigma}^{+}\bm{\gamma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0
as above,
we have
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\widehat{W}_{\mathcal{E}}^{L+1}\rangle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}\rangle
.
Intuitively, this means that, even after conditioning on
\bm{f}_{0}=\bm{c}
,
the conditional distribution of
\widetilde{W}_{0}^{L+1}
is practically
the same as the original distribution. We can then proceed exactly
as in the case when
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}>1/2
, with
\widehat{W}_{\mathcal{E}}^{L+1}

taking the role of
\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}
. The program then encodes
the evolution of
\bm{f}

conditioned on

\bm{f}_{0}=\bm{c}
.
4
4

4

Formally, we can also have
\bm{c}
as initial scalars, but since they
are fixed with
n
, they can be absorbed into the Nonlin that defines

\widehat{W}_{\mathcal{E}}^{L+1}
.

Assumption 3.1.1
.

For the above reason, we will assume
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}>1/2
, and remark whenever the case
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}=1/2
involves subtleties.

First Backward Pass

Next, we write the backward pass

\displaystyle d\bm{x}_{0}^{L}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathcal{M}}

\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}

\displaystyle d\bm{h}_{0}^{l}

\displaystyle:=d\bm{x}_{0}^{l}\odot\phi^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{0}^{l})

\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}

\displaystyle d\bm{x}_{0}^{l-1}

\displaystyle:=W_{0}^{l\top}d\bm{h}_{0}^{l}

where
\bm{1}_{\mathcal{M}}
is the
\mathcal{M}
-dimensional vector of all 1s, recall,
d\bm{z}
mathematically equals
\theta_{f}^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{z}}f
.

The error signal at the output is expressed using
\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}
followed by
Avg
as in
Lemma

2.6.2
.
5
5

5

Here,
\mathring{\bm{f}}_{0}=0
if
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}>1/2
; otherwise,

\bm{f}_{0}=\mathring{\bm{f}}_{0}
is the
\bm{c}
we conditioned on in
Eq.

3.9
.

\bm{\chi}_{0}:=\varepsilon_{0}(\bm{f}_{0}).

Mirroring
Eq.

3.5
, we also define

\displaystyle\delta W_{1}^{L+1}

\displaystyle:=-\eta Q_{0}^{L+1}(\bm{x}_{0}^{L}\bm{\chi}_{0})

\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}

to represent the (normalized) change in
W^{L+1}
due to the first
gradient step.

t
th Forward Pass,
t\geq 1

Overview

We iteratively define
\delta\bm{z}_{t}
to mathematically represent
\theta_{z}^{-1}(\bm{z}_{t}-\bm{z}_{t-1})
,
for
z\in\{x^{l},h^{l}\}_{l}
. Then we eventually set

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\bm{z}_{0}+\theta_{z}\delta\bm{z}_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{z}\delta\bm{z}_{t}

\displaystyle=\hyperref@@ii[eqn:outernonlin_order1]{\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}}{}(\bm{z}_{0},\delta\bm{z}_{1},\ldots,\delta\bm{z}_{t};\theta_{z})

Likewise, we will define
\delta W_{t}^{L+1}
so that
W_{t}^{L+1}=\theta_{f}\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}+\theta_{L+1}(\delta W_{1}^{L+1}+\cdots+\delta W_{t}^{L+1})
.
In the program, we will not directly use
W_{t}^{L+1}
but instead
use its normalized version

\displaystyle\widehat{W}_{t}^{L+1}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}+\theta_{L+1/f}(\delta W_{1}^{L+1}+\cdots+\delta W_{t}^{L+1})

(3.10)

\displaystyle=\hyperref@@ii[eqn:outernonlin_order1]{\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}}{}(\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1},\delta W_{1}^{L+1},\ldots,\delta W_{t}^{L+1};\theta_{L+1/f})

(3.11)

where
\theta_{L+1/f}=\theta_{L+1}/\theta_{f}
. Mathematically,
\widehat{W}_{t}^{L+1}=\theta_{f}^{-1}W_{t}^{L+1}
.

The Construction of (Pre)Activations

We start with
h=h^{1}
: By
Eq.

3.7
, we have

\delta\bm{h}_{t}:=-\eta Q_{t}^{1}(d\bm{h}_{t-1}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{t-1})\bm{\xi}^{\top})\bm{\xi}=\hyperref@@ii[eqn:outernonlin_order1]{\texttt{Nonlin}^{1}}(d\bm{h}_{t-1};\bm{\xi},\eta,\bm{\chi}_{t-1}).

For higher layers, if for brevity we write
h=h^{l}
,
x=x^{l-1}
, and
W=W^{l}
, then

h=Wx
. By
Eq.

3.6
, we have, mathematically,

\displaystyle\theta_{h}\delta\bm{h}_{t}

\displaystyle=\theta_{x}W_{t-1}\delta\bm{x}_{t}+(W_{t}-W_{t-1})\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\theta_{x}\left(W_{0}\delta\bm{x}_{t}+\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}(W_{s}-W_{s-1})\delta\bm{x}_{t}\right)+(W_{t}-W_{t-1})\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\theta_{x}\left(W_{0}\delta\bm{x}_{t}-\eta\theta_{W}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Q_{s-1}^{l}(d\bm{h}_{s-1}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{s-1})\bm{x}_{s-1}^{\top})\frac{\delta\bm{x}_{t}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\eta\theta_{W}Q_{t-1}^{l}(d\bm{h}_{t-1}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{t-1})\bm{x}_{t-1}^{\top})\frac{\bm{x}_{t}}{n}.

Recall
\theta_{x/h}=\theta_{h}^{-1}\theta_{x},\theta_{W/h}=\theta_{h}^{-1}\theta_{W},\theta_{Wx/h}=\theta_{h}^{-1}\theta_{W}\theta_{x}
.
We construct

\displaystyle\delta\bm{h}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\theta_{x/h}W_{0}\delta\bm{x}_{t}-\eta\theta_{Wx/h}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}Q_{s-1}^{l}(d\bm{h}_{s-1}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{s-1})\bm{x}_{s-1}^{\top})\frac{\delta\bm{x}_{t}}{n}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\eta\theta_{W/h}Q_{t-1}^{l}(d\bm{h}_{t-1}\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\chi}_{t-1})\bm{x}_{t-1}^{\top})\frac{\bm{x}_{t}}{n}

\displaystyle=\hyperref@@ii[eqn:outernonlin_order2]{\texttt{Nonlin}^{2}}{}(\textsc{\ref{instr:matmul}}(W_{0},\delta\bm{x}_{t}),d\bm{h}_{0},\ldots,d\bm{h}_{t-1};

\displaystyle\phantom{=\hyperref@@ii[eqn:outernonlin_order2]{\texttt{Nonlin}^{2}}{}(}\bm{x}_{0},\ldots,\bm{x}_{t-1},\delta\bm{x}_{t};

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}_{t}^{l}\rangle\odot\phi^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}d\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{0}^{l\top}d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle-\eta\mathring{\theta}_{W^{l}}\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}_{s}^{l-1}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{s}}\langle d\bm{h}_{s}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}}\rangle

(3.21)

where
\mathring{\theta}_{W^{l}}=\mathbb{I}(r_{l}=0)
.

9.

Error signal

\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t}=\varepsilon_{t}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}).

From this description of the infinite-width limit, it’s clear that the resulting dynamics is both faithful and stable.

.

Theorem 3.1.3
.

Adopt
2.8.2
.
Consider a parametrization satisfying

2.25
,
2.26
,
2.27
and
2.28
.
8
8

8

For reader’s convenience, these equations are resp. the proposed conditions for stability at init, faithful at init, stable and faithful throughout training, and nontriviality.

If
r=0
, then the following are true of this parametrization:

1.

not in the operator regime

2.

feature learning

3.

feature learning in the
L
th layer

4.

prefeature learning

prefeature learning in the

prefeature kernel evolution in the

10.

if there is feature learning
or

or

or

, then there is feature learning
and

and
prefeature learning
and

l,\ldots,L
.

Proof.

WLOG, assume
\mathcal{M}=1
where the sole input is nonzero; our construction will work obviously for general
\mathcal{M}
by masking the error signal
\varepsilon_{t}
.
Correspondingly, we use notation
h^{l}
instead of
\bm{h}^{l}
, etc.

Outline

We will show that as learning rate
\eta\to\infty
,
\langle h^{l}_{1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}h^{l}_{1}\rangle,\langle x^{l}_{1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{l}_{1}\rangle\to\infty
, so that
\langle h^{l}_{1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}h^{l}_{1}\rangle\neq\langle h^{l}_{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}h^{l}_{0}\rangle
and
\langle x^{l}_{1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{l}_{1}\rangle\neq\langle x^{l}_{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{l}_{0}\rangle
for sufficiently large
\eta
.
This would imply (pre)feature kernel evolution and (pre)feature learning of the parametrization.
In addition, we will show that
\mathring{f}_{1}
(output of function after 1 step of update) asympotically grows like
\eta^{s}
for some
s>0
.
If
s\neq 1
, then the dynamics cannot satisfy the the operator equation
Eq.

2.29
, which is linear in
\eta
.
If
s=1
, then we can calculate that
\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\eta^{-1}\mathring{f}_{1}\neq\partial_{\eta}\mathring{f}_{1}|_{\eta=0}
, so that the update is not perfectly linear in
\eta
, violating
Eq.

2.29
, so we are not in the operator regime.
The other claims will also naturally follow over the course of the proof.

The general reasoning is that, when
\eta
is large, by the asymptotic homogeneity of
\phi
(
2.8.2
(3)),
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}h^{l}_{1}\rangle
should roughly be of order
\eta^{1+\delta+\cdots+\delta^{j}}
where
j
is the number of
\ell\leq l
with
r_{l}=0
, and the order of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{l}_{1}\rangle
is the
\delta
th power of that.
This would show
\langle x^{l}_{1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{l}_{1}\rangle\to\infty
.
Some further calculation shows that
\mathring{f}_{1}
is of the same order as
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{l}_{1}\rangle
or
\eta
more than that.
This would violate the operator equation
Eq.

2.29
either because
\mathring{f}_{1}
does not scale linearly in
\eta
or because it’s not perfectly linear in
\eta
.

This reasoning should work for “generic” activations.
However, for concreteness, we impose the conditions of
2.8.2
to be able to easily prove that certain pathological behavior cannot happen where certain inner products vanish, such as
\langle x^{l}_{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{l}_{1}\rangle
, allowing this reasoning to become rigorous.

Proof of theorem

Let
s_{k}=1+\delta+\cdots+\delta^{k-1}

with convention
s_{0}=0
. Let
e_{l}=s_{\sigma},
where

\sigma=\sum_{\ell=1}^{l}\mathbb{I}(r_{\ell}=0)
is the partial count of
how many
r_{l}
s are zero up to layer
l
.
Because
r=\min_{l}r_{l}=0
,
e_{l}>0
for some
l
and is nondecreasing.

Define
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{h}_{1}^{l}\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x_{1}^{l}\rangle/\eta^{e_{l}}

and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{x}_{1}^{l}\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x_{1}^{l}\rangle/\eta^{e_{l}\delta}
.
Design
\varepsilon_{0}
so that
\mathring{\chi}_{0}=-1
. Then we can
see that

\forall l\in[0,L-1],\ \mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{h}_{1}^{l+1}\rangle=\begin{cases}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}dh_{0}^{l}\rangle\langle x_{0}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\tilde{x}_{1}^{l}}\rangle&\text{if $r_{l+1}=0$}\\
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{0}^{l}\tilde{x}_{1}^{l}\rangle&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\forall l\in[1,L],\ \mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{x}_{1}^{l}\rangle=\begin{cases}\mathrm{relu}^{\delta}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{h}_{1}^{l}\rangle)&\text{if $r_{l}=0$}\\
\phi(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{h}_{1}^{l}\rangle)&\text{else if $r_{\ell}>0$ for all $\ell\leq l$}\\
\mathrm{relu}^{\delta}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{h}_{1}^{l}\rangle)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(Pre)Feature kernel evolution

Using this formula and the lemmas above, we can perform an easy induction
to see that 1)
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{x}_{1}^{l}\rangle\geq 0
and is greater than
0 with nonzero probability, and 2)
\langle\tilde{h}_{1}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}h_{0}^{l}\rangle\geq 0

for all
l
.

This already implies that
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x_{1}^{L}\rangle
scales like
\eta^{e_{L}\delta}

and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}h_{1}^{L}\rangle
scales like
\eta^{e_{L}}
in
\eta
, so
the (pre)feature kernel evolves.

Output scaling; violation of the operator equation

By
Eq.

2.28
, either

a_{L+1}+c_{L+1}=1
(last update is maximal) or
a_{L+1}+b_{L+1}+r=1
(last layer initialization is maximal).

If the last layer update is maximal, then its contribution
to the output has the property

\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\eta^{-(e_{L}\delta+1)}\langle\widehat{W}_{1}^{L+1}-\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x_{1}^{L}\rangle=\langle x_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{x}_{1}^{L}\rangle>0

by
Lemma

3.1.5
(and the fact that
\mathrm{relu}^{\delta}

and
\phi
both preserve positivity). This means that the contribution
scales like
\eta^{e_{L}\delta+1}
in
\eta
.

If the last layer initialization is maximal, then
its contribution to the output has the property

\lim_{\eta\to\infty}\eta^{-e_{L}\delta}\langle\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x_{1}^{L}\rangle=\langle\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{x}_{1}^{L}\rangle>0

by Stein’s lemma and
Lemma

3.1.5
. This means the
contribution scales like
\eta^{e_{L}\delta}
in
\eta
.
9
9

9

An important role of
2.8.2
is to prevent pathological cases where the above expectations vanish.

Thus, the output of the function scales either like
\eta^{e_{L}\delta+1}

or
\eta^{e_{L}\delta}
.
This means that, if the last layer update is maximal or
\delta
is not among the discrete set of values where
e_{L}\delta=1
, then
\mathring{f}_{1}
does not scale linearly in
\eta
.

If the last layer update is not maximal and
\delta
takes one of such values (such as
\delta=1
when
r_{l}=0
for exactly one
l
), then one can calculate

\langle\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\tilde{x}_{1}^{L}\rangle\neq\partial_{\eta}\langle\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}{x}_{1}^{L}\rangle|_{\eta=0}.

(3.22)

which means that
\mathring{f}_{1}
is not perfectly linear in
\eta
.

In either case,
Eq.

2.29
cannot be satisfied.

∎

The above proof (especially the section on “Output scaling”) in fact also yields the following.

Theorem 3.1.4
.

If
Eq.

2.28
is not satisfied in
3.1.3
(but
r
is still 0), then the parametrization is trivial.

Helper Lemmas

The significance of the positivity and sign preservation properties in
2.8.2
is that they allow us to apply the following lemmas in our reasoning.

Lemma 3.1.5
.

If
F,G:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
preserve positivity
and are nonnegative,
10
10

10

Obviously
F,G
need to be measurable for the expectation to make sense but let’s not get too pedantic;
rather all functions in this paper are measurable by default.

then

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}F(X)G(Y)>0

for any nonzero random variables
X,Y
with
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}XY\geq 0
.

Proof.

Obviously,
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}XY\geq 0
implies there is nonzero probability that
both
X
and
Y
, and thus
F(X)
and
G(Y)
are positive. Since

F
and
G
are nonnegative, the conclusion follows.
∎

Lemma 3.1.6
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}
be nonnegative random variables.
Suppose
Q
preserves sign. Then the function
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
defined
by

F(\mathbf{z})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}Q(\mathbf{z}X)Y

is identically 0 if
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}XY=0
, but otherwise satisfies

\operatorname{sign}F(\mathbf{z})=\operatorname{sign}\mathbf{z},\ \forall\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}.

Again,
here
\operatorname{sign}
takes value in
\{-1,0,1\}
.

In the language of kets, this implies: For nonnegative kets
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle

and any ket
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}z\rangle
,

F(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}z\rangle)\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}Q(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}z\rangle\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}})\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle

is 0 almost surely if
\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle=0
but otherwise satisfies

\operatorname{sign}F(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}z\rangle)=\operatorname{sign}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}z\rangle

Proof.

If
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}XY=0
, then
XY
is almost surely 0 because
X
and
Y

are nonnegative. Then
Q(\mathbf{z}X)Y
is almost surely 0 because
Q

preserves sign. So its expectation is 0, hence
F
is identically
0.

Otherwise,
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}XY>0
and there is nonzero probability that
X

and
Y
are both positive. In this event,
Q(\mathbf{z}X)Y
has the same
sign as
\mathbf{z}
; otherwise
Q(\mathbf{z}X)Y=0
. Taking average, we see
that
\operatorname{sign}F(\mathbf{z})=\operatorname{sign}\mathbf{z}
as desired.
∎

Proof of Maximal Update Limit

In
\mu
P (
2.5.1
), all of the
\mathring{\theta}
s in
Section

3.1.3
will equal 1.
Some straightforward simplifications then lead to
2.5.2
for the shallow case and
2.7.1
for the deep case.
(We made some simplification in notation as well:
w_{t}^{L+1}
in
2.7.1
corresponds to
\widehat{W}_{t}^{L+1}
in

3.1.3
and

in
2.7.1
corresponds to
W_{t}^{1}
in
Section

3.1.3
)

3.3
Proof of Neural Tangent Limit

In this section, we prove
2.4.7
by specializing the limit formula in
Section

3.1.3
above for general abcd-parametrizations.

In NTP (
2.4.1
), all
r_{l}
equal
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
(
Eq.

2.23
).
In particular, from
Eq.

3.13
, we see that

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l}_{t}\rangle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{l}_{0}\rangle,\quad\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}_{t}\rangle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}_{0}\rangle

for all
l
and all
t
.
Likewise, from
Eq.

3.19
, we see that

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\widehat{W}_{t}^{L+1}\rangle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\widehat{W}_{0}^{L+1}\rangle

and from

3.21
, that

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}d\bm{x}_{t}^{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\widehat{W}_{t}^{L+1}\rangle\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}_{t}^{l}\rangle\odot\phi^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}d\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{0}^{l\top}d\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle

so that, inductively,

\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}_{t}\rangle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{x}^{l}_{0}\rangle,\quad\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}_{t}\rangle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}_{0}\rangle

for all
l
and all
t
.
These consequences justify the notational decision to drop the
t
subscript in
Section

2.4
.
We now adopt this notation as well in the rest of this section.
However, note that
\delta\mathring{f}_{t}
,
\delta W_{t}^{L+1}
,
\delta\bm{h}^{l}_{t}
, and
\delta\bm{x}^{l}_{t}
still vary with
t
.

In addition, plugging in the NTP values of abcd to
Eqs.

3.14
,
3.17
and
3.20
gives

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}\delta\bm{x}^{l}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\phi^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{h}^{l}\rangle)\odot\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}^{l}_{t}\rangle.

(3.23)

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\talloblong$}\delta\bm{h}^{l}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{0}\delta\bm{x}^{l-1}_{t}\rangle-\eta\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t-1}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}^{l-1}}\rangle

(3.24)

\displaystyle\delta\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\langle\delta W_{t}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}^{L}\rangle+\langle\widehat{W}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{x}_{t}^{L}\rangle

(3.25)

To show
2.4.7
, we need to calculate
\delta\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}
and show it equals
-\eta\mathcal{K}_{Q}(\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t-1})
.

There are two contributions to
\delta\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}
from
Eq.

3.25
.
We first calculate the former.
By
Eq.

3.18
,

\displaystyle\langle\delta W_{t}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}_{t}^{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\eta\overline{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t-1}^{\top}\langle\bm{x}_{t-1}^{L}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}_{t}^{L}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\eta\overline{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t-1}^{\top}\langle\bm{x}^{L}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}^{L}}\rangle

(3.26)

(where the bar notation abbreviates
Q_{t-1}^{L+1}
)
which matches with the output weight contribution from
Eq.

2.8
.

To calculate the latter term from
Eq.

3.25
, we employ the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

For any
l\in[2,L]
,

\langle d\bm{h}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle=\langle d\bm{h}^{L-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{L-1}\rangle-\eta\langle{d\bm{h}^{l}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t-1}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}^{l-1}}\rangle.

For
l=1
, we have

\langle d\bm{h}^{1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{1}\rangle=-\eta\langle{d\bm{h}^{1}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{1}\rangle_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t-1}}\bm{\xi}^{\top}}\bm{\xi}.

Proof.

For
l=1
, this follows trivially from
Eq.

3.16
. For
l\in[2,L]
,
by
Eq.

3.24
,

\langle d\bm{h}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{l}\rangle=\langle d\bm{h}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{0}^{l}\delta x_{t}^{l-1}\rangle-\eta\langle{d\bm{h}^{l}}\overline{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}d\bm{h}^{l}\rangle{}_{\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t-1}}\langle\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}}{\bm{x}^{l-1}}\rangle.

So it remains to show
\langle d\bm{h}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{0}^{l}\delta\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle=\langle d\bm{h}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle
.
But applying
Lemma

4.8.3
,

\displaystyle\langle d\bm{h}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}W_{0}^{l}\delta\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle W_{0}^{l\top}d\bm{h}^{l}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle=\langle d\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{x}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d\bm{x}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\phi^{\prime}(\bm{h}^{l-1})\odot\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d\bm{x}^{l-1}\odot\phi^{\prime}(\bm{h}^{l-1})\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d\bm{h}^{l-1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{l-1}\rangle

as desired.
∎

Then the latter term from
Eq.

3.25
is

\langle\widehat{W}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{x}_{t}^{L}\rangle=\langle\widehat{W}^{L+1}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\phi^{\prime}(\bm{h}^{L})\odot\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{L}\rangle=\langle\widehat{W}^{L+1}\odot\phi^{\prime}(\bm{h}^{L})\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{L}\rangle=\mathrm{Diag}\langle d\bm{h}^{L}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\delta\bm{h}_{t}^{L}\rangle

which, by
Eq.

3.26
and a trivial induction with
Lemma

3.3.1
, gives

\delta\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t}=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}}(\mathring{\bm{\chi}}_{t-1})=-\eta\mathcal{K}_{\bm{Q}}(\varepsilon_{t-1}(\mathring{\bm{f}}_{t-1}))

as desired.

Chapter 4
Proof of Master Theorem

To prove our main foundational theorem
2.6.10
, we had an editorial choice: we could bash our way to a proof by repeating (over and over again)
analytical
arguments involving the likes of Holder and Cauchy-Schwarz, or we could encapsulate them into neat objects with neat properties that then make the underlying
algebraic
structure more transparent.
Even though the writing is more arduous, we chose the latter way, because of this transparency and because it builds a more extensible foundation for future work.
1
1

1

extensible
in the sense that properties can be used as black-boxes for more advanced theorems. This is definitely a more
algebraic
style, compared to analysts who tend to open black boxes and constantly tweak the insides.

The drawback is perhaps that there can be many long sections building up the underlying structures before the payoff — but that’s the choice we made, and the reader is stuck with it, for better (hopefully) or worse.

4.1
Basic Tools

Here we just record some basic lemmas that we would use frequently.
The reader can skip this on first read and come back when necessary.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

For an integer
m
, and complex numbers
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
,
i\in[k]
,

\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}\right|^{m}\leq k^{m-1}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left|a_{i}\right|^{m}.

Proof.

Expand the power in the LHS using the multinomial theorem, apply AM-GM to each summand, and finally aggregate using triangle inequality.
∎

Lemma 4.1.2
.

Let
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of random variables with zero mean.
If for some
p\in\mathbb{N}
and for all
n
,
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}X_{n}^{2p}\leq cn^{-1-\lambda}
, for some
\lambda>0
, then
X_{n}\to 0
almost surely.

Proof.

By Markov’s inequality, for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr(|X_{n}|>\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\Pr(X_{n}^{2p}>\epsilon^{2p})\leq\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}X_{n}^{2p}/\epsilon^{2p}\leq cn^{-1-\lambda}/\epsilon^{2p}

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\Pr(|X_{n}|>\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n}cn^{-1-\lambda}/\epsilon^{2p}<\infty.

By Borel-Cantelli Lemma, almost surely,
|X_{n}|\leq\epsilon
for all large
n
.
Then, if we pick a sequence
\{\epsilon_{k}>0\}_{k}
converging to 0, we have that, almost surely, for each
k
,
|X_{n}|\leq\epsilon_{k}
for large enough
n
— i.e. almost surely,
X_{n}\to 0
.
∎

Lemma 4.1.3
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a random vector with
iid components such that
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}x_{1}=0
and
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}|x_{1}|^{p}<\infty

for all integer
p\geq 1
. Then there exist constants
C_{p}
, independent
of
n
, for all
p\geq 1
such that
S\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}x_{\alpha}

satisfies

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}|S|^{p}<C_{p},\quad\forall p\geq 1.

Furthermore,
C_{p}
can be taken to be
P_{p}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{p})

where

•

\nu_{k}
is an upper bound on the signed
k
th moment
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}x_{1}^{k}

of
x_{1}

•

P_{p}
is a polynomial that depends only on
p
.

Proof.

See
[
12
, prop H.2]
.
∎

4.1.1
Review of Moore-Penrose Pseudo-Inverse

We recall Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse and some properties of it.

Definition 4.1.4
.

For
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
, a pseudo-inverse of
A
is defined as a matrix
A^{+}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
that satisfies all of the following criteria

\displaystyle AA^{+}A

\displaystyle=A,

\displaystyle A^{+}AA^{+}

\displaystyle=A^{+},

\displaystyle(AA^{+})^{\top}

\displaystyle=AA^{+},

\displaystyle(A^{+}A)^{\top}

\displaystyle=A^{+}A.

The following facts are standard.

•

If
A
has real entries, then so does
A^{+}
.

•

The pseudo-inverse always exists and is unique.

•

When
A
is invertible,
A^{+}=A^{-1}
.

•

(A^{\top})^{+}=(A^{+})^{\top}
, which we denote as
A^{+\top}
.

•

A^{+}=(A^{\top}A)^{+}A^{\top}=A^{\top}(AA^{\top})^{+}
.

•

AA^{+}
is the orthogonal projector to the column space of
A
;

I-A^{+}A
is the orthogonal project to the null space of
A
.

•

If
A
has singular value decomposition
A=U\Lambda V
where
U
and
V
are orthogonal and
\Lambda
has the singular values on its diagonal, then
A^{+}=V^{\top}\Lambda^{+}U^{\top}
where
\Lambda^{+}
inverts all nonzero entries of
\Lambda
.

•

For any collection of vectors
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
in a Hilbert space,
w\mapsto\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}v_{i}(\Sigma^{+})_{ij}\langle v_{j},w\rangle
, where
\Sigma_{ij}=\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle
, is the projection operator to the linear span of
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

4.1.2
Baranyai’s Theorem

Figure 4.1:

A graphical illustration of Baranyai’s Theorem for
n=8,r=2
.
Here
G^{8}_{2}
is just the usual complete graph on 8 vertices.
A
perfect matching
here reduces to the usual meaning on graphs: a set of 4 edges that covers all 8 vertices.
Every edge above is colored, and for each color, the edges with that color form a perfect matching.

Image source:

wikipedia.org

The complete hypergraph
G^{n}_{r}
is a hypergraph containing
n
vertices in which every subset of
r
vertices forms a hyperedge.
A
perfect matching
of it is a set of
\nicefrac{{n}}{{r}}
hyperedges that (thought of as subsets of vertices) partitions the vertices of
G^{n}_{r}
.

Theorem 4.1.5

(Baranyai’s Theorem)
.

Suppose integer
r
divides integer
n
.
The collection of all
\binom{n}{r}
hyperedges in
G^{n}_{r}
can be partitioned into
\binom{n}{r}\frac{r}{n}
perfect matchings.

See
Fig.

4.1
for a graphical illustration.
The hyperedges of
G^{n}_{r}
are just the
r
-element subsets of
[n]
.
A version of Baranyai’s Theorem also holds for ordered
r
-element subsets, i.e., length-
r
sequences of distinct elements of
[n]
.

Theorem 4.1.6

(Ordered Baranyai’s Theorem)
.

Suppose integer
r
divides integer
n
.
The collection of all
n(n-1)\cdots(n-r+1)
length-
r
sequences of distinct elements of
[n]
can be partitioned into
r(n-1)\cdots(n-r+1)
perfect matchings.

The proof follows from
4.1.5
and symmetrization.

4.2
Basic Objects and Operations

In this section, we define the basic objects and operations on them recurring in our quest to understand Tensor Programs.
In the next, we describe their properties.

4.2.1
Fixed-Dimensional Random Variables and Vectors

Notation

We will predominantly consider sequences of random objects indexed
by an integer
n
(see
Section

4.2.2
below). In this context,
to emphasize objects that do not vary with
n
, we will use uppercase
letters like
X,Y,\ldots
for scalar random variables and their bolded
versions
\bm{X},\bm{Y},\ldots
for random vectors, matrices, or tensors.
The latter will have components denoted by superscripts, e.g.,
\bm{X}=(X^{1},\ldots,X^{k})

if
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

4.2.2
Space of Random Sequences

Prior papers in the Tensor Programs series often talk about objects
like scalars, vectors, and matrices whose size varies with a global
notion of “width” denoted
n
. Formally, each such object is
a sequence (of scalars, vectors, or matrices) in
n
, but to be intuitive,
these works downplay this sequence aspect (for example, by suppressing
the dependence on
n
notationally).

However, here we need to talk about more complex high order tensors
who can contain both dimensions that scale with
n
and those that
do not. In addition, we will formulate a notion of “vanishing”
tensors that is really an asymptotic property as
n\to\infty
rather
than a nonasymptotic one; this notion turns out to interact cleanly
with typical operations like pseudo-Lipschitz nonlinearities that
is involved in Tensor Programs. As such, for these reasons, we will
be explicit that the objects we play with here are sequences in
n
:

Definition 4.2.1
.

For any integer
s\geq 0
, denote by
\mathcal{S}^{s}
the set of all
random sequences
x
of order-
s
tensors with dimensions
\underbrace{n\times n\times\cdots\times n}_{s}

(i.e., sequences
x
with
x(n)\in\otimes^{s}\mathbb{R}^{n}
for all
n=1,2,\ldots
).
We call the objects in
\mathcal{S}^{0}
,
\mathcal{S}^{1}
,
\mathcal{S}^{2}

respectively
n
-scalars
,
n
-vectors
, and
n
-matrices
.
For general
s
, we call the objects in
\mathcal{S}^{s}

n
-tensors
.
Often, we will drop the prefix “
n
-” when the context is clear.

So, for example,
\mathcal{S}^{0}
contains all infinite sequences
of scalar random variables, and
\mathcal{S}^{1}
contains all infinite
sequences of random vectors of linearly increasing size.

Notation

We will use Greek letters
\alpha,\beta,\ldots
(with values in
[n]
)
to denote indices of an
n
-tensor. For higher order
n
-tensors
in
\mathcal{S}^{s}
, we also use their bolded counterparts
{\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}},\ldots

(with values in
[n]^{s}
) to denote multi-indices, where (for example)

{\bm{\alpha}}
is understood to have components
{\bm{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s})
.
For example, if
x\in\mathcal{S}^{s}
, then
x(n)
has entries
\{x(n)_{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{s}}:\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in[n]\}=\{x(n)_{{\bm{\alpha}}}:{\bm{\alpha}}\in[n]^{s}\}
.
We can also mix single indices and multi-indices, e.g.,
\{x(n)_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}:\alpha\in[n],{\bm{\beta}}\in[n]^{s-1}\}
.

As in prior works, even though we will work with sequences of tensors

(x(n))_{n\geq 1}
, we will suppress the dependence on
n
notationally
and talk about
x
as if it’s a fixed tensor. So, for example, for
an
n
-matrix
x
,
x_{\alpha\beta}
refers to the entry
x(n)_{\alpha\beta}

where the
n
is from context.

4.2.3
Multi-Tensors

In many results we shall discuss, we often talk about lists of
n
-tensors,
e.g.,
x^{1},\ldots,x^{k}\in\mathcal{S}^{s}
. Going forward, it will
be helpful to think of such lists as (a sequence of) a single tensor
of shape
n\times n\times\cdots\times n\times k
(or other arrangements
of dimensions, as discussed below) for each
n
. We generalize this
further in the following definition.

Definition 4.2.2

(Multi-Tensors)
.

For any integers
s\geq 0
and
k\geq 0
, let

\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}
denote the set of all random sequences (in

n
) of tensors of shape
\underbrace{n\times\cdots\times n}_{s}\times k
.

In general, suppose
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times\cdots\times k_{r}}
. Then

\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
denotes the same but for shape
\underbrace{n\times\cdots\times n}_{s}\times k_{1}\times\cdots\times k_{r}
.
We shall call any element of such a space a
multi-tensor
. When

s=0,1,2
, we also call such elements
multi-scalar
,
multi-vector
,
and
multi-matrix
respectively.

Intuitively,
\mathcal{S}^{s}
can be read as
\otimes^{s}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
so that
\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times\cdots\times k_{r}}

can be read as
\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times\cdots\times k_{r}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k_{r}}
,
which makes the tensor shapes more apparent.

More abstractly, we can let
\mathcal{V}
be any finite-dimensional Euclidean
space, in which case
\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
is the space of
sequences of tensors with shape
\underbrace{n\times\cdots\times n}_{s}
,
taking values in
\mathcal{V}
. However, in this work, we will primarly
concern ourselves with the
\mathcal{V}
being tensor spaces as in
4.2.2
.

Definition 4.2.3
.

In this work, by
Euclidean space
we mean any finite-dimensional
real vector space. We will use notation
\mathcal{V}
and its cousins to
denote such spaces.

In this abstraction, we will also talk about nonlinearities that map
between Euclidean spaces (e.g.,
\psi:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
), generalizing
the scalar functions (e.g.,
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
) typical of prior
works. This generality allows us to compose nonlinearities more naturally,
which simplifies our presentation conceptually and our proofs technically.

Notation

We will always use lower case letters
x,y,z,\ldots
to denote
n
-tensors
(i.e., elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}
). On the other hand, we
will always use their bolded counterparts
\bm{x},\bm{y},\bm{z},\ldots
to
denote multi-tensors (i.e., elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}\otimes\mathcal{V}
).

We think of a multi-tensor as a (fixed-size) tuple of
n
-tensors:
for example,
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}
is thought of as a tuple

(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k})
of
n
-tensors
x^{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{s}
.
As in this example, we will always use letters
i,j,k,\ldots
in
superscript for “constant-sized” indices (corresponding to
\mathbb{R}^{k}

in the example) to distinguish from the Greek letters
\alpha,\beta,\ldots

in subscript for the “
n
-sized” indices. This is consistent
with the notation in
Section

4.2.1
for fixed-dimensional
objects. So
\bm{x}
here has elements
\{x_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{i}:{\bm{\alpha}}\in[n]^{s}\text{ and }i\in[k]\}
.
In particular, for any
{\bm{\alpha}}\in[n]^{s}
,
\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\{x_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{i}\}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
;
for any
i\in[k]
,
x^{i}=\{x_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{i}\}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\in\mathcal{S}^{s}
.
Note that when we pick the
i
th component
x^{i}
of
\bm{x}
, the
letter
x
is not bolded.

In contrast, we will need to talk about a list of multi-tensors as
well,
\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}
, where we use the superscript letters

r,s,t,\ldots
to denote the indices of this list. Each multi-tensor
has components
\bm{x}^{t}=\{x^{ti}\}_{i}=\{x_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{ti}\}_{i,{\bm{\alpha}}}=\{\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{t}\}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}
.
Again, notationally, notice that
\bm{x}^{t}
means a multi-tensor in
a sequence, but
x^{i}
means the
i
th component of multi-tensor

\bm{x}
.

4.2.4
Constant Tensors

Definition 4.2.4

(Notation for constant sequences)
.

Often we will need to talk about some sequence
(in
n
) that equals a fixed value, say
\vartheta
, for all
n
.
Then we shall denote this sequence by
\vartheta
as well, which
should not cause confusion in our contexts.

4.2.5
IID Tensors

Definition 4.2.5
.

Let
\bm{X}
be any random object. Then
\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 2$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 2$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 2$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\ldots

denote iid copies of
\bm{X}
:
\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\bm{X}
for all

i
and
\bm{X},\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 2$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 2$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 2$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\ldots
are mutually
independent. If
\bm{Y}
is another random object, then
\bm{Y}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\bm{Y}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 2$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 2$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 2$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\ldots

are iid copies of
\bm{Y}
that furthermore satisfy
(\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\bm{Y}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})

for each
i
.

In other words, the collection of all objects with superscript
\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}

forms an “isomorphic world” to the collection with superscript

\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle j$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle j$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle j$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\kern 0.5pt}}
. We will always use the above notation when we want to
make a “constant number” (wrt
n
) of iid copies (except in
4.2.6
below). However, we
will also at times need to make
n
(or powers of
n
) iid copies,
for which we use the following notation instead.
We first give the general but abstract definition before substantiating it with examples.

Definition 4.2.6
.

For every integer
s\geq 0
, We define the
iid operator

\mathrm{iid}:\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{S}^{s+1}\otimes\mathcal{V}

as follows.
If
