\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
, then
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{x})\in\mathcal{S}^{s+1}\otimes\mathcal{V}

is the multi-tensor satisfying, for any
\alpha\in[n]
,

\mathrm{iid}(\bm{x})(n)_{\alpha}=\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle\alpha$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}\kern 0.5pt}}}(n).

Example 4.2.7
.

If
Z\in\mathbb{R}
is a (scalar) random variable, we can think of
Z
as the sequence in
\mathcal{S}^{0}
identically equal to
Z
(c.f.
4.2.4
).
Then
\mathrm{iid}(Z)
denotes
the sequence in
\mathcal{S}^{1}
where, for each
n\geq 1
, the
n
th
element
\mathrm{iid}(Z)(n)
is the
n
-dimensional random vector where
each entry is an iid copy of
Z
.
2
2

2

Furthermore,
\mathrm{iid}(Z)(n)
is identical to the first
n
elements of
\mathrm{iid}(Z)(n+1)
. However, we will never use this fact here.

Similarly, if
\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{V}
for some Euclidean space
\mathcal{V}
,
then
for each
n
,
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{Z})(n)_{\alpha}
is an iid copy of
\bm{Z}
for every

\alpha\in[n]
.

In general, if
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times\cdots\times k_{r}}

is a random tensor, then
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{Z})\in\mathcal{S}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times\cdots\times k_{r}}

is the sequence whose
n
th element is the shape-
(n\times k_{1}\times\cdots\times k_{r})

tensor
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{Z})(n)
where, for each
\alpha\in[n]
,
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{Z})(n)_{\alpha}

is an iid copy of
\bm{Z}
.

Example 4.2.8
.

If
Z\in\mathbb{R}
is a random variable, then
\mathrm{iid}^{2}(Z)=\mathrm{iid}(\mathrm{iid}(Z))
is the sequence of
n\times n
matrices with iid entries drawn from
Z
.

When we iterate
\mathrm{iid}
and want to clarify or emphasize the shape information, we will
also write
\mathrm{iid}_{n}
for
\mathrm{iid}
,
\mathrm{iid}_{n\times n}
for
\mathrm{iid}^{2}
,

\mathrm{iid}_{n\times n\times n}
for
\mathrm{iid}^{3}
, and so on.
So
\mathrm{iid}_{n\times n}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
is the sequence of
n\times n
standard Gaussian matrices.

Remark 4.2.9
.

Note that
\mathrm{iid}
is only well defined up to distributional equality.
Thus, if for example one wants to prove almost sure equality and the proof involves
\mathrm{iid}
, then one needs to carefully check that the usage of
\mathrm{iid}
makes sense.

4.2.6
Averaging over
n

Definition 4.2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a finite-dimensional Euclidean space, and let
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}^{s+r}\otimes\mathcal{V}

be a multi-tensor. For
{\bm{\alpha}}\in[n]^{s},{\bm{\beta}}\in[n]^{r}
, we write

\left\langle\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\frac{1}{n^{r}}\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}\in[n]^{r}}\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}}\in\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}

for averaging over multi-index
{\bm{\beta}}
while fixing multi-index

{\bm{\alpha}}
.

This notation can be nested, e.g.,
\left\langle F\left(\left\langle\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\right)\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}
for
some function
F
. When
F
is identity, this is obviously just

\left\langle\bm{x}_{\bm{\gamma}}\right\rangle_{\bm{\gamma}}
.

4.2.7
Implicit Broadcasting of Nonlinearities on Multi-Tensors

Given a nonlinearity
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
and
n
-vectors
x^{1},\ldots,x^{k}
,
the expression
y=\phi(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k})
denotes another
n
-vector

y
with entries
y_{\alpha}=\phi(x_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots,x_{\alpha}^{k})

(with the multi-tensor notation
\bm{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k})
, we can
also write
y=\phi(\bm{x})
). In the terminology of linear-algebra software
(such as numpy), we say “
\phi
is implicitly broadcast across
the dimension
n
.”

In general, this implicit broadcast rule holds for any
n
-tensors
or multi-tensors.

Definition 4.2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be any finite-dimensional Euclidean
space (e.g.,
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times\cdots\times k_{r}}
). For any
multi-tensor
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
and
\phi:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}
,
the expression
\phi(\bm{x})
denotes a new
n
-tensor
y\in\mathcal{S}^{s}

with
y_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\phi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})
.
3
3

3

Unpacking this a bit: for each
{\bm{\alpha}}\in[n]^{s}
,
\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\in\mathcal{V}
,
so
\phi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})
is well-defined and yields a value in
\mathbb{R}

More generally, if
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
is another Euclidean space and
\phi:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
,
then
\phi(\bm{x})\in\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
with
y_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\phi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})
.

In other words, the implicit broadcasting lifts
\phi:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

to a function
\phi:\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

for any
s\geq 0
.

4.2.8
Nonlinear Outer Products

An outer product of two vectors
x,y
is the matrix
W
with entries

W_{\alpha\beta}=x_{\alpha}y_{\beta}
. This can be written trivially
as
W_{\alpha\beta}=\psi(x_{\alpha};y_{\beta})
for
\psi(-;-)

being the product function. This is generalized by a notion of
nonlinear
outer product
, where
\psi
can be any function, not just product.
We make the definition below in full generality, but for first reading,
it may help to mentally set
\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{r},\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
to
be
\mathbb{R}
to understand the basic idea.

Definition 4.2.12

(Nonlinear Outer Product)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{r},\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

be any finite dimensional Euclidean spaces. Suppose
\psi:\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{r}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
,
where we format
\psi
’s arguments in blocks
\psi(-;-;\cdots;-)
,
with the
t
th block “
-
” corresponding to
\mathcal{V}_{t}
. Then
given
\bm{x}^{t}\in\mathcal{S}^{s_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{t}
for
t=1,\ldots,r
,
we write

\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})

for the multi-tensor
\bm{y}
in
\mathcal{S}^{\sum_{t}s_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

with entries

\bm{y}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}=\psi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}^{r}),\quad\text{for all }\text{${\bm{\alpha}}_{t}$$\in[n]^{s_{t}}$}.

We call this the
\psi
-outer product of
\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}
.

Let’s unpack the multi-tensor notation a bit. For example, if
\bm{x}^{t}=(x^{t1},x^{t2})

for every
t=1,\ldots,r
, then

\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}=\psi(x_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}}^{11},x_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}}^{12};\cdots;x_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}^{r1},x_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}^{r2}).

In other words, arguments in the same block have matching (multi-)indices,
while arguments in different blocks have freely varying indices. This
in particular generalizes
4.2.11
, which applies when
there is only one block in
\psi
. Likewise, the implicit broadcasting
in this nonlinear outer product lifts
\psi:\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{r}\to{\cal V}^{\prime}

to a function
\psi:\mathcal{S}^{s_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{S}^{s_{r}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{r}\to\mathcal{S}^{\sum_{i}s_{i}}\otimes{\cal V}^{\prime}
.

We isolate the case when
\psi:\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{r}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

is the identity function with
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{r}
.

Definition 4.2.13
.

(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})
is called the
semicolon product
of
\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}
.
It has entries

(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}=(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}^{r}).

The importance of this operation is that: for any
\psi
, the
\psi
-outer
product is just the composition of the semicolon product followed
by application of
\psi
as in
4.2.11
.

\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})=\psi(\bm{x}),\quad\text{where}\quad\bm{x}=(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r}).

(4.1)

This fact simplifies the proofs involving nonlinear outer products.

Consistency with “Nonlinearities with Parameters”

The semicolon notation in nonlinear outer product is consistent with
previous papers in the Tensor Programs series. There, the nonlinearities
are all of the form
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}
, applied to
k

n
-vectors
x^{1},\ldots,x^{k}
and
l

n
-scalars
\theta^{1},\ldots,\theta^{l}

of the program to produce a single vector:

\psi(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k};\theta^{1},\ldots,\theta^{l}).

In light of
4.2.12
, this is now interpreted as a
\psi
-outer
product of the multi-vector
\bm{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k})
and the multi-scalar

\bm{\theta}=(\theta^{1},\ldots,\theta^{l})
.

4.3
Vanishing and Bounded Moments

4.3.1
Moment-Bounded Multi-Tensors

For the following discussion, let
\mathcal{V}
be finite-dimensional Eucldiean
spaces and let
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
be a multi-tensor.
For first time reading, one may mentally set
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}
, so that

\mathcal{S}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{S}^{s}
, to get the basic intuitions
more quickly.

In this work, we will especially focus on
\bm{x}
where each entry
of
\bm{x}(n)
has “typical size
O(1)
” as
n\to\infty
. We formalize
this “typical size
O(1)
” criterion as follows.

Definition 4.3.1
.

We say a multi-tensor
\bm{x}
is
entrywise moment-bounded
, or
just
moment-bounded
for short, if the following holds: for
every integer
p\geq 1

\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\tilde{O}(1).

with
\tilde{O}
from
1.2.5
.
We write
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
for the space of such multi-tensors.

Unpacking the big-O notation,
\bm{x}
is moment-bounded iff, for
every integer
p\geq 1

and every
\epsilon>0
, we have

n^{-\epsilon}\cdot\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0,\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

Intuitively, if one thinks of the entries
\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}
as samples from a distribution

\mathcal{D}
, then
\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}

is the (empirical)
p
th moment of
\mathcal{D}
. Thus,
moment-boundedness

just means that
\mathcal{D}
has bounded empirical moments of every order (ignoring logarithmic factors),
i.e., samples from
\mathcal{D}
has typical size
O(1)
.

Basic Properties

First, one can note the following trivial property that is useful
for simplifying proofs.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

\bm{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k})
is moment-bounded iff each of its components

x^{1},\ldots,x^{k}
is moment-bounded.

A related property holds for “components along
n
”:

Proposition 4.3.3
.

If random
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
has all moments, then
\mathrm{iid}^{r}(\bm{Z})
is moment-bounded for all
r\geq 0
.

Moment-boundedness is closed under applications of polynomially bounded
functions: in short,

•

\psi(\bm{x})
is moment-bounded if
\bm{x}
is, and

•

more generally,
\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})
is moment-bounded
if
\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}
are.

We make this formal in the following

Proposition 4.3.4
.

Consider moment-bounded multi-tensors

\bm{x}^{t}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{t}
for each
t=1,\ldots,r
.
Let
\psi(-;\cdots;-):\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{r}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

be polynomially bounded. Then
\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})
is moment-bounded
as well:
\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{\sum_{t}s_{t}}\otimes{\cal V}^{\prime}
.

For example, for
r=1
, when we take
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
as the sum
and product functions, we have that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}
is closed under entrywise
sum and product. Similarly, when we take
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{k\times l+l\times m}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times m}

to be the matrix multiplication function (multiplying matrices of
sizes
k\times l
and
l\times m
to get one of size
k\times m
),
we derive the closure of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
under matrix
multiplication (after setting
l=m=k
).

Proof.

By polynomially-boundedness of
\psi
,
for any
q>0
, there are
C,p
such that, for any
\bm{z}^{t}\in\mathcal{V}_{t},t=1,\ldots,r
,
we have

\|\psi(\bm{z}^{1};\cdots;\bm{z}^{r})\|_{q}^{q}\leq C(1+\|\bm{z}^{1}\|_{p}^{p}+\cdots+\|\bm{z}^{r}\|_{p}^{p}).

Therefore, for
{\bm{\beta}}\in[n]^{s}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\|\psi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}^{r})\|_{q}^{q}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\langle(1+\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}}^{1}\|_{p}^{p}+\cdots+\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}^{r}\|_{p}^{p})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}

\displaystyle=C\left(1+\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{1}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}+\cdots+\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{r}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\right).

But, for each
t\in[r]
, since
\bm{x}^{t}
is moment-bounded, we have

n^{-\epsilon}\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{t}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

for any
\epsilon>0
. Therefore,

n^{-\epsilon}\left\langle\|\psi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}^{r})\|_{q}^{q}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}}\leq n^{-\epsilon}C\left(1+\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{1}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}+\cdots+\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{r}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\right)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

as well. Since
q
and
\epsilon
are arbitrary in this argument,
this shows
\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})
is moment-bounded.
∎

4.3.2
Vanishing Multi-Tensors

A mentioned above, a notion of “vanishing” will play a central
role in what follows:

Definition 4.3.5
.

We say a multi-tensors
\bm{x}
is
entrywise vanishing
, or just

vanishing
for short, if

\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\|^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\tilde{O}(n^{-1}),

with
\tilde{O}
from
1.2.5
.
We write
\mathcal{S}_{0}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
for the space of such multi-tensors.
4
4

4

We emphasize that “vanishing” is a shorthand for “entrywise-vanishing”
and
not
interpreted as “normwise-vanishing”. For example,
an entrywise-vanishing vector can still have
\Omega(1)
norm, such
as the vector with entries
n^{-1/2}
; a random Gaussian matrix with
iid
\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
entries will have
\Theta(1)
operator norm almost
surely as
n\to\infty
. But since we will never talk about “normwise-vanishing,”
for our purposes it will not cause confusion to abbreviate “entrywise-vanishing”
to just “vanishing.”

Unpacking the big-O notation,

\bm{x}
is vanishing iff, for every
\epsilon>0
,

n^{1-\epsilon}\cdot\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\|^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0,\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

At first, using the same intuition as above, one may think of vanishing
tensors as those whose entries have typical size
O(1/\sqrt{n})
.

But notice that “vanishing” is defined only via
L^{2}
norm,
while “moment-bounded” is defined via every
L^{p}
norm. This
is an important technical distinction. The primary purpose of this
distinction is that when
x
is an
n
-vector and
W
is an iid
matrix with (for example)
\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
entries,

Wx
is vanishing if
x
is; see

4.3.14
.

This is because of the well-known almost sure operator-norm bounds

[
38
,
48
]
on such iid matrices
W
. If we defined “vanishing”
based on other
L^{p}
norm as well, then we cannot make the same
statement as it will be much more difficult to control the
L^{p}

norm of
Wx
.

As a result of this difference in the definitions, “vanishing”
implies “moment-bounded”
only for scalars and vectors
(i.e.,

\mathcal{S}_{0}^{s}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}
only for
s=0,1
); see
Lemma

4.3.7

below. For example, consider the
n
-matrix
x
with all-zero entries
except the value
\sqrt{n}
in its top left corner. It is vanishing
(because
\left\langle|x_{{\bm{\alpha}}}|^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\Theta(1/n)
)
but not moment-bounded (e.g.,
\left\langle|x_{{\bm{\alpha}}}|^{8}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\Theta(n^{2})
).
The takeaway here is that while moment-bounded tensors can be thought
of as “every entry is
O(1)
”, vanishing tensors can have a small
number of entries that explode with
n
, as long as the quadratic
mean of the entries has size
O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
.

We summarize these intuitions succinctly as follows (because they
are important to understand sooner than later):

moment-bounded

\displaystyle\approx\text{dense entries all of size $O(1)$}

vanishing

\displaystyle\approx\text{potentially sparse entries, $O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})$ in quadratic mean}.

Basic Properties

Like for moment-boundedness, the “vanishing” property is reducible
to components, which makes proofs a bit simpler.

Proposition 4.3.6
.

\bm{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k})
is vanishing iff each of its components

x^{1},\ldots,x^{k}
is vanishing.

As mentioned above,
\mathcal{S}_{0}^{s}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}
only for
s=0,1
.

Lemma 4.3.7
.

Let
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
for
Euclidean space
\mathcal{V}
. Whenever
s=0,1
or else
p<\frac{2s}{s-1}
,
we have

\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0.

Consequently,
\mathcal{S}_{0}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
for

s=0,1
. i.e., all vanishing multi-scalars and multi-vectors are
moment-bounded. But this is false for
s\geq 2
.

Proof.

For clarity, we prove the claim for
x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}^{s}
; the generalization
to
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
follows from this case componentwise.

By
L^{p}
norm inequalities, for all
p\geq 2
,

\|x(n)\|_{p}\leq\|x(n)\|_{2}

so

\|x(n)\|_{p}^{p}/n^{s}\leq\|x(n)\|_{2}^{p}/n^{s}=K{}^{p/2}

where

K\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\|x(n)\|_{2}^{2}/n^{s\cdot\frac{2}{p}}.

When
s=0
or 1 or when
p<\frac{2s}{s-1}
, we have
s\cdot\frac{2}{p}>s-1
,
so that, for any sufficiently small
\delta>0
,

K<n^{1-\delta}\cdot\|x(n)\|_{2}^{2}/n^{s}\quad\text{for all $n$}.

But since
x
is vanishing, the RHS goes to 0 almost surely, and
therefore so does
K
. We thus have

\|x(n)\|_{p}^{p}/n^{s}=\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\|_{p}^{p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=K^{p/2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

as desired.
∎

The following yields a sufficient condition for vanishing that is
often easier to show than directly showing vanishing itself. Roughly
speaking, it says an
n
-tensor is vanishing if every entry looks
like
O(1/\sqrt{n})
as measured by every power mean expectation.

Proposition 4.3.8
.

Suppose
v\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}
. If there exist constants
C_{p,\varepsilon}

for all
\varepsilon>0
and all integers
p\geq 1
such that we have
the following inequality

n^{-s}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\|v\|_{2p}^{2p}=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle v_{\alpha}^{2p}\right\rangle_{\alpha}\leq C_{p,\varepsilon}n^{-p+\varepsilon}

for all
p,\varepsilon
and
n
, then
v
is vanishing.

Proof.

WLOG we assume
s=1
, since the general case follows by unrolling

v
into a (giant) vector.

To show
v
is vanshing, we need to show
n^{-\epsilon}\|v\|^{2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

for any
\epsilon>0
. By
Lemma

4.1.2
, we just need to
show that for some
q,\gamma>0
, we have
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}(n^{-\epsilon}\|v\|^{2})^{q}=O(n^{-1-\gamma})
.
But
\|v\|\leq n^{1/2-1/2q}\|v\|_{2q}
for all
q\geq 1
. Thus, for
any
q\geq 1
,

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}(n^{-\epsilon}\|v\|^{2})^{q}\leq n^{-\epsilon q}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}n^{q-1}\|v\|_{2q}^{2q}\leq n^{q-\epsilon q}C_{q,\varepsilon}n^{-q+\varepsilon}=n^{\varepsilon-\epsilon q}C_{q,\varepsilon}.

Whence we can take any small
\varepsilon>0
and
q>(1+\varepsilon)/\epsilon

to have
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}(n^{-\epsilon}\|v\|^{2})^{q}=O(n^{-1-\gamma})
, as desired.
∎

4.3.3
Equivalence Modulo Vanishing Multi-Tensors

Definition 4.3.9
.

Let
\bm{x},\bm{y}
be multi-tensors of the same shape. We say
\bm{x}

is equivalent to
\bm{y}
, written

\bm{x}\equiv\bm{y}
, if
\bm{x}-\bm{y}
is
vanishing.

Since our writing convention suppresses
n
, this notation may be
ambiguous: To disambiguate, the equivalence
\bm{x}\equiv\bm{y}
is a notion
between multi-tensors as sequences, i.e., it should be read as
\{\bm{x}(n)\}_{n}\equiv\{\bm{y}(n)\}_{n}
,
NOT as a sequence of equivalences
\bm{x}(n)\equiv\bm{y}(n)
, one for each

n
.

The
\bm{x},\bm{y}
here will all have
\Theta(1)
-sized entries in our
applications. By the discussion above regarding vanishing multi-tensors,

\bm{x}\equiv\bm{y}
just means that
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
have roughly the
same entries. But note that, as vectors, matrices, or tensors,
\bm{x}

can definitely differ from
\bm{y}
nontrivially in norm, because e.g.,
a vanishing vector can have
\Theta(1)
norm.

We first note a trivial but useful property.

Proposition 4.3.10
.

(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k})\equiv(y^{1},\ldots,y^{k})
iff
x^{1}\equiv y^{1},\ldots
,
and
x^{k}\equiv y^{k}
.

Equivalence is preserved under most operations, as summarized below:

1.

“smooth” mapping
\phi
: (
4.3.11
and
4.3.12
)

(a)

For example, if
\bm{x}\equiv\bm{y}
are both moment-bounded, then
\phi(\bm{x})\equiv\phi(\bm{y})
.
This holds more generally for “smooth” nonlinear outer products.

2.

averaging over
[n]
: (
Lemma

4.3.13
)

(a)

If
\bm{x}\equiv\bm{y}
, then
\langle\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}}\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\langle\bm{y}_{{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}}\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}

3.

multiplication by operator-norm-bounded matrices: (
4.3.14
)

(a)

If
x\equiv y
are
n
-vectors and
W
is an
n
-matrix that almost
surely has bounded operator norm, then
Wx\equiv Wy
. This holds
in particular for
W
having iid, zero-mean entries of size
\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})
.

Preservation under Nonlinear Outer Products

Proposition 4.3.11
.

Consider multi-tensors
\bm{x}^{t},\bm{y}^{t}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{t}

for each
t=1,\ldots,r
and a function
\psi(-;\cdots;-):\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{t}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
If
\psi
is pseudo-Lipschitz and
\bm{x}^{t}\equiv\bm{y}^{t}
for all

t
, then

\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})\equiv\psi(\bm{y}^{1};\cdots;\bm{y}^{r}).

Proof.

Note that this holds for the semicolon product (i.e., when
\psi

is identity and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{t}
): If
we shorthand
\bm{\Delta}=(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})-(\bm{y}^{1};\cdots;\bm{y}^{r})
,
then

\bm{\Delta}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}=(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1}-\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}^{r}-\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}^{r})\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

so that, with
{\bm{\alpha}}=({\bm{\beta}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{\beta}}_{r})
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\|\bm{\Delta}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\|^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1}-\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1}\|^{2}+\cdots+\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}^{r}-\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}^{r}\|^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{1}-\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{1}\|^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}+\cdots+\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{r}-\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{r}\|^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}.

Then since
\bm{x}^{1}-\bm{y}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}-\bm{y}^{r}
are all vanishing,
so is
\bm{\Delta}
. Thus, we get
(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})\equiv(\bm{y}^{1};\cdots;\bm{y}^{r})
.

By
Eq.

4.1
, this means, for proving
4.3.11
,
it suffices to show
\psi(\bm{x})\equiv\psi(\bm{y})
when
\psi:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

and
\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
with
\bm{x}\equiv\bm{y}
, where

\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
are arbitrary Euclidean spaces. WLOG, assume
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{k}

for some integer
k\geq 0
. We can further assume
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
to be
\mathbb{R}
,
because the general case follows componentwise from this. We proceed
as follows.

By the definition of pseudo-Lipschitz, we have

\displaystyle|\psi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}})-\psi(\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}})|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}}-\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}}\|u_{{\bm{\beta}}}

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad u_{{\bm{\beta}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}1+\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}}\|_{p}^{p}+\|\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}}\|_{p}^{p}

for some constants
C,p>0
. By
4.3.4
,
(u_{{\bm{\beta}}})_{{\bm{\beta}}}

form a moment-bounded tensor
u\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}
. Thus

\left\langle|\psi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}})-\psi(\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}})|^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\leq C\left\langle\|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\beta}}}-\bm{y}_{{\bm{\beta}}}\|^{2}u_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}=C\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle(x_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{i}-y_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{i})^{2}u_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}.

Now fix
i
and let
v=x^{i}-y^{i}
. By assumption,
v
is vanishing.
We will show
n^{1-\epsilon}\left\langle v_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2}u_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

for any
\epsilon>0
, for any
i
. By the above inequality, this
will prove the desired result. Below, we shall abbreivate
\langle\bullet_{{\bm{\beta}}}\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}

as just
\langle\bullet\rangle
.

By Holder’s inequality, for any
q,r>2
such that
\frac{2}{q}+\frac{2}{r}=1
,
we have

\left\langle v^{2}u^{2}\right\rangle\leq\left\langle v^{q}\right\rangle^{2/q}\left\langle u^{r}\right\rangle^{2/r}.

For any
\epsilon>0
, we shall choose
q
(and consequently
r

by the relation
\frac{2}{q}+\frac{2}{r}=1
) barely larger than 2
such that

\displaystyle n^{1-\epsilon/2}\left\langle v^{q}\right\rangle^{2/q}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

(4.2)

\displaystyle n^{-\epsilon/2}\left\langle u^{r}\right\rangle^{2/r}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

(4.3)

from which follows
n^{1-\epsilon}\left\langle v^{2}u^{2}\right\rangle

as we wanted. Now,
Eq.

4.3
holds for any
\epsilon>0

and
r>0
because
u
is moment-bounded.

For
Eq.

4.2
, notice
5
5

5

Here, the definition of “vanishing” in terms of L2 norm becomes
critical, and if we were to define “vanishing” using general Lp
norm, then the proof wouldn’t go through.

\left\langle v^{q}\right\rangle^{2/q}=n^{-2s/q}\|v\|_{q}^{2}\leq n^{-2s/q}\|v\|_{2}^{2}=n^{s(1-2/q)}\left\langle v^{2}\right\rangle

by standard
L^{p}
norm inequality. We shall choose
q
just slightly
above
2
so that
s(1-2/q)<\epsilon/2
. Then
1-\epsilon/2+s(1-2/q)<1-\delta

for some positive
\delta>0
and hence

n^{1-\epsilon/2}\left\langle v^{q}\right\rangle^{2/q}\leq n^{1-\epsilon/2+s(1-2/q)}\left\langle v^{2}\right\rangle<n^{1-\delta}\left\langle v^{2}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

where the convergence to 0 is because
v
is vanishing.
∎

While most nonlinearities we encounter will be pseudo-Lipschitz (globally),
occasionally we need to work with the functions that are only locally
Lipschitz around some point (for example, the matrix inverse function
is locally Lipschitz around a nonsingular matrix). Equivalence to
deterministic constant is preserved under locally Lipschitz mapping:

Proposition 4.3.12
.

Consider moment-bounded multi-scalar
\bm{\theta}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{V}

such that
\bm{\theta}\equiv\mathring{\bm{\theta}}
for some deterministic

\mathring{\bm{\theta}}\in\mathcal{V}
.
6
6

6

Recall this means the random sequence
\bm{\theta}
is equivalent to
the sequence that equals
\mathring{\bm{\theta}}
identically; c.f.
4.2.4
.
Let
\psi:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
be locally Lipschitz at
\mathring{\bm{\theta}}
.
Then

\psi(\bm{\theta})\equiv\psi(\mathring{\bm{\theta}}).

Proof.

WLOG assume
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Let
U
be the open neighborhood of

\mathring{\bm{\theta}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\psi
is Lipschitz
on
U
with Lipschitz constant
L
. Almost surely,
\bm{\theta}\in U

as
n\to\infty
because
\bm{\theta}\equiv\mathring{\bm{\theta}}
. Therefore,
almost surely,
\|\psi(\bm{\theta})-\psi(\mathring{\bm{\theta}})\|\leq L\|\bm{\theta}-\mathring{\bm{\theta}}\|

as
n\to\infty
. Since
\bm{\theta}-\mathring{\bm{\theta}}
is vanishing,
we have
\psi(\bm{\theta})-\psi(\mathring{\bm{\theta}})
is vanishing as
well.
∎

Preservation under Averaging

Lemma 4.3.13
.

Suppose
\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}
are
equivalent. With
{\bm{\beta}}
denoting any subset of indices, let
\left\langle\bm{x}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}

be the multi-tensor
\bm{x}^{\prime}
with entries
\bm{x}^{\prime}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\left\langle\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}
.
Then

1)
\left\langle\bm{x}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}},\left\langle\bm{y}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}

are both moment-bounded;

2) we have

\left\langle\bm{x}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\left\langle\bm{y}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}.

Proof.

Note 1) follows easily from Jensen’s inequality. So we shall focus
on 2) in the remainder.

We first make a few simplifications: 1) We assume
{\bm{\beta}}
is just
the single last index and use the unbolded font
\beta
instead.
The general case follows from the obvious induction. 2) WLOG we can
assume
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}
because the general case follows componentwise
from this. We then use unbolded font
x,y
instead of
\bm{x},\bm{y}
.
3) By linearity of
\equiv
, it suffices to prove this for
y\equiv 0

(i.e.,
x
and
y
are both vanishing).

Let
{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s-1})\in[n]^{s-1}
denote the
first
s-1
indices. Then, by power-mean inequality, for every
{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}
,

\left\langle x_{{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}\beta}\right\rangle_{\beta}^{2}\leq\left\langle x_{{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}\beta}^{2}\right\rangle_{\beta}.

Therefore,

\left\|\left\langle x_{\bullet\beta}\right\rangle_{\beta}\right\|^{2}=\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}}\left\langle x_{{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}\beta}\right\rangle_{\beta}^{2}\leq n^{s-1}\left\langle x_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}

where
{\bm{\beta}}
ranges over all
[n]^{s}
. Then

n^{1-\epsilon}\cdot\left\|\left\langle x_{\bullet\beta}\right\rangle_{\beta}\right\|^{2}/n^{s-1}\leq n^{1-\epsilon}\left\langle x_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0

where the almost sure convergence is because
x
is vanishing. Thus
we have
\left\langle x_{\bullet\beta}\right\rangle_{\beta}
is
vanishing, as well.
∎

Preservation under Matrix Multiplication

Proposition 4.3.14
.

Given
n
-vectors
x,y\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}
with
x\equiv y
and an
n
-matrix

W\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{2}
with almost surely bounded operator norm, we have

Wx\equiv Wy,

no matter how
W
is correlated with
x
and
y
.

In particular, this applies when
W(n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathrm{iid}_{n\times n}(Z)

where
Z
is a sub-Gaussian random variable with zero mean and unit variance.
7
7

7

We expect this operator norm bound to hold more generally for
Z
that is zero-mean, unit variance, and has finite fourth moment, due to
[
48
]
.
But this result assumes the matrices
W(n),n\geq 1
, are upper left blocks of an infinite iid matrix.
It should be possible to relax this assumption in
[
48
]
, but since this is not crucial here, we will just leave this task to future works for which this is more critical.

Proof.

The first statement is trivial since the definition of “vanishing”
only depends on
L^{2}
norm. The second statement follows from well-known operator norm bounds on iid matrices with sub-Gaussian entries.
∎

4.3.4
Distributional Equivalence (aka Dequivalence)

We will also need the distributional version of equivalence. In the
simplest case, we have the following definition.

Definition 4.3.15
.

For any multi-tensor
\bm{x},\bm{y}
of the same
shape, we say
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
are
distributionally equivalent, or dequivalent
, written

\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{y},

if there exist multi-tensors
\hat{\bm{x}},\hat{\bm{y}}

with
\hat{\bm{x}}\equiv\bm{x}
and
\hat{\bm{y}}\equiv\bm{y}
such that
\hat{\bm{x}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\hat{\bm{y}}
.

Like for equivalence,
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{y}
is a notion between multi-tensors
as sequences, i.e., it should be read as
\{\bm{x}(n)\}_{n}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\{\bm{y}(n)\}_{n}
,
NOT as a sequence of equivalences
\bm{x}(n)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{y}(n)
, one for
each
n
.

However, note that, contrary to equality, distributional equality
in general is not reducible componentwise:
(x^{1},x^{2})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}(y^{1},y^{2})

is usually stronger than the conjunction of
x^{1}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}y^{1}

and
x^{2}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}y^{2}
. Thus, we need to define a more general
notion of distributional equivalence of list of objects.

Definition 4.3.16
.

Suppose we have two lists of multi-tensors

\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}
and
\bm{y}^{1},\ldots,\bm{y}^{r}
, such that

x^{t}
and
\bm{y}^{t}
have the same shape for every
t
, but their
shapes can vary with
t
. We say
\left(\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}\right)

and
\left(\bm{y}^{1},\ldots,\bm{y}^{r}\right)
are
distributionally
equivalent,
written

\left(\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}\right)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\left(\bm{y}^{1},\ldots,\bm{y}^{r}\right),

if there exist
\hat{\bm{x}}^{t}\equiv\bm{x}^{t}
,
\hat{\bm{y}}^{t}\equiv\bm{y}^{t}

for all
t\in[r]
such that
(\hat{\bm{x}}^{1},\ldots,\hat{\bm{x}}^{r})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}(\hat{\bm{y}}^{1},\ldots,\hat{\bm{y}}^{r})
.

This definition is especially necessary for reasoning about the Gaussian
conditioning trick inside the induction proof of the Master Theorem.

Finally, we note that, while
4.3.16
seems to be strictly
more general than
4.3.15
, in fact they are logically
equivalent:

\left(\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}\right)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\left(\bm{y}^{1},\ldots,\bm{y}^{r}\right)\iff\left(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r}\right)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\left(\bm{y}^{1};\cdots;\bm{y}^{r}\right)

(4.4)

where the RHS uses
4.3.15
on semicolon products.
8
8

8

When
\bm{x}^{t}
all have the same number of
n
-dimensions, then

\left(\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r}\right)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\left(\bm{y}^{1},\ldots,\bm{y}^{r}\right)

can be interpreted as distributional equivalence ala
4.3.15

of two multi-tensors fromed from concatenation. But in general
\bm{x}^{t}

have different number of
n
-dimensions for different
t
, where
the semicolon product still applies.
Nevertheless, we explicitly formulated
4.3.16
as it
is more transparent.

As discussed above,
(\bm{z},\bm{x})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}(\bm{z}^{\prime},\bm{y})
is strictly stronger
than the conjunction of
\bm{z}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{z}^{\prime}
and
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}y
.
However, they are logically equivalent if we replace
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}y

with the
conditional dequivalence
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}\bm{y}
,
as
defined below, where
R
is the relation
\bm{z}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{z}^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.3.17

(Conditional distributional equivalence)
.

The expression
R:\bm{z}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{z}^{\prime}
means “we give the relation

\bm{z}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{z}^{\prime}
the name
R
.”
In this case, we define
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}\bm{y}
to mean
(\bm{z},\bm{x})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}(\bm{z}^{\prime},\bm{y})
.
This is called a
conditional dequivalence
.

More generally, if
R:\bm{z}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R^{\prime}}\bm{z}^{\prime}
is itself a conditional
equivalence, we recursively define
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}\bm{y}
to mean

(\bm{z},\bm{x})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R^{\prime}}(\bm{z}^{\prime},\bm{y})
.
Finally, when
R=\emptyset
is the empty relation (equivalence of
nothing to nothing), we define
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}\bm{y}
as just the
unconditional dequivalence
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{y}
.

For example, we may recursively write
R_{0}:\bm{x}_{0}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{y}_{0}
and
R_{i}:\bm{x}_{i}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R_{i-1}}\bm{y}_{i}
.
Then the conditional dequivalence
R_{i}
can be unpacked into
(\bm{x}_{0},\ldots,\bm{x}_{i})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}(\bm{y}_{0},\ldots,\bm{y}_{i})
.

Wasserstein Distance Interpretation

For two multi-tensors
\bm{x},\bm{y}
of the same shape, thought of as sequences
\{\bm{x}(n)\}_{n},\{\bm{y}(n)\}_{n}
, we may form a new sequence
W_{2}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
of their Wasserstein distances
\{W_{2}(\mathcal{D}_{\bm{x}(n)},\mathcal{D}_{\bm{y}(n)})\}_{n}
, where
W_{2}
’s underlying metric for each
n
is the scaled Euclidean distance
d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sqrt{\langle\|\mathbf{x}_{\bm{\alpha}}-\mathbf{y}_{\bm{\alpha}}\|^{2}\rangle_{\bm{\alpha}}},
and
\mathcal{D}_{\bm{x}(n)},\mathcal{D}_{\bm{y}(n)}
denote the measures of
\bm{x}(n),\bm{y}(n)
.
Then,

\text{morally,}\quad\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{y}\iff W_{2}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2}).

This is not exactly rigorous because Wasserstein distance is defined using
expected movement
while equivalence is defined using an
almost sure
notion of “vanishing.”
This is a technical issue that can be sidestepped if one were to define
\tilde{O}
using convergence of expectations rather than almost sure.
Since we will not actually use this Wasserstein distance interpretation in this work, we will not attempt to make this more formal.
This connection is purely to aid the reader’s intuitive understanding.

Basic Properties

Conditional dequivalence naturally inherits the basic
properties of equivalence.
Consider any conditional dequivalence named
R
. In summary, conditional
dequivalence is preserved under:

1.

“smooth” mapping
\phi
: (
4.3.18
)

(a)

For example, if
R^{\prime}:\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}\bm{y}
are all moment-bounded,
then
\phi(\bm{x})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R^{\prime}}\phi(\bm{y})
. This holds more generally
for “smooth” nonlinear outer products.

2.

averaging over
[n]
: (
Lemma

4.3.19
)

(a)

If
R^{\prime}:\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}\bm{y}
, then
\langle\bm{x}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R^{\prime}}\langle\bm{y}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}
.

3.

multiplication by operator-normed-bounded matrices: (
4.3.20
)

(a)

If
x,y
are
n
-vectors with
R^{\prime}:x\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}y
and
W
is
an
n
-matrix that almost surely has bounded operator norm, then

Wx\equiv_{R^{\prime}}Wy
. This holds in particular for
W
having iid,
zero-mean entries of size
\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})
.

The formal statements are as follows. The proofs are all trivial given
the corresponding statements in
Section

4.3.3
.

Proposition 4.3.18
.

Consider multi-tensors
\bm{x}^{t},\bm{y}^{t}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{t}

for each
t=1,\ldots,r
and a function
\psi(-;\cdots;-):\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{t}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
Let
R
be any dequivalence. If
\psi
is pseudo-Lipschitz
and
R^{\prime}:(\bm{x}^{1},\ldots,\bm{x}^{r})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}(\bm{y}^{1},\ldots,\bm{y}^{r})
,
then

\psi(\bm{x}^{1};\cdots;\bm{x}^{r})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R^{\prime}}\psi(\bm{y}^{1};\cdots;\bm{y}^{r}).

Lemma 4.3.19
.

Suppose
\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}\otimes\mathcal{V}

satisfy
R^{\prime}:\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}\bm{y}
for some dequivalence

R
. Then, with
{\bm{\beta}}
denoting any subset of indices,

\left\langle\bm{x}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R^{\prime}}\left\langle\bm{y}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}},

where
\left\langle\bm{x}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}
is
the multi-tensor
\bm{x}^{\prime}
with entries
\bm{x}^{\prime}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}=\left\langle\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}
,
and likewise for
\left\langle\bm{y}_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}
.

Proposition 4.3.20
.

Given an
n
-matrix

W\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{2}
with almost surely bounded operator norm and
n
-vectors
x,y\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}
with
R:x\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{W}y
,
9
9

9

x\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{W}y
means dequivalence conditional on the trivial equivalence
W\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}W
.
we have

Wx\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}Wy.

In particular, this applies when
W(n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathrm{iid}_{n\times n}(Z)

where
Z
is a random variable with zero mean, unit variance, and
finite fourth moment.

4.4
Getting Equivalence From Distributional Equivalence

Here we discuss several situations, in order of easy to hard, that allow us to “upgrade” dequivalence to equivalence.
The most important result (
Lemma

4.4.5
) in this section reads roughly as follows:
If
\bm{x}
is
iid along the
n
-dimensions, then
\langle\phi(\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\langle\phi(\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}

(where, recall, for each
i
,
\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}}
is an iid copy of

\bm{x}
, such that
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\ldots,\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}}
are
mutually independent).

Trivial Conversion

Sometimes, we can directly convert
\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}
to
\equiv
:

Proposition 4.4.1
.

If
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\bm{y}

and, for each
n
,
\bm{y}(n)
is deterministic, then
\bm{x}\equiv\bm{y}
.

Proof.

This just follows from that fact that, for any random vectors
\bm{X}

and
\bm{Y}
, if
\bm{X}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\bm{Y}
and
\bm{Y}
is deterministic, then

\bm{X}\overset{\text{a.s.}}{=}\bm{Y}
.
∎

Easy Conversion

In other times, two objects formed from dequivalent ingredients are actually (absolutely) equivalent.
This happens typically when we are averaging over many iid things, essentially because of the Law of Large Numbers.
The most basic example is:

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Let
Z\in\mathbb{R}
be a random variable for whom moments
of every order exists. Then, as elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}
,

\left\langle\mathrm{iid}(Z)_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\alpha}\equiv\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}Z.

Here
\mathrm{iid}(Z)
is only well-defined up to distributional equality, so what we mean here is: whenever
z\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathrm{iid}(Z)
, we have
\left\langle z_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\alpha}\equiv\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}Z.

In other words, if
z\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}z^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathrm{iid}(Z)
, then we in fact have
\langle z_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha}\equiv\langle z^{\prime}_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha}
, not just
\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}
as from
Lemma

4.3.19
.

Proof.

WLOG assume
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}Z=0
. Then because
Z
has moments of every order,
by
Lemma

4.1.3
,
Y\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{\alpha}\mathrm{iid}(Z)_{\alpha}
has moments
of every order
p
bounded by some constant
C_{p}
independent
of
n
. To show
\left\langle\mathrm{iid}(Z)_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}Y

is vanishing, we need to show that for every
\epsilon>0
,
n^{1-\epsilon}\left\langle\mathrm{iid}(Z)_{\alpha}\right\rangle_{\alpha}^{2}=n^{-\epsilon}Y^{2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0
.
But
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}|n^{-\epsilon}Y^{2}|^{p}\leq C_{2p}n^{-\epsilon p}
for every

p>0
. In particular, taking
p>1/\epsilon
, we can apply
Lemma

4.1.2

to get the desired result.
∎

For example, we will frequently use the following corollary of this:

Lemma 4.4.3
.

If
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}

and
\bm{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{l}
satisfy
(\bm{x},\bm{y})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})

for random vectors
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
with finite joint
moments of every order, then

\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{y}\equiv\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\bm{X}\bm{Y}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times l}.

In other words, the RHS has entries
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}X^{i}Y^{j}
for each
i\in[k],j\in[l]
.

Proof.

The
(i,j)
th entry of
\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{y}
is just
\langle x_{\alpha}^{i}y_{\alpha}^{j}\rangle_{\alpha}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\langle\mathrm{iid}(X^{i}Y^{j})_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha}

which is equivalent to
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}X^{i}Y^{j}
by
Lemma

4.4.2
. Then
we can upgrade this to equivalence by
4.4.1
:

(\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{y})^{ij}\equiv\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}X^{i}Y^{j}
, as desired.
∎

Medium Conversion

For our main theorem involving nonlinear outer products, we need the
following (much stronger) generalization of
Lemma

4.4.2
, which roughly says
\langle\psi(\bm{x};\mathrm{iid}(\bm{Z})_{\alpha})\rangle_{\alpha}\equiv\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{Z}}\psi(\bm{x};\bm{Z})
in the simplest case.

Lemma 4.4.4
.

Let
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{0}

be moment-bounded. For each
t=1,\ldots,r
, let
\bm{Z}^{t}\in\mathcal{V}_{t}

be a (fixed-dimensional) random vector whose moments exist for all
orders. Assume
\bm{Z}^{1},\ldots,\bm{Z}^{r}
are all mutually independent.
For each
t
, define
s_{t}\geq 1
and
\bm{z}^{t}\in\mathcal{S}^{s_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{t}

such that
\bm{z}^{t}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathrm{iid}^{s_{t}}(\bm{Z}^{t})
. Assume
\bm{x},\bm{z}^{1},\ldots,\bm{z}^{r}

are mutually independent. Let
\psi:\mathcal{V}_{0}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{r}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

be a pseudo-Lipschitz function. Then, with
{\bm{\beta}}_{t}\in[n]^{s_{t}}

and
{\bm{\beta}}={\bm{\beta}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{\beta}}_{r}
,

\left\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{z}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1};\cdots;\bm{z}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}^{r})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\Psi(\bm{x})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

where

\Psi:\mathcal{V}_{0}\to\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\quad\Psi(\xi)\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\psi(\xi;\bm{Z}^{1};\cdots;\bm{Z}^{r}),

where the expectation is taken over
\bm{Z}^{1},\ldots,\bm{Z}^{r}
.

As discussed above, this implies

\left\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{z}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1};\cdots;\bm{z}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}^{r})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\left\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{z}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\bm{z}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{r}}^{r\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}},

(4.5)

which is an upgrade over the
\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}
from
Lemma

4.3.19
.

Proof.

WLOG assume
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}
since the general case follows from this
componentwise. Furthermore, we will assume
r=1
because the general
case follows from this by induction. Let

\bar{\psi}(\xi;\zeta^{1})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\psi(\xi;\zeta^{1})-\Psi(\xi).

Then for any
\xi
,
\bar{\psi}(\xi;\bm{Z}^{1})
has mean zero (over
the randomness of
\bm{Z}^{1}
). Let

y\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\left\langle\bar{\psi}(\bm{x};\bm{z}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s_{0}}.

We want to show
y
is vanishing.

Notationally, we have usually written
\bm{x}
when we really mean
\bm{x}(n)
,
the
n
th element of
\bm{x}
as sequence of tensors. This is not ambiguous
typically, but here we do want to talk about both semantics. Therefore,
in this proof, we will be explicit: for emphasis, we write
\bm{x}[1,\infty)=\{x(n)\}_{n=1}^{\infty}

for the sequence interpretation and still write just “
\bm{x}
”
for the
n
th element of it (with dependence on
n
suppressed as
usual).

Now, we will prove
y
is vanishing by showing that, with probability
1 on the distribution of
\bm{x}[1,\infty)
,
y
is vanishing conditioned
on
\bm{x}[1,\infty)
.

By
Lemma

4.1.3
applied to
\bar{\psi}(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}};\bm{z}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1}}^{1})

with fixed
{\bm{\alpha}}
(using the independence of
\bm{z}^{1}
from
\bm{x}
),
we have

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[y_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{2p}|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}]\leq D_{p}(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})n^{-ps_{1}}\leq D_{p}(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})n^{-p}

for some polynomially-bounded function
D_{p}:\mathcal{V}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
independent
of
{\bm{\alpha}}
. (More specifically,
D_{p}(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})
is a polynomial
in the moments of the random variable
\psi(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}};\bm{Z}^{1})

conditioned on
\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}
, which are themselves polynomially
bounded functions of
\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}
). Here we used the assumption
that
s_{1}\geq 1
in the 2nd inequality. Then

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle y_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{2p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\mid\bm{x}\right]=\left\langle\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[y_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{2p}|\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}]\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\leq n^{-p}\left\langle D_{p}(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}.

Because
\bm{x}
is moment-bounded, so is
\left\langle D_{p}(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\in\mathcal{S}^{0}
,
so that, for every
\varepsilon>0
,

n^{-\varepsilon}\left\langle D_{p}(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0.

Thus, for almost every sequence
\bm{x}[1,\infty)
,

n^{-\varepsilon}\left\langle D_{p}(\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}})\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\leq C_{p,\varepsilon}(\bm{x}[1,\infty))\quad\text{for all $n$}

for some constant
C_{p,\varepsilon}(\bm{x}[1,\infty))
dependent on
the whole sequence
\bm{x}[1,\infty)
. Then

n^{-\varepsilon}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle y_{{\bm{\alpha}}}^{2p}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\mid\bm{x}[1,\infty)\right]\leq C_{p,\varepsilon}(\bm{x}[1,\infty))n^{-p}\quad\text{for all $n$}

satisfying
4.3.8
, implying
y

is vanishing conditioned on
\bm{x}[1,\infty)
. Since this argument
holds for every
\bm{x}[1,\infty)
and every
\varepsilon>0
, we have
the desired result.
∎

Advanced Conversion

In the most advanced case, we need
Eq.

4.5
to hold even when the
\bm{x}
and
\bm{z}^{i}
are strongly correlated.
This will turn out to be the most important case, since it forms the induction base for our main theorem.

Lemma 4.4.5

(IID Equivalence)
.

Let
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a random vector
whose moments exist for all orders. Let
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}

satisfy
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})
. Let
\bm{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{l}

be such that
\bm{c}\equiv\mathring{\bm{c}}
for some deterministic
\mathring{\bm{c}}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
.

Let
\psi:(\oplus^{s+r}\mathbb{R}^{k})\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}
be pseudo-Lipschitz.
Then, with
{\bm{\beta}}=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r})
,
10
10

10

The iid copies
\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\ldots,\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}}
are only well-defined up to distributional equality, so
Eq.

4.6
should be read as: for
any
instantiation of
\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\ldots,\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}}
, the equivalence holds.

\langle\psi(\bm{x};\cdots;\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}};\bm{c})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\langle\psi(\bm{x};\cdots;\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\mathring{\bm{c}})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}

(4.6)

as elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{s}
(where, recalling
4.2.5
,
\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}},\ldots,\bm{x}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}}

are iid copies of
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})
, independent from
\bm{x}
).

We in fact don’t need to assume
\mathring{c}
is deterministic,
but doing so simplifies the proof.

We remind the reader the notations in
Eq.

4.6
.
To be very explicit, if
s=1
and
T=\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}};\bm{c})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}
,
then each entry
T_{\alpha}
is

T_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{n^{r}}\sum_{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r}}\psi(x_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots,x_{\alpha}^{k}\ ;\ x_{\beta_{1}}^{1},\ldots,x_{\beta_{1}}^{k}\ ;\ \cdots\ ;\ x_{\beta_{r}}^{1},\ldots,x_{\beta_{r}}^{k}\ ;\ c^{1},\ldots,c^{l}).

(4.7)

Likewise for
\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\mathring{\bm{c}})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}
.

Intuitively, when all the
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r}
are distinct,
then
\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}},\ldots,\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}}
are roughly independent
as well, but this is obviously not the case when
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r}

are not distinct. However, among all possible values of the tuple

(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r})
, the nondistinct ones constitute a
minority, vanishing with
n
. Therefore, we hope to say that they
contribute vanishingly to the sum
Eq.

4.7
so as to
establish
Eq.

4.6
.

By combining with
Lemma

4.4.4
, we get

Lemma 4.4.6
.

In the same scenario as
Lemma

4.4.5
,
if
s=1
, then

\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}};\bm{c})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\Psi(\bm{x})

where

\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\Psi(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}},\ldots,\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}}\psi(\mathbf{x};\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\mathring{\bm{c}}),\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},

(with the expectation taken over
\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\bm{X}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}}
).

4.4.1
Proof of
Lemma

4.4.5

Preliminary Reduction and Setup

We assume in the proof that
s=1
, as the general case is a straightforward
modification.

WLOG, we can assume
\bm{x}
is an iid copy of
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})
by
4.3.11
.
Applying
4.3.11
again to
\bm{c}
, we see

\psi(\bm{x};\bm{x};\cdots;\bm{x};\bm{c})\equiv\psi(\bm{x};\bm{x};\cdots;\bm{x};\mathring{\bm{c}}).

So we can assume WLOG
\bm{c}=\mathring{c}
and furthermore just absorb
it into
\psi
since
\mathring{\bm{c}}
is deterministic. It remains
to show

\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\langle\psi(\bm{x};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}.

Let
\Delta\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{r+1}
be the tensor

\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}=\Delta_{\alpha\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{r}}=\psi(\bm{x}_{\alpha};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}})-\psi(\bm{x}_{\alpha};\bm{x}_{\beta_{1}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\bm{x}_{\beta_{r}}^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle r$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle r$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\kern 0.5pt}}}).

Note the following properties of
\Delta
:

Proposition 4.4.7
.

1) If
\alpha
does not appear in
{\bm{\beta}}

and all indices in
{\bm{\beta}}
are distinct, then
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}=0
.
2) If
{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}
is another multi-index with the same property and
that furthermore does not intersect
{\bm{\beta}}
, then
\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}

and
\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}^{\prime}}
are independent conditioned on
\bm{x}_{\alpha}
.

Then our goal is to show
\left\langle\Delta_{\bullet{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}

is vanishing, i.e., for any
\epsilon>0
,

n^{1-\epsilon}\left\langle\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\mathrm{a.s.}}}0.

By
4.3.8
, we just need to show there
exist constants
C_{p}
for all integers
p\geq 1
such that

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}\right\rangle_{\alpha}\leq C_{p}n^{-p}

(4.8)

for all
n
and
p
.

Fix
n
and
p
. Below, we shall bound each
\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}

individually. By symmetry in
\alpha
, we WLOG fix
\alpha=n
. Let

n^{\prime}
be the largest multiple of
r
smaller than
n
.

The Core Insight

We succinctly overview the core insight here, with more explanation
below. There are 3 steps of reasoning:

1. For large
n
,
\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}
is dominated
by the subset sum
\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}
where

\Gamma\subseteq[n^{\prime}]^{r}
consists of all
{\bm{\beta}}
that do not contain
repeating indices (i.e.,
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r}
are all distinct).
(Note such
{\bm{\beta}}
does not contain
\alpha
either since
\alpha=n>n^{\prime}

by assumption).

2. But by Ordered Baranyai’s Theorem (
4.1.6
), there is a partition
of
\Gamma
into perfect matchings
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\ldots
i.e., such that each
\Gamma_{i}

consists of multi-indices
\Gamma_{i}=\{{\bm{\beta}}^{1},\ldots,{\bm{\beta}}^{n^{\prime}/r}\}

and
{\bm{\beta}}^{1},\ldots,{\bm{\beta}}^{n^{\prime}/r}
partition
[n^{\prime}]
(i.e.,
each
\beta\in[n^{\prime}]
appear in exactly one of
{\bm{\beta}}^{j}
).
For example, if
n^{\prime}=4
and
r=2
, then
\Gamma=\{(1,2),(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4),(3,4)\}\cup\{\text{mirror image}\}
.
This is partitioned into the perfect matchings
\{(1,2),(3,4)\}\sqcup\{(1,3),(2,4)\}\sqcup\{(1,4),(2,3)\}
along with their mirror images.
Back to the general case, we thus have

\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}=\sum_{i}\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma_{i}}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}.

(4.9)

3. But, conditioned on
\bm{x}_{\alpha}
, each
\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma_{i}}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}

is a sum of independent, mean-zero random variables by
4.4.7
.
Therefore it has typical size
O(\sqrt{n^{\prime}/r})
which is just
O(\sqrt{n})

because
r
is constant. As a result, the whole sum
Eq.

4.9

is of order
n^{r-1/2}
so that

n^{-r}\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}=O(n^{-1/2}).

This will be formalized as moment bounds

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}\leq C_{p}n^{-p}

from which
Eq.

4.8
would follow.

Of the 3 steps above, only 1 and 3 need further explanation. We do
so below.

Filling in the Details

1. Sum Decomposition

Let
R_{1}
be the set of
{\bm{\beta}}
that contains at least one of

n^{\prime}+1,\ldots,n
. Let
R_{2}
be the set of
{\bm{\beta}}
that contains
repeating indices. Then the complement of
\Gamma
in
[n]^{r}

is a subset of
R_{1}\cup R_{2}
.

Now note that
|R_{1}\cup R_{2}|=\Theta(n^{r-1})
(where the hidden
constant can depend on
r
since
r=\Theta(1)
). Furthermore, because

\bm{X}
(the distribution of
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
’s
\alpha
-slices)
has all moments and
\psi
is pseudo-Lipschitz and thus polynomially
bounded, there exist constants
D_{q}
for every
q\geq 1
such that
for all
\alpha,{\bm{\beta}}
, we have
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}|\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}|^{q}\leq D_{q}=O(1)
.
Therefore, we have

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}(\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}\not\in\Gamma}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}})^{2p}\leq|R_{1}\cup R_{2}|^{2p-1}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}\not\in\Gamma}\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}=O(n^{(r-1)(2p-1)+(r-1)})=O(n^{(r-1)2p}).

Thus, after multiplying by
(n^{-r})^{2p}
, we get

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\not\in\Gamma}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}=O(n^{-2p}).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}+\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\not\in\Gamma}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq C\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}+C\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\not\in\Gamma}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq C\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}+O(n^{-2p})

where
C=2^{2p-1}
(by
Lemma

4.1.1
). Thus, to show
Eq.

4.8
,
it suffices to show

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}\leq C_{p}n^{-p}

(for a different set of constants
C_{p}
).

3. Bounding Moments

Let
\left\langle-\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}
denote average
over
{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma
. Recall that, by Ordered Baranyai’s Theorem, we have
partitioned
\Gamma
into
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\ldots
where each

\Gamma_{i}
consists of multi-indices
\Gamma_{i}=\{{\bm{\beta}}^{1},\ldots,{\bm{\beta}}^{n^{\prime}/r}\}

such that
{\bm{\beta}}^{1},\ldots,{\bm{\beta}}^{n^{\prime}/r}
partition
[n^{\prime}]
. Consequently,
for each
i
,
\{\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}:{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma_{i}\}
is
mutually independent conditioned on
\bm{x}_{\alpha}
. We first deduce

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}\leq\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}^{2p}=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma_{i}}\right\rangle_{i}^{2p}\leq\left\langle\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma_{i}}^{2p}\right\rangle_{i}

where the equality follows from
Eq.

4.9
and the last
inequality follows from power-mean inequality. Again, conditioned
on
\bm{x}_{\alpha}
, each
\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma_{i}}

is a mean of independent, mean-zero random variables by
4.4.7
.
But by
Lemma

4.1.3
, there are real polynomially bounded
functions
F_{p}
of
\bm{x}_{\alpha}
for each integer
p
such that

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma_{i}}^{2p}\mid\bm{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\right]<F_{p}(\bm{x}_{\alpha})(n^{\prime}/r)^{-p}

simultaneously for every
i
.
11
11

11

Specifically, if
H_{\alpha}
is the random variable distributed
identically as
\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}},{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma
(which have
the same distribution for any
{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma
), then by
Lemma

4.1.3
,

F_{p}
is a polynomial in the moments of
H_{\alpha}
conditioned
on
\bm{x}_{\alpha},
and these moments are themself polynomially bounded
in
\bm{x}_{\alpha}
because
\psi
is polynomially bounded.
Therefore, altogether,

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{\alpha{\bm{\beta}}}\mathbb{I}_{{\bm{\beta}}\in\Gamma}\right\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{2p}\leq C_{p}n^{-p}

where
C_{p}=2^{p}r^{p}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}F_{p}(\bm{x}_{\alpha})=2^{p}r^{p}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}F_{p}(\bm{X})

(which is finite because
\bm{X}
has all moments, and where
2^{p}\geq(n/n^{\prime})^{p}

is there as a loose conversion factor from
n^{\prime}
to
n
).

4.5
Matrix Pseudo-Inverse

Lemma 4.5.1
.

Suppose
12
12

12

i.e.,
\Lambda(n)
is a random
k\times k
matrix for each
n
;
c.f.
4.2.2

\Lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that
\Lambda\equiv\mathring{\Lambda}

for a deterministic
\mathring{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
.
13
13

13

Recall this means the random sequence
\Lambda
is equivalent to
the sequence that equals
\mathring{\Lambda}
identically; c.f.
4.2.4
.
If
\mathring{\Lambda}
is full rank, then

\Lambda^{+}\equiv\mathring{\Lambda}^{-1}

as well.

Proof.

Apply
4.3.12
to
\psi
being the matrix pseudo-inverse
function, which is locally Lipschitz at any full rank matrix.
∎

Earlier we defined spaces like
\mathcal{S}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}
which
roughly contains sequences (in
n
) of
n\times k
matrices. Likewise,
we can define
\mathbb{R}^{k}\otimes\mathcal{S}^{1}
which contain the transposes
of
\mathcal{S}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}
, i.e.,
k\times n
matrices.

Lemma 4.5.2
.

Suppose
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is a random vector
and
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
14
14

14

i.e.,
X
is a sequence of random
\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
matrices, distributionally
equivalent to
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})
; c.f.
4.2.2
If
\bm{X}
has moments of all orders and its covariance
\Lambda\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}

(which is deterministic, independent of
n
) is full rank, then as
elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
15
15

15

Recall, as discussed in
4.2.2
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}

means sequence in
n
of moment-bounded
n\times k
matrices.

n\bm{x}^{+\top}\equiv\bm{x}\Lambda^{-1}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}n\ \mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})^{+\top}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})\Lambda^{-1}.

(4.10)

Here
n\bm{x}^{+\top}
is the sequence
n\mapsto n\bm{x}(n)^{+\top}
,
and likewise for
n\ \mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})^{+\top}
;
\bm{x}\Lambda^{-1}
at index

n
is the matrix multiplication between
\bm{x}(n)
(shape
n\times k
)
and
\Lambda^{-1}
(shape
k\times k
).

Note that
n\bm{x}^{+\top}
is the correct scaling with
n
so that
this matrix has
\Theta(1)
sized entries (it is elucidating to consider
the example when
k=1
and
\bm{x}
is just an
n
-vector).

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\bm{x}}
be an instance of
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})
such that
\bm{x}\equiv\tilde{\bm{x}}
.

By standard pseudo-inverse facts (
Section

4.1.1
),
n\bm{x}^{+}=(\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x})^{+}\bm{x}^{\top}.

By
Lemma

4.3.13
,
\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}\equiv\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\bm{x}}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{x}}
.
By
Lemma

4.4.2
,
\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\bm{x}}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{x}}\equiv\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}=\Lambda
.
By
Lemma

4.5.1
and the nonsingularity of
\Lambda
, we have

\left(\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}\right)^{+}\equiv\Lambda^{-1}.

(4.11)

Therefore, since equivalence is preserved under matrix multiplication
(which is certainly pseudo-Lipschitz; see
4.3.11
),
we have

n\bm{x}^{+\top}=\bm{x}(\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x})^{+}\equiv\bm{x}\Lambda^{-1}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\tilde{\bm{x}}\Lambda^{-1}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X})\Lambda^{-1}

as desired.
16
16

16

Technically, we need to check that
\Lambda^{-1}
and
\tilde{\bm{x}}^{\top}

are both moment-bounded for
4.3.11
to apply.
But this check passes trivially.

∎

Lemma 4.5.3
.

In the setting of
Lemma

4.5.2
, suppose
there is additionally
\bm{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{l}
such that
(\bm{x},\bm{y})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})

(as sequences in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{1}\otimes(\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l})
) for some
(fixed wrt
n
) random vector
\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
. Then, as elements
of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k\times l}
,
17
17

17

Recall
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k\times l}
means sequence in
n
of
moment-bounded
k\times l
matrices; c.f.
4.2.2

\bm{x}^{+}\bm{y}\equiv\Lambda^{-1}\Gamma

(4.12)

where
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times l}
is the deterministic matrix with
entries
\Gamma^{ij}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}X^{i}Y^{j}
.
18
18

18

Note the RHS is just a deterministic
\mathbb{R}^{k\times l}
matrix, representing
the constant sequence as in
4.2.4
.

Proof.

let
(\tilde{\bm{x}},\tilde{\bm{y}})
be an instance of
\mathrm{iid}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})

such that
(\bm{x},\bm{y})\equiv(\tilde{\bm{x}},\tilde{\bm{y}})
. Then by standard pseudo-inverse facts (
Section

4.1.1
),

\bm{x}^{+}\bm{y}=(\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x})^{-1}(\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{y})\equiv\Lambda^{-1}(\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\bm{x}}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{y}})\equiv\Lambda^{-1}\Gamma

where the first equivalence follows from
Eq.

4.11

and
4.3.11
, and the last equivalence follows
from
Lemma

4.4.2
.
∎

4.6
Uniformized Tensor Programs

Here, we “uniformize” different variations of Tensor Programs, in the sense that we squash all the different instructions into a single step.
This allows us to streamline the induction in our proofs.

4.6.1
Uniformized
\textsc{Netsor}\top

Given initial matrices
A^{1},\ldots,A^{L}\in\bm{\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}}^{n\times n}
and initial
vectors
g^{1},\ldots,g^{M_{0}}\in\bm{\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}
, consider the following
iteration for
i=M_{0}+1,\ldots,M
that generates new vectors
g^{M_{0}+1},\ldots,g^{M}\in\bm{\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}
:

g_{\alpha}^{i}\leftarrow\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}W_{\alpha\beta}^{i}x_{\beta}^{i},\quad\text{where }x_{\alpha}^{i}=\phi^{i}(g_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots,g_{\alpha}^{i-1}).

(4.13)

Here each
\phi^{i}
is a chosen scalar function with
(i-1)
arguments
and
W^{i}
is an
n\times n
matrix. Each matrix
W^{i}
equals
to either some matrix
A^{j}
of the program or its transpose
A^{j\top}
.
The matrices
W^{i}
for different
i
can possibly be the same.
Thus each program is entirely determined by the data
\{A^{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}\cup\{g^{i}\}_{i=1}^{M_{0}}\cup\{\phi^{i}\}_{i=M_{0}+1}^{M}

along with the correspondence between
W^{i}
and
A^{j}
or
A^{j\top}
.

This type of program is obviously a subset of
\textsc{Netsor}\top
(
[
44
]
) but it’s also easy to see that they have the same expressive power.

4.6.2
Uniformized
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top

Now extend the previous formulation to include additionally initial
scalars
c^{1},\ldots,c^{M_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}
and extend
Eq.

4.13

to produce both a new vector
g^{i}
and a new scalar
c^{i}
at
each step
i=M_{0}+1,\ldots,M
:

\displaystyle g_{\alpha}^{i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}W_{\alpha\beta}^{i}x_{\beta}^{i},\quad c^{i}\leftarrow\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}x_{\beta}^{i},

(4.14)

\displaystyle\text{where }x_{\alpha}^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{i}}\sum_{{\bm{\beta}}}\phi^{i}(\bm{g}_{\alpha};\bm{g}_{\beta};\cdots;\bm{g}_{\beta_{i}};\bm{c})

(4.15)

\displaystyle=\langle\phi^{i}(\bm{g}_{\alpha};\bm{g}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{g}_{\beta_{i}};\bm{c})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}

(4.16)

where
\phi^{i}:\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{i-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}^{i-1}}_{i+2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
each
{\bm{\beta}}=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{i})
ranges over all tuples
in
[n]^{i}
, and
\bm{g}=(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i-1}),\bm{c}=(\bm{c}^{1},\ldots,\bm{c}^{i-1})

at iteration
i
.

Here we are tying together the tensor order of
\phi^{i}
with the
iteration index
i
, to simplify the formulation. But note that
\phi^{i}

can always ignore all but the first block of inputs, for example,
to replicate
Eq.

4.13
. By the same reasoning as
Section

4.6.1
,
this formulation of
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
is equivalent to
2.6.1
.

4.6.3
Setup and Constructions

Below, we typically consider the following setup for both uniformized
\textsc{Netsor}\top
and uniformized
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
.
It is essentially the same as
2.6.3
except that the initial scalars need to converge at the rate of
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
.
This is needed to prove the vectorwise convergence results below.

Setup 4.6.1
.

Assume

1.

Every entry of every
A^{l}
is sampled iid from
\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
.

2.

Every entry of every initial vector
g^{i}
is sampled iid from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

3.

Each initial scalar
c^{i}
is
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
.

4.

All nonlinearities
\phi^{i}
are pseudo-Lipschitz.

In addition,
since both uniformized
\textsc{Netsor}\top
and uniformized
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
are subsets of the
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
language, the construction of kets in
2.6.5
make sense for both of them.
Finally, in the context of
n\to\infty
, we think of the program’s objects as sequences in
n
, i.e.,
A^{j}\in\mathcal{S}^{2}
,
g^{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}
,
c^{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{0}
.

4.7
\textsc{Netsor}\top
Master Theorem, Vectorwise Convergence

Definition 4.7.1
.

Consider a Tensor Program (of any variation). Let
x^{1},\ldots,x^{k}
be the set of all vectors
in the program. Then we define
(\mathring{x}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{x}^{k})

as
\mathrm{iid}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{1}\rangle,\ldots,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{k}\rangle)
, i.e., each
\alpha
-slice

(\mathring{x}_{\alpha}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{x}_{\alpha}^{k})
is
an iid sample from
(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{1}\rangle,\ldots,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x^{k}\rangle)
.
19
19

19

Note that

(\mathring{x}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{x}^{k})
is only well defined
up to distributional equality, and thus it only makes sense to talk
about them up to distributional equality or coarser notions like (conditional)
dequivalence, but not up to equivalence.

Both
\mathring{x}
and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle
are “limits” of a vector
x
in some sense, but
\mathring{x}
always has the same shape as
x
(same for
\mathring{\theta}
for scalar
\theta
) while
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle
collapses the
n
-dimension.
In this and the next section, we aim to show that
x\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathring{x}
in different kinds of Tensor Programs.
This essentially means that
x
and
\mathring{x}
, random vectors with larger and larger dimensions, become closer and closer in distribution as
n\to\infty
(see
Section

4.3.4
) —
hence the
vectorwise convergence
in the section title.

Theorem 4.7.2
.

Consider a
\textsc{Netsor}\top
program in
4.6.1
.
