Consider any collection of vectors

y^{1},\ldots,y^{k}
in the program. Then they are moment-bounded and

(y^{1},\ldots y^{k})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}(\mathring{y}^{1},\ldots\mathring{y}^{k}).

In other words,
(y^{1},\ldots y^{k})
is distributed like
n
iid
copies of
(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y^{1}\rangle,\ldots\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y^{k}\rangle)
, modulo vanishing vectors.
This is a stronger result than
[
44
]
, at the cost of assuming faster convergence of initial scalars in
4.6.1
.

Remark 4.7.3
.

Let us comment that, in this proof, we do not use the property that
equivalence is preserved under iid matrix multiplication
4.3.14
(this property is a crucial reason underlying the definition of “vanishing”), because of
\textsc{Netsor}\top
’s rank stability property.
This however will be crucial in
Section

4.8
to prove the analogous result for
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
.

We recall some terminology from prior works.

Definition 4.7.4
.

A vector is called a G-var if it is an initial vector or generated by
MatMul
.

4.7.1
Proof Setup

[
44
]

Here

x^{i}

g^{i}

y^{i}

x^{i}

u^{j}

h^{j}

v^{j}

y^{j}

\Upsilon

\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}/n

\mathring{\Upsilon}

\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle

\Lambda

\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{y}/n

\mathring{\Lambda}

\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle

\Gamma

\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{x}/n

\mathring{\Gamma}

\gamma

\bm{x}^{\top}x/n

\mathring{\gamma}

\bm{h}^{\top}x/n

\mathring{\delta}

\langle\bm{h}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle

Table 4.1:
Notation mapping between
[
44
]
and here.
We improved the notation to be more intuitive, leveraging our new bra-ket notation.

We will nontrivially leverage parts of the Master Theorem proof in
[
44
]
.
Our notation differs from there (hopefully improved for first time readers), but a mapping is provided in
Table

4.1
.
By
4.3.18
, it suffices to show this for all G-vars of the program, since other vectors are pseudo-Lipschitz images of G-vars. We furthermore WLOG assume the formulation in
Eq.

4.13

and proceed to show this for vectors
g^{1},\ldots,g^{M}
(all G-vars
in the program).

By the
\textsc{Netsor}\top
Master Theorem
[
44
]
, we know that the matrices

(\frac{1}{n}g^{i\top}g^{j})_{i,j=1}^{M}
and
(\frac{1}{n}x^{i\top}x^{j})_{i,j=1}^{M}

converge almost surely to deterministic matrices
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}

and
\Xi\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
. By rank stability
[
44
, sec L.5]
,
WLOG, we can assume

Assumption 4.7.5
.

WLOG, we assume
\Omega
and
\Xi

are both full rank.

Indeed,
[
44
, sec L.5]
says that
\Xi
(resp.

\Omega
) is singular iff there are linear dependencies (with constant
coefficients) between
x^{i}
s (resp.
g^{i}
s), which we can get
rid of by rewriting the program in the obvious way.
20
20

20

Rank stability is a highly nontrivial but technical result of
[
44
]
. While seemingly small, it allows us to drastically simplify the proof because we do not have to think about “corner cases” where the rank of a matrix drops suddenly in the limit, which can lead to all kinds of pathological behaviors.
Readers interested in full rigor should consult
[
44
]
for the proof of this result.

We induct on
i
, starting from the base case
i=M_{0}
.

Base Case:
i=M_{0}.

This holds by
4.6.1
that the initial vectors are sampled iid.

Inductive Step

Here we assume the dequivalence

R:(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}(\mathring{g}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{g}^{i})

(4.17)

as well as their moment-boundedness
and we shall show

g^{i+1}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}\mathring{g}^{i+1},

and their moment-boundedness,
where the conditional dequivalence can be unpacked into

(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i},g^{i+1})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}(\mathring{g}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{g}^{i},\mathring{g}^{i+1}).

(4.18)

For brevity, write
g\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}g^{i+1},x\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}x^{i+1},W\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}W^{i+1}

so that in this notation, by
Eq.

4.13
,
g=Wx
.

We apply Gaussian conditioning trick to obtain

\displaystyle(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i},g)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i},\omega+\sigma\Pi^{\perp}z)

(4.19)

where
\omega\in\mathcal{S}^{1},\Pi^{\perp}\in\mathcal{S}^{2}
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{0}

are functions of
g^{1},\ldots,g^{i}
, and
z\in\mathcal{S}^{1},z\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathrm{iid}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))

is a fresh iid standard Gaussian vector, independent from everything
else.

4.7.2
Proof Plan

Below, we will give the exact formulas for
\omega,\Pi^{\perp},\sigma

and we will show that

1.

there is a pseudo-Lipschitz function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{i}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\omega\equiv\psi(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i})
,
21
21

21

with
\psi
applied coordinatewise as usual

2.

there is a deterministic
\mathring{\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\sigma\equiv\mathring{\sigma}
,
and

3.

\Pi^{\perp}z\equiv z
.

These claims already imply that
g
is moment-bounded.
Furthermore, applying
Eq.

4.17
and
4.3.18

to
Eq.

4.19
, the above claims imply

(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i},g)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}(\mathring{g}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{g}^{i},\psi(\mathring{g}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{g}^{i})+\mathring{\sigma}z)

Using the exact formulas below, by the same calculations of
[
44
]
,
the RHS can be shown to be
\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}(\mathring{g}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{g}^{i},\mathring{g}^{i+1})
,
yielding
Eq.

4.18
as desired.
22
22

22

In particular, we do not change any of the formulas for the large

n
limit in our induction; we only “upgrade the mode of convergence”
to (distributional) equivalence. So it is easy to verify this claim.

4.7.3
Exact Formulas

Preliminary Definitions

We define the following objects.

•

Let
J
(resp.
J^{\prime}
) be the set of indices
j\leq i
such that
W^{j}=W^{i+1}

(resp.
W^{j}=W^{i+1\top}
), so that
g^{j}=Wx^{j}
(resp.
g^{j}=W^{\top}x^{j}
)
by construction.

•

Let
\bm{g}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{J}
(resp.
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{J}
)
be the matrix with column vectors
g^{j}
(resp.
x^{j}
) for all

j\in J
, so that
\bm{g}=W\bm{x}
. (Note
\bm{g}
is a distinct object
from and does not contain
g=g^{i+1}
; likewise for
\bm{x}
and
x=x^{i+1}
)

•

Likewise, let
\bm{h}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{J^{\prime}}
(resp.
\bm{y}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{J^{\prime}}
)
be the matrix with column vectors
g^{j}
(resp.
x^{j}
) for all

j\in J^{\prime}
, so that
\bm{h}=W^{\top}\bm{y}
.

Exact Formulas

Then, by the same calculations as in
[
44
]
,
\omega,\Pi^{\perp},\sigma

have the following exact formulas:

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=(\bm{g}\bm{x}^{+}+\bm{y}^{+\top}\bm{h}^{\top}-\bm{y}^{+\top}\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{x}\bm{x}^{+})x

(4.20)

and

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\|\Pi_{\bm{x}}^{\perp}x\|^{2}}{n}}

(4.21)

\displaystyle\text{with }\Pi_{\bm{x}}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}I-\bm{x}\bm{x}^{+}

and

\Pi^{\perp}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}I-\bm{y}\bm{y}^{+}.

(4.22)

Further Constructions

Now we further define
\mathring{\bm{g}}
to be the matrix with column
vectors
\mathring{g}^{j}
for all
j\in J
; likewise we define

\mathring{\bm{x}},\mathring{\bm{h}},\mathring{\bm{y}}
. By induction hypothesis
(
Eq.

4.17
), we have

(\bm{g},\bm{x},\bm{h},\bm{y})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}_{R}(\mathring{\bm{g}},\mathring{\bm{x}},\mathring{\bm{h}},\mathring{\bm{y}})

(4.23)

because of
4.3.18
(as this relation is
a pseudo-Lipschitz image of
Eq.

4.17
).

Now note

\mathcal{S}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{J\times J}\ni\frac{1}{n}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{x}\equiv\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{J\times J}

by
Eq.

4.23
and
Lemma

4.4.3
,
where the RHS is a deterministic object independent of
n
. Likewise,

\mathcal{S}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{J^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}\ni\frac{1}{n}\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{y}\equiv\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{J^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}.

But both
\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle
and
\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle
are principal
submatrices of
\Xi
by construction, so by Sylvester’s Criterion
and
4.7.5
,

\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle\text{ and }\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle\text{ are nonsingular.}

(4.24)

The significance of this is that now we can use
Lemma

4.5.3

on
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
.

4.7.4
Showing 3)

Using
Eq.

4.22
and
Lemma

4.5.3
, we
have

\Pi^{\perp}z=z-\bm{y}\bm{y}^{+}z\equiv z-\bm{y}\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle^{-1}\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}z\rangle

But
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}z\rangle
is independent from
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle
(because
z
is independent
from
\bm{y}
by construction) and is distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
so the
\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}z\rangle
vanishes. In conclusion,

\Pi^{\perp}z\equiv z

as desired. This shows 3).

4.7.5
Showing 2)

Writing out the definition of
\sigma^{2}
(
Eq.

4.21
),
we see

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|\Pi_{\bm{x}}^{\perp}x\|^{2}}{n}=\frac{1}{n}x^{\top}x-(\frac{1}{n}x^{\top}\bm{x})(\bm{x}^{+}x)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{n}x^{\top}x-(\frac{1}{n}x^{\top}\bm{x})\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle^{-1}\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle-\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle^{-1}\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\mathring{\sigma}^{2}

where the second line follows by
Lemma

4.5.3
, and the third
line follows by
Eq.

4.23
and
Lemma

4.4.3
.

Note that
\mathring{\sigma}^{2}
is deterministic. By
4.7.5
,

\mathring{\sigma}^{2}>0
(because
\mathring{\sigma}
is a
1\times 1

Schur complement of
\Xi
). Then the square root function is locally
Lipschitz around
\mathring{\sigma}^{2}
. So by
4.3.12
,
we get

\sigma\equiv\mathring{\sigma}.

4.7.6
Showing 1)

Finally, we can apply the same strategy to show
\omega=Ex
is equivalent
to a linear combination of
\mathring{\bm{g}}
and
\mathring{\bm{y}}

with deterministic coefficients, albeit with slightly more calculations.

By
Eq.

4.20
, we can decompose

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=(\bm{g}\bm{x}^{+}+\bm{y}^{+\top}\bm{h}^{\top}-\bm{y}^{+\top}\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{x}\bm{x}^{+})x

\displaystyle=A+B-C

where

A\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{g}\bm{x}^{+}x,\quad B\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{y}(\frac{1}{n}\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{y})^{+}(\frac{1}{n}\bm{h}^{\top}x),\quad C\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\bm{y}(\frac{1}{n}\bm{y}^{\top}\bm{y})^{+}(\frac{1}{n}\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{x})(\bm{x}^{+}x).

Then the same deduction as before (involving
Lemma

4.4.3

and
Lemma

4.5.3
) shows

A\equiv\bm{g}\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle^{-1}\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle,\quad B\equiv\bm{y}\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle^{-1}\langle\bm{h}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle,\quad C\equiv\bm{y}\langle\bm{y}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{y}\rangle^{-1}\langle\bm{h}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{x}\rangle^{-1}\langle\bm{x}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}x\rangle

Thus,
A,B,C
, and thus
\omega
are clearly equivalent to linear
combinations of (the columns of)
\bm{g}
and
\bm{y}
. Consequently,

\omega
is also a pseudo-Lipschitz image of
g^{1},\ldots,g^{i}

because
\bm{y}
is. This proves 1).

4.8
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
Master Theorem, Vectorwise Convergence

Definition 4.8.1

(Integrated Program)
.

Given an uniformized
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program
\pi
(
Eq.

4.14
),
we construct a parallel
uniformized
\textsc{Netsor}\top
program
\underline{\pi}
, called the
integrated program
, as follows.

\underline{\pi}
’s vectors will be
denoted with an underline
\underline{g}^{1},\ldots,\underline{g}^{M}

(which will turn out to be equivalent to their counterparts without underlines).

•

The initial matrices of
\pi
and
\underline{\pi}
are identical and using the same symbols
A^{1},\ldots,A^{L}
.

•

The initial vectors
\underline{g}^{1},\ldots,\underline{g}^{M_{0}}
are the same
as those of the original program
g^{1},\ldots,g^{M_{0}}
.

•

new vectors
\underline{g}^{i}
for
i=M_{0}+1,\ldots,M
are generated iteratively
as follows: For each such
i
, define
\underline{\phi}^{i}:\mathbb{R}^{i-1}\to\mathbb{R}

by

\underline{\phi}^{i}(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\mathbin{\overset{\text{{def}}}{=}}$}}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}}\phi^{i}(\mathbf{x};\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\underline{\bm{g}}\rangle^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\ldots;\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\underline{\bm{g}}\rangle^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\mathring{\bm{c}})

for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{i-1}
, where
\underline{\bm{g}}
denotes
(\underline{g}^{1},\ldots,\underline{g}^{i-1})
,
\bm{c}
denotes
(c^{1},\ldots,c^{i-1})
, and
\phi^{i}
is from
Eq.

4.16
.
Then
\underline{g}^{i}
is generated as

\displaystyle\underline{g}_{\alpha}^{i}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}W_{\alpha\beta}^{i}\underline{x}_{\beta}^{i},\quad\text{where}\quad\underline{x}_{\alpha}^{i}=\underline{\phi}^{i}\left(\underline{\bm{g}}_{\alpha}\right).

Note all scalars
c^{i}
of
\pi
are replaced
by their deterministic limits
\mathring{c}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}
as constructed
in
2.6.5
and absorbed into the nonlinearities
\underline{\phi}^{i}
.

The name
integrated program
refers to the intuition that we are replacing the averaging
\langle\phi^{i}(\bm{g}_{\alpha};\bm{g}_{\beta_{1}};\cdots;\bm{g}_{\beta_{i}};\bm{c})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}
with integration
\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}}\phi^{i}(\bm{g}_{\alpha};\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{g}\rangle^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\cdots;\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{g}\rangle^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\mathring{\bm{c}})
.

Lemma 4.8.2
.

Consider a uniformized
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program

\pi
in
4.6.1
and its integrated
\textsc{Netsor}\top
program
\underline{\pi}
as constructed
above. Then for
\underline{\bm{g}}=(\underline{g}^{1},\ldots,\underline{g}^{M})
,
we have
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{g}\rangle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\underline{\bm{g}}\rangle
(as random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{M}
)
and

\bm{g}\equiv\underline{\bm{g}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathring{\underline{\bm{g}}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathring{\bm{g}}.

In particular, this implies that for (non-uniformized)
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
programs,
\bm{x}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathring{\bm{x}}
for
\bm{x}
being the collection of all vectors, i.e., we have the vectorwise convergent version of the Master Theorem, a strengthening of the 2nd half of
2.6.10
(which assumes initial scalars are
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
).

Before we prove this, let us remark that

Lemma

4.8.2
automatically tells us that identities of kets, such as
[
44
, Lemma L.3]
, that hold for
\textsc{Netsor}\top
programs automatically hold for
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
programs:

Lemma 4.8.3

(
[
44
, Lemma L.3]
)
.

For any vectors
x,y
and matrix
W
in a
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program, the following identities hold.

\displaystyle\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wy\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle W^{\top}x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle

\displaystyle\langle x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}Wy\hat{\rangle\mkern-3.0mu}\mkern 3.0mu

\displaystyle=\mkern 3.0mu\dot{\mkern-3.0mu\langle}W^{\top}x\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}y\rangle

Proof of
Lemma

4.8.2
.

The distributional equality
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{g}\rangle\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\underline{\bm{g}}\rangle
follows
straightforwardly from the definition of these random vectors. Thus
we also have
\mathring{\bm{g}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\mathring{\underline{\bm{g}}}
(since
they are just iid samples of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\bm{g}\rangle
and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\underline{\bm{g}}\rangle
).
We also have from
4.7.2
that
\underline{\bm{g}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\equiv}\mathring{\underline{\bm{g}}}
.
So it remains to prove that
\bm{g}\equiv\underline{\bm{g}}
.

We will induct on
i
to show that
(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i})\equiv(\underline{g}^{1},\ldots,\underline{g}^{i})
.
Note that, by
4.3.11
, this implies
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{i+1})\equiv(\underline{x}^{1},\ldots,\underline{x}^{i+1})
,
and by
Lemma

4.3.13
, this also implies
(c^{1},\ldots,c^{i+1})\equiv(\mathring{c}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{c}^{i+1})
.

The base case of
i=M_{0}
is trivial since both programs share their
initial vectors.

Assuming the induction hypothesis for
i
(i.e.,
(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i})\equiv(\underline{g}^{1},\ldots,\underline{g}^{i})
),
we shall prove it’s also true for
i+1
: i.e., we need to show
g^{i+1}\equiv\underline{g}^{i+1}

in addition.

For brevity, write
\phi=\phi^{i+1}
,
\underline{\phi}=\underline{\phi}^{i+1},W=W^{i+1}
,

x=x^{i+1}
. Let
\bm{g}
denote
(g^{1},\ldots,g^{i})
; likewise
for
\underline{\bm{g}}
. Let
\bm{c}
denote
(c^{1},\ldots,c^{i})

and
\mathring{\bm{c}}
denote
(\mathring{c}^{1},\ldots,\mathring{c}^{i})
.
Then since
\bm{g}\equiv\underline{\bm{g}}
and
\bm{c}\equiv\mathring{\bm{c}}

by IH, we have by
4.3.11
that

\phi(\bm{g};\ldots;\bm{g};\bm{c})\equiv\phi(\underline{\bm{g}};\ldots;\underline{\bm{g}};\mathring{\bm{c}})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{b}}^{i+2}.

Finally, by
Lemma

4.4.6
, we get

\langle\phi(\underline{\bm{g}};\underline{\bm{g}}_{\beta_{1}};\ldots;\underline{\bm{g}}_{\beta_{i}};\mathring{\bm{c}})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}}\phi(\underline{\bm{g}};\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\underline{\bm{g}}\rangle^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle 1$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\ldots;\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\textstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\talloblong$}}{\scalebox{0.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\talloblong$}}\underline{\bm{g}}\rangle^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\displaystyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 1.0pt\framebox{$\textstyle i$}\kern 1.0pt}{\kern 0.7pt\framebox{$\scriptstyle i$}\kern 0.7pt}{\kern 0.5pt\framebox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\kern 0.5pt}}};\mathring{\bm{c}})=\underline{\phi}^{i+1}(\underline{\bm{g}};\mathring{\bm{c}})=\underline{x}.

So, altogether,

x=\langle\phi(\bm{g};\bm{g}_{\beta_{1}};\ldots;\bm{g}_{\beta_{i}};\bm{c})\rangle_{{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv\underline{x}.

Finally, by
4.3.14
, we arrive at

g^{i+1}=Wx\equiv W\underline{x}=\underline{g}^{i+1}

as desired.
∎

Remark 4.8.4
.

In this proof, the preservation of equivalence under iid matrix multiplication
4.3.14
(proved using off-the-shelf operator norm tail bounds) is crucial.
It allowed us to circumvent a complex (and probably ill-fated) Gaussian conditioning argument inside a larger, more involved induction loop.
This preservation property is a key factor behind the
4.3.5
of “vanishing.”
The slight downside to this is that initial scalars need to be
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
(to get the equivalence going in the first place), but that is satisfied most of the time in practice.

4.9
Proof of
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
Master Theorem
2.6.10

With
Lemma

4.8.2
, the only things left to prove are: 1) We can weaken the requirement that the initial scalars are
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
(
1.2.5
) to just that they converge to 0 almost surely; in turn the conclusion needs to be weakened as well to almost sure convergence instead of the stronger
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
convergence.
2) We can swap the Gaussian setup
2.6.3
with the non-Gaussian setup
2.6.4
and not only still obtain all of the above results but also show all scalars converge in
L^{p}
as well, for all
p\in[1,\infty)
.

4.9.1
Relaxing Initial Scalar Requirement

This will follow from the “uniformly locally Lipschitz” property of
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
programs:
For any scalar
\theta
in a program, we can treat
\theta
as a random function
\theta_{n}(\bm{c})
of initial scalars
\bm{c}
(with randomness coming from sampling of initial matrices and vectors) for any finite
n
.
Then for any
\bm{c}
, there is a neighborhood of
\bm{c}
and a constant
L>0
such that
\theta_{n}
is almost surely Lipschitz for all
n
on that neighborhood with Lipschitz constant
L
.

This property in particular implies that
\theta_{n}
is equicontinuous over
n
almost surely, and thus how fast or slow
\bm{c}_{n}
converges to
\mathring{\bm{c}}
does not affect the limit
\lim_{n\to\infty}\theta_{n}(\bm{c}_{n})
.

This ‘‘uniformly locally Lipschitz’’ property can be shown by proving a sub-Weibull
23
23

23

i.e., the probability of deviating
t
away from the limit is bounded by a function of the form
\exp(-C(nt)^{\alpha})
for some power
\alpha>0
(usually
<1
).

tail bound on the convergence of any scalar in a program, and then performing a chaining argument
[
37
]
.
The full details will be given in a future paper.
24
24

24

Note that only the Dynamical Dichotomy Theorem (i.e., classification of abcd-parametrizations) needs to deal with initial scalars that converge slower than
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
. In particular, the NT and
\mu
-limits only need the version of the Master Theorem with
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
initial scalars.

4.9.2
Proof of Non-Gaussian
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
Master Theorem

The non-Gaussian
\textsc{Netsor}\top
Master Theorem (
[
12
]
) states that, under
2.6.4
, the scalars of the program converge almost surely and in
L^{p}
for every
p\in[1,\infty)
.
Our goal here is to adapt its proof to the more general
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
case.

The overarching proof strategy of the non-Gaussian
\textsc{Netsor}\top
Master Theorem was to smoothly interpolate between the non-Gaussian program and the Gaussian program (c.f.
[
12
, Defn 5.1]
) and to show that for large enough
n
, the scalars of the program do not change much along this interpolation.
In fact, the total change along this interpolation is
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
(see the last chain of equation/inequalities before
[
12
, Sec 5.1]
).
So if we can adapt all of the reasoning to
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
, then we can obtain the
\tilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
convergence in
2.6.10
as well under
2.6.4
.

The key technical insight enabling all of this is a bound on the moments of mixed derivatives of a non-Gaussian program’s scalars and vectors against the program’s matrices.

To obtain the non-Gaussian
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
Master Theorem, the main task is to generalize this key technical insight, with which the interpolation trick carries over to our case easily. This is done in
Lemmas

4.9.2
and
4.9.3
below, which are useful in their own right beyond this context.
The full proof of the non-Gaussian Master Theorem can be then be straightforwardly adapted from
[
12
]
.

Setup 4.9.1
.

Consider
2.6.4
, but allow the variances of matrix entries to differ from
n^{-1}
but still bounded above by
\nu_{2}n^{-1}
for some
\nu_{2}>0
common to all matrix entries.

Below, we invoke the notion of
oblivious constants
from
[
12
, Defn I.1]
.
This is a technical notion needed to finish the proof of the the non-Gaussian Master Theorem, but the first time readers can ignore the comments on oblivious constants.

Lemma 4.9.2

(Expected Smoothness of Vectors)
.

Consider a
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program under
4.9.1
.
Then, for any polynomially smooth
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{rM+M}\to\mathbb{R}
, any
p\geq 1
, and any multiset
\mathcal{P}
of the program’s matrix entries
\{W_{\alpha\beta}\}_{\alpha,\beta,W\in\mathcal{W}}
,

\sup_{{\bm{\alpha}}\in[n]^{r}}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left|\partial^{\mathcal{P}}\psi(\bm{g}_{\alpha_{1}};\cdots;\bm{g}_{\alpha_{r}};\bm{c})\right|^{p}=O(1)\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$.}

(4.25)

where constant in the big-O is
(p,\mathcal{P})
-oblivious wrt
\psi
and the program.

This generalizes
[
12
, Lemma I.3]
to
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
.

Proof.

In the original proof in
[
12
, Sec I]
, replace
\phi(x^{1},\ldots,x^{k})

with
\phi(x^{1};\cdots;x^{k})
everywhere.
As the original proof essentially factors through arguments about the “supremum” over all indices
\alpha\in[n]
, it can be adapted straightforwardly to the “supremum” over all multi-indices
{\bm{\alpha}}\in[n]^{r}
.
∎

Lemma 4.9.3

(Expected Smoothness of Scalars)
.

Consider a program in
4.9.1
.
Then for any
p\geq 1
, any
nonempty
multiset
\mathcal{P}
of the program’s matrix entries
\{W_{\alpha\beta}\}_{\alpha,\beta,W\in\mathcal{W}}
,, and any scalar
c
of the program,

\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left|\partial^{\mathcal{P}}c\right|^{p}=O(n^{-p})\quad\text{as $n\to\infty$.}

(4.26)

Furthermore, the constant in the big-O is
(p,\mathcal{P})
-oblivious wrt the program.

This generalizes
[
12
, Lemma J.6]
to
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
.

Proof.

Construct the backpropagation program (
2.9.14
), which is another
\textsc{Ne}{\otimes}\textsc{or}\top
program.
Then proceed as in the proof of
[
12
, Lemma J.6]
.
∎

Chapter 5
Experiments

5.1
Numerical Verification

We perform numerical experiments to validate our theory. It is intractable to compute the exact infinite-width limits for general
\bm{Q}
, since the expectations required to evaluate the infinite-width dynamics in both limits do not admit an analytical solution (even for the Neural Tangent Limit). We thus employ Monte Carlo simulations to approximate these expectations.

We test
2.4.9
and
2.7.3
by training ReLU MLPs (
L=4
for NTP and
L=2
for
\mu
P) on
\mathbb{R}^{10}
Gaussian inputs and a unit output. We use the standard L2 loss function, regressing to random targets. We train networks with different widths using the Adam optimizer with a learning rate of
\eta=0.2
and
\epsilon=10^{-4}
, using 100 training samples and conducting 10 trials for each width. To account for varying initial outputs and loss derivatives per weight initialization, we subtract the initialized network output from the output for each sample, ensuring that the output is zero at initialization for all inputs.

We approximate the infinite-width training dynamics by estimating the expectations in
2.4.4
and
2.7.3
using Monte Carlo simulations. As the initial loss derivatives are deterministic with zero outputs, the infinite-width dynamics can be estimated without actually constructing a network. To compare the finite and infinite width neural networks’ evolution, we assess the output on random inputs at each iteration. Our results are summarized in
Fig.

5.1
and
Fig.

5.2
. As anticipated, the training dynamics converge to the infinite-width dynamics as the width increases.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 5.1:
Adam training dynamics of finite and infinite-width networks in NTP. We train networks of widths 64 (a), 512 (b), 7000 (c), and track the outputs for 4 random inputs (one per row) at each iteration as the network trains. We compute the output distribution over 10 independent runs for each network, and compare with the infinite-width dynamics (black curve). As the width grows, the network function converges to that of the infinite-width dynamics captured in
2.4.9
.

Adam training dynamics of finite and infinite-width networks in
\mu

We compute the output distribution over 10 independent runs for each network, and compare with the infinite-width dynamics (black curve). As the width grows, the network function converges to that of the infinite-width dynamics captured in
2.7.3
.
